A note on the making of this book

There You Are was made through an iterative collaboration between C.S. Gould and Blinka and other AI systems, working across drafting, structural revision, continuity auditing, editorial argument, and the construction of this digital edition.

Corey set the creative direction, the characters, the moral framework and the final shape of the novel; selected, revised, rejected and approved the prose; and made every final editorial decision.

The collaboration is named here because the process shaped the book, and because a story about hidden machinery should not hide its own pipes.

PART I

OPENINGS

1

THE HOLLOW-CORE DOOR

Lyle found the door behind his building on a Thursday night, propped against the dumpster beneath a handwritten sign that said:

FREE. NO BEDBUGS.

The second sentence suggested a history.

The alley security light held a dull orange that made the wet cardboard look older than it was. The door was ordinary hollow-core, beige beneath several generations of landlord white, the bottom swollen from rain. Somebody had capped the edges in thin copper and cut a shallow groove around the perimeter. Water sat inside the groove in a continuous dark line.

Lyle stood over it holding a six-pack with four beers left. Condensation had softened the cardboard handle against his palm.

He had been looking for a desk.

More accurately, he had been looking at desks online for three months and resenting them for costing money. The door seemed flat, approximately desk-shaped, and available without a conversation.

He got his fingers under the copper edge. The metal was colder than the night. Water ran out of the groove onto his sneakers.

He carried it upstairs.

Halfway to the second floor, one corner caught the railing with a hollow tok that traveled up through his wrists and knocked the breath out of him. On the third floor, Mrs. Alvarez opened her door, looked at the door, looked at Lyle, and closed hers again without comment.

By the time he reached his apartment, he hated the project and felt committed to it.

He leaned the door against the kitchen wall. The linoleum under his wet sneakers was the same slightly sticky shade it had been all year. The fridge gave its usual click-and-sigh.

Something inside the door answered.

Not a loose screw. Not debris sliding through cardboard lattice.

A low metallic vibration traveled through the slab and into the floor. Lyle felt it through the soles of his shoes, into the bones of his feet.

He waited.

The refrigerator clicked on.

The sound stopped.

“Okay,” he said.

He opened another beer. The tab left a neat crescent in his thumb. He sucked it without thinking and tasted metal.

Iris was at Margot’s for another week, which meant there was nobody present to ask why he had brought home part of an abandoned building. He enjoyed the freedom for almost three minutes. The apartment held its ordinary smells — laundry powder, radiator dust, the faint onion of a kitchen used alone.

Then the door sounded again.

This time he put his ear against it.

The interior was cold. Colder than the kitchen. Somewhere beneath the cheap veneer, something vibrated in sympathy with his breathing.

Lyle straightened. The back of his neck knew before he did.

He checked the knob hole. The hardware had been removed, but something remained wrapped inside the hollow space. He worked two fingers through to the second knuckle and touched damp cloth.

It gave the way a thing gives when it has been packed in wet and left.

He got a butter knife from the drawer that stuck.

The cloth came free reluctantly, snagging on the cardboard ribs inside the door. It might once have been part of a shirt. The fabric had gone almost black and smelled of copper, river mud, and the inside of an old radiator. Grit clung under his fingernail.

Wrapped inside was an instrument about the length of his hand.

It was made of brass or bone or something that had spent enough time in water to become both. Two narrow prongs curved away from a threaded stem. One end tapered to a point polished smooth by use. Mineral deposits had formed around the joints like little pale teeth.

Lyle turned it over under the kitchen’s single good bulb.

It looked medical.

It also looked industrial.

It looked, with equal credibility, like something once sold in a velvet bag to lonely men.

He held it to the light. One prong threw back a dull gold; the other stayed filmed with alley grit.

“Classy,” he said.

The instrument trembled between his fingers.

Not a rattle. A slow regular flex, twice, with a pause between, like something turning over in its sleep.

He nearly dropped it.

The copper groove around the door had begun to empty. The rainwater moved slowly along the channel, gathering at the missing knob hole as though the entire frame had tilted toward the object.

Lyle set the instrument in a cereal bowl. A faded dinosaur still swam under the glaze. A hairline crack he had been ignoring since spring crossed one side.

The vibration stopped.

He stood there, waiting for his common sense to arrive. His hands hung empty and wet at his sides.

It did not.

He ran the tap. The pipes knocked once in the wall.

The first splash struck the instrument and darkened immediately. By the time the bowl was half full, the water had turned the colour of weak tea. Thin mineral threads unwound from the prongs and curled toward the surface.

The smell changed.

Not stronger. Older. Wet penny. A basement opened late.

Lyle bent closer. His elbows stuck slightly to the laminate.

The water trembled.

He thought, briefly, of a hand held flat against the other side of glass.

Then the bowl cracked.

Water spread across the counter, under the microwave, through a stack of unopened mail. Lyle grabbed the nearest towel, which proved to be an oven mitt, then sacrificed one of Iris’s school T-shirts before remembering the actual towels existed.

The water ran off the counter.

It crossed the kitchen floor in branching lines, taking the same three routes twice, which floors do not let water do. In the stove’s low spill of light the paths looked almost deliberate.

Some of it disappeared between the boards with a soft continuous hush.

Lyle watched it go. He wiped his hands on his jeans and still felt the mineral grit under one nail.

Somebody began pounding on the apartment door.

2

FORTY MINUTES

Harold from 2B stood in the hallway holding a saucepan.

Water dripped steadily into it from the dark stain spreading along Lyle’s ceiling line — tik… tik… tik — each drop a small clear bell in the gravy-coloured hall carpet. The bare bulb over the landing made Harold’s silver hair look thinner on the side that still stood straight up.

Harold was seventy-two, widowed, and built from the soft, durable materials of a man who had survived three surgeries and forty years of union meetings. He wore a navy tracksuit with a stripe gone ivory and plaid slippers darkened at the toes. One shoulder of the tracksuit was already wet.

“You’re leaking,” Harold said.

“I’m aware of how that sounds.”

“You’re leaking into my bedroom.”

“That sounds worse.”

Harold held up the saucepan.

A drop landed inside it.

“Can I come in?”

Lyle stepped aside. The apartment still smelled like wet penny and school cotton from the T-shirt on the floor.

Harold entered and immediately saw the hollow-core door against the kitchen wall — beige under landlord white, copper edge dull, bottom still swollen from the alley. The groove held a last thin shine of water.

He stopped so completely the next drop missed the pan and hit the linoleum.

“What is that?”

“A desk.”

“That is a door.”

“It’s in transition.”

Harold walked toward the kitchen. His slippers made soft adhesive sounds on the wet floor.

Lyle moved faster.

“Careful. There’s water.”

“I gathered that from my ceiling.”

The cracked dinosaur bowl sat in the sink. The key lay inside it beneath several inches of brownish water, mineral teeth dull under the tea-dark surface. Thin streams still crossed the counter and found, again, the same three routes toward the boards.

Harold leaned closer.

“Is that rust?”

“Probably.”

“You don’t know?”

“I’ve had a difficult evening.”

Harold touched the wet counter with two fingers.

His fingers went still.

The saucepan slipped from his other hand and struck the floor with a soft metallic clang that rolled under the fridge.

Lyle reached for him.

“Harold?”

Harold inhaled.

His shoulders rose under the tracksuit.

When they lowered, they settled somewhere new — not younger; more arranged. The room seemed to tilt around that arrangement: the door in peripheral vision, the key in the bowl, Lyle’s hands half-raised and useless.

He straightened slowly, vertebra by vertebra. One hand went to his jaw. He opened his mouth, shifted it left, then right, testing the hinge. A small click near the joint.

Lyle stopped moving. The fridge hummed like it wanted no part of this.

Harold looked down at his own hands.

He flexed them. Harold’s knuckles, spotted and thick — the motion precise, as if someone were learning a glove from the inside.

His expression was not confusion.

It was appraisal.

Then he looked at Lyle.

“You put it in a cereal bowl.”

Harold’s voice had not changed. The words had. They arrived without Harold’s preparatory throat-clear.

“What?”

“The key.”

Harold stepped toward the sink. A slipper skated half an inch and corrected.

Lyle blocked him. Hallway soap on Harold; under it, copper and rain still clinging to the door.

“Sit down.”

“No.”

“I think you’re having a stroke.”

“I think your diagnosis is compromised by panic and very limited education.”

Harold reached past him toward the bowl.

Lyle caught his wrist.

The skin was warm. The pulse beneath it was too fast, ticking against Lyle’s thumb like another drop in another pan.

Harold looked down at Lyle’s hand.

Then back up.

“Take your hand off me.”

The sentence was quiet enough that Lyle obeyed. His palm stayed open in the air a second too long.

Harold lifted the key from the water. Brown droplets ran down the prongs onto the dinosaur’s remaining eye.

The prongs vibrated against his palm hard enough that Lyle saw the skin shiver.

His face changed.

Only slightly.

Relief moved through it so quickly Lyle might have imagined it.

“Where did you find this?”

“In the alley.”

Harold stared at him. A drip from the ceiling hit the empty saucepan on the floor — the only friendly sound in the room.

“The alley.”

“Behind the dumpster.”

A laugh escaped Harold’s mouth. Sharp and sudden, nothing like Harold’s wheezing chuckle.

“Of course.”

“Who are you?”

Harold ignored the question.

He turned the object in his hands, running one thumb over the mineral buildup. The touch was intimate. Familiar. Tool-knowledge, not curiosity.

Lyle backed toward his phone on the table, past the damp island of unpaid bills.

Harold noticed.

“Don’t.”

“I’m calling an ambulance.”

“For whom?”

“Harold.”

Harold glanced down at the tracksuit, the wet shoulder, the plaid slippers darkening where kitchen water had found them.

“Yes. He is concerned.”

“Can he hear me?”

A pause. The hall clock ticked with unnecessary confidence.

“Not clearly.”

She said it the way you answer a question about someone asleep in the next room, and went back to what she had been doing, which was rolling Harold’s shoulders one at a time and reading the results. Heat liniment rose faintly from the tracksuit.

“God, he’s held together with string and spite.” The left arm went up and stopped short of vertical; the elbow trembled. “What’s happened here.”

“He had the shoulder done.”

“Somebody had it done to him.” She lowered the arm carefully, which was somehow worse than if she had dropped it. “There’s a man in here who hasn’t reached above his own head in four years and has stopped noticing.”

“What did you do to him?”

“I used an opening.”

“That is not an answer.”

“It’s the one you’re getting.”

Lyle picked up his phone. The screen woke on Iris’s gap-tooth school photo for half a second before he could turn it away.

Harold crossed the room much faster than Harold should have been able to move and took it from him. The motion nearly made his bad knee buckle. His free hand caught the counter; knuckles whitened on wet laminate.

For the first time, pain showed.

Not hers, Lyle thought.

Harold’s.

She looked down at the knee, annoyed by the information it had contributed. A muscle jumped once under the tracksuit.

Lyle said, “Get out of him.”

Her head lifted.

He was certain now that the person looking at him was a woman, though he could not have explained why without sounding deranged. It was not softness or pitch or gesture. It was simply the shape of her attention — exits and obligations measured in the same glance. Behind her, the hollow-core door waited in the edge of sight like a third party.

“I will,” she said.

“When?”

“When the body begins rejecting me.”

“That sounds medically bad.”

“It’s rarely pleasant.”

“How long?”

She checked the wall clock. Its second hand had a hitch Lyle had never fixed.

“Perhaps forty minutes.”

Lyle stared at her. Below them, water found Harold’s bedroom floor in a thin continuous thread.

“That’s your estimate?”

“It’s Harold’s blood pressure.”

“Do you know him?”

“I know his body is unhappy and that he thinks your kitchen smells like a wet penny.”

“Can he see this?”

Her gaze flicked to the door, to the copper edge, to the paths the water had chosen twice.

“Fragments.”

“What does that mean?”

“It means you ask too many questions while pointing none of them in a useful direction.”

“You’re inside my neighbour.”

“Yes.”

“He didn’t say you could do that.”

Something tightened around her mouth. Harold’s mouth. The difference was becoming a technicality Lyle could not afford.

For half a second, Lyle thought she might leave.

Instead, she placed the key carefully beside the sink and corrected its angle until it sat parallel with the counter. A last bead of brown water ran to the drain.

“No,” she said. “He didn’t.”

From below came the faint sound of water striking Harold’s bedroom floor.

The woman looked toward it. Harold’s chin lifted with the look.

“Your leak.”

“My leak?”

“You activated the water.”

“I poured tap water into a bowl.”

“With the key submerged.”

“I didn’t know.”

“Most damage begins that way.”

“That sounded rehearsed.”

“It wasn’t.”

Harold’s hand began to tremble. The key on the counter did not.

She watched the tremor travel from fingers to wrist.

The irritation left her face.

“How do I stop this?” Lyle asked.

“You don’t.”

“How do I get you out?”

“You wait.”

“That’s it?”

“Bodies are very good at defending themselves once they know they’re occupied.”

Harold’s breathing had become shallow. Sweat stood at the hairline where the upright silver still refused to lie down.

The woman gripped the counter. The laminate squeaked under her palms.

Lyle moved toward her.

She held up one hand. The fingers were Harold’s; the stop was not.

“Don’t.”

“You’re going to fall.”

“That will be Harold’s problem in approximately ten seconds.”

“Who are you?”

She looked at him, and something in the angle of it changed — less inventory, more decision.

“Freya.”

“Just Freya?”

“Do you require references?”

Harold’s knees gave.

Lyle caught him beneath the arms. The tracksuit was damp. Hallway soap and fear-sweat. For one strange instant, Freya remained present in Harold’s face while Lyle held the body upright. Furious, frightened, embarrassed — three things Harold’s face had never practiced holding together. The key stayed parallel on the counter. The door did not move.

“Keep the key dry,” she said.

Then she was gone.

Harold sagged against him.

The full weight of the body arrived at once — pounds, heat, a man who had stopped helping hold himself.

Lyle lowered him to the floor. Linoleum cold through his jeans.

Harold’s right hand kept going for another second and a half — a small precise correcting movement against the lino, squaring nothing with nothing — and then stopped.

Harold opened his eyes.

He looked at Lyle, at the wet kitchen, at his own bare hand in a brownish smear of old water. The hollow-core still leaned where Lyle had left it, copper edge catching the single good bulb.

“Why are you hugging me?”

“You fell.”

“I did not.”

“You absolutely did.”

Harold tried to stand and winced when he put weight on his knee. The sound he made was pure Harold: offended by physics.

“What happened?”

Lyle looked at the key beside the sink, parallel, still, mineral teeth dull gold.

“Water.”

Harold stared at him for several seconds. A drop hit the saucepan. Neither of them laughed.

“I have always disliked you,” he said.

“That seems fair.”

Later, after Harold’s daughter collected him — coat half on, voice inventing legal categories — and threatened Lyle with both a plumber and litigation, Lyle sat at the kitchen table with the key wrapped in a dry dish towel. The good towel. His hands stayed on the wrap a long time, as if the fabric might forget its job if he let go.

He wrote seventeen questions on the back of an overdue electric bill. The pen dug through where the paper had gone soft.

He crossed out eleven.

At midnight the fridge sounded like a person thinking. He filled a clean glass with water under the single good bulb.

He held the key above it. His hand shook once and steadied. The copper groove on the door across the room held no water now — only a darker line where water had been.

He did not lower the key.

At twelve-fifteen, he poured the water into the sink and watched it take an ordinary path, for once.

At twelve-twenty, he filled the glass again.

The key waited in the towel like a small held breath. The door waited against the wall. The apartment smelled, faintly, of wet penny still.

3

THERE YOU ARE

For four days, nothing happened.

Lyle kept the key dry.

Mostly.

He carried it from room to room wrapped in the dish towel and told himself this was because he did not trust the plumbing. At night he set it on the windowsill beside his bed, where the cheap blue streetlight through the blinds laid a cold stripe across the wrap. The hollow-core door stayed against the kitchen wall. Sometimes, passing it, he felt the copper edge with the back of a knuckle without meaning to.

He tried talking to it once.

“Freya?”

The object did nothing. The towel stayed warm only with his own hand.

He felt ridiculous.

He said it again anyway.

Harold returned from urgent care with a knee brace, a prescription adjustment, and no memory of entering Lyle’s kitchen. He did remember a dream in which he hated Lyle’s furniture with unusual intensity. In the hallway the gravy carpet still held a pale ring where the saucepan had sat.

“You don’t have enough chairs,” Harold said, brace clicking once as he shifted weight.

“I have four chairs.”

“None of them are where they should be.”

Lyle watched him go. His own hands were empty; he put them in his pockets so they would stop looking that way.

On the fifth day, a package arrived.

The driver was a woman in her thirties with a dark ponytail, a knee brace, and the tense efficiency of someone twelve stops behind schedule.

She stood on the landing with a cardboard box tucked beneath one arm. The bare bulb made the wet shoulders of her uniform shine. Rain had darkened the fabric; the package was wet along one corner.

“Lyle Mercer?”

“That depends.”

She glanced at the label.

“It says Lyle Mercer.”

“Then yes.”

He reached for the box.

Her fingers touched his.

Nothing happened. Skin, cold rain, a paper cut on her knuckle that was not his business.

Lyle felt both relieved and disappointed.

Then a drop of water slid from the box onto the threshold.

The key, wrapped on the kitchen counter beyond the open door, gave a faint metallic hum. Inside the apartment the wet-penny smell lifted as if someone had opened a drawer.

The driver looked past him — past his face, into the kitchen, toward the place the hum had come from.

Her grip tightened on the box.

“Ma’am?”

She inhaled sharply.

The scanner fell against its wrist strap.

Her free hand rose to her jaw and did the thing Harold’s had done, and did it faster, because this body was thirty years younger and the hinge worked.

Lyle’s heart stumbled. Behind him the hollow-core was a beige rectangle in the corner of his eye.

The driver looked at him.

A crease appeared between her eyebrows.

It was not the driver’s crease. He had no proof of that.

“You’re late,” he said.

Her expression sharpened.

“Excuse me?”

Lyle almost apologized.

Then she looked toward the kitchen before looking at him.

The same order.

Object first.

Person second.

Something loosened in his chest.

“There you are,” he said.

The driver went still.

Rainwater dripped from the package between them onto the landing’s scuffed paint.

Freya looked down at the stranger’s hand holding the box. Then she looked back at Lyle.

“You recognized me.”

“You do the jaw thing.”

“I do not have a jaw.”

“You borrow one very judgmentally.”

Freya stepped inside.

Lyle moved aside automatically, then remembered she had not been invited and nearly stepped back in front of her. The hesitation amused her. The driver’s wet boot left a dark map on his linoleum that joined the older water routes without asking.

She placed the package on the nearest chair.

Not the floor.

The chair.

Then she adjusted it two inches away from the wall.

Lyle pointed.

“That too.”

“What?”

“You fix things before you insult me.”

“I have not insulted you.”

“You’ve been here fourteen seconds.”

“I’m pacing myself.”

She moved toward the kitchen and unwrapped the key. The single good bulb caught the mineral teeth. Freya’s borrowed fingers closed on the prongs with the same intimate certainty Harold’s had shown — tool, not toy.

The driver’s hands were callused along the palms. Freya turned them over once, noticing.

“Who is she?” Lyle asked. He kept his own hands visible on the back of a chair.

Freya glanced at him.

“The woman whose shift you are currently ruining.”

“What’s her name?”

“I don’t know.”

“Can she hear us?”

“Some of it.”

“How much?”

Freya looked away — toward the door, then the key, then nothing.

“Enough that she will remember disliking you.”

“She already did.”

“She has excellent instincts.”

Lyle took the package from the chair and inspected the wet corner. Cardboard gone soft; ink bloomed on the label.

“How did this happen?”

“The water touched the key’s field.”

“The key has a field?”

“No. I’m simplifying.”

“Don’t.”

“You will regret that request.”

The driver’s bad knee shifted. The brace clicked.

Freya corrected her weight onto the other leg. The counter took her palm; laminate cool and still faintly sticky from four nights of ordinary living.

Lyle noticed.

“Does that hurt her?”

“Yes.”

“You’re still doing it.”

Freya’s eyes met his. The driver’s eyes. The attention shape was Freya’s.

“You wanted me to come back.”

“I didn’t ask you to use her.”

“You prepared water.”

“I kept the key dry.”

“You kept it near the open door during a storm.”

“That is not the same thing.”

“No,” she said. “It isn’t.”

Again, no defence.

Lyle hated how effective that was.

He retrieved the overdue electric bill from the counter. Eleven questions crossed out; six remaining in his handwriting, the pen digs still visible where the paper had been soft.

Freya saw the questions.

“You wrote them down.”

“Some of them.”

“How disciplined.”

“Are you dead?”

“Yes.”

“Are you a ghost?”

“No.”

“What are you?”

“Still dead.”

“That’s not helpful.”

“It is accurate.”

Freya leaned against the counter, then immediately straightened when the driver’s knee protested. A flash of mineral smell when her hip brushed the sink edge near where the key had lain in tea-dark water days ago.

Lyle watched her disguise the pain.

“How long do you have?”

She checked the driver’s pulse at the wrist, two fingers precise.

“Less than Harold.”

“Because of the knee?”

“Because she is angry.”

“At you?”

“At being interrupted.”

Lyle lowered the paper.

“You can feel that?”

“I can feel everything the body does.”

“Can she feel you?”

Freya did not answer.

The refrigerator clicked off.

The apartment became very quiet. Radiator dust. Laundry powder under the copper. Rain on the landing outside the still-open door.

Lyle looked at the stranger’s face and tried to imagine another person beneath the expression, dreaming through it.

Freya tapped her fingers against the counter.

Two fast.

One slow.

Two fast.

“You did that before,” Lyle said.

Her hand stopped.

“When?”

“Harold.”

“I was not Harold.”

“You know what I mean.”

“No. I know what you’re trying to mean.”

Lyle moved closer. The loose caster on his chair complained when he left it.

Freya’s posture changed.

Not retreat exactly.

Preparation. The hollow-core stayed in her peripheral vision the way it stayed in his.

“You adjusted your jaw,” he said. “You line things up. You tap when you’re annoyed.”

“I am frequently annoyed. That is not identity.”

“You look at the key before you look at me.”

“That is survival.”

“You came back.”

“That is logistics.”

“Sure.”

Freya’s mouth tightened.

Lyle smiled.

The expression made her angrier, which confirmed more than it should have.

“There you are,” he said again.

This time she stepped back.

The driver’s shoulder struck the refrigerator. The door shuddered; something small inside rattled and stopped.

Lyle stopped smiling.

Freya stared at him as though he had opened a door behind her.

“Don’t say that.”

“Why?”

“Because you don’t know where I am.”

“I know you’re here.”

“You know I am inside someone.”

“I know it’s you.”

“You know three gestures.”

“I knew before you spoke.”

Freya looked toward the hall. Rain still ticked on the landing.

The driver’s breathing had quickened.

Lyle could not tell whose fear it was.

He softened his voice.

“When you’re gone, are you asleep?”

“No.”

“Can you see anything?”

“No.”

“Can you hear?”

“No.”

“Can you tell how much time passes?”

Freya’s fingers began the tapping pattern again.

She stopped them with the other hand. The calluses on the driver’s palm rasped softly.

“What is it like?” he asked.

She looked at him.

The driver’s face was younger than Harold’s, sharper, easier to imagine as Freya’s if Lyle let himself. He understood suddenly how dangerous that habit could become.

“It is not like anything,” she said. “That is the problem.”

“Does it hurt?”

Freya considered lying.

He saw the decision happen — a small stillness in the borrowed face before the truth.

“Yes.”

The driver’s knee buckled.

Freya caught herself on the table. The electric bill slid; Lyle’s hand stopped it without looking.

Lyle reached out, then waited.

She nodded once.

He supported her elbow.

The contact was practical. Almost formal. His fingers registered the uniform’s wet cold and the heat underneath.

Still, neither of them moved away.

“How do I bring you back without doing this to someone?” he asked.

“You can’t.”

“There has to be a way.”

“There are better ways.”

“What does that mean?”

“It means willingness changes the pressure.”

“So someone can agree?”

“Yes.”

“And you didn’t ask Harold.”

“No.”

“Or her.”

“No.”

Lyle looked toward the front door. Open. Rain. The building’s margarine light on the landing.

Freya followed his gaze.

“You can hate me later,” she said.

“I don’t hate you.”

“That is not necessarily better.”

Her body shuddered.

Lyle tightened his grip, then remembered himself.

Freya looked at his hand around the driver’s arm.

“Let go.”

He did. His hand felt suddenly underemployed.

She steadied herself. Weight on the good leg. Key still on the counter, not parallel now — she had left it where the towel fell.

“When I leave, keep her sitting down. Her knee is swollen. Give her the package. Do not tell her she was possessed.”

“What do I tell her?”

“You are very creative when avoiding responsibility. I have confidence in you.”

“Will you come back?”

Freya looked at him.

The driver’s face held the question differently than Harold’s could have — younger hinge, sharper crease, same attention underneath.

“I should not.”

“That’s not what I asked.”

“No.”

“Will you?”

Freya smiled despite herself.

“I want to know what else you notice.”

Then her eyes rolled upward.

The driver collapsed into Lyle’s arms. Dead weight, rain-cold uniform, the box edge bruising his ribs.

He lowered her onto the chair. The one she had already squared to the wall.

She woke almost immediately and shoved him away.

“What the hell?”

“You fainted.”

“I do not faint.”

“You did today.”

She checked her scanner.

Seven minutes had passed.

Her knee had swollen badly beneath the brace. She pressed two fingers there and hissed through her teeth.

She looked at Lyle, then at the package. Then, for half a second, at the hollow-core door, as if it had spoken.

“Did you open this?”

“No.”

“Did you touch me?”

“Only when you fell.”

Her face said she did not believe him.

Lyle signed for the package. The pen dug; his hand was not quite steady.

He gave her a bag of frozen peas for the knee. Frost stuck to his fingers and melted.

She left with the peas and no explanation. Her wet boot maps on the linoleum dried into ghosts of the routes the key-water had already taught the floor.

Lyle watched her get into the van and sit in it for a while before she started the engine. Through the rain-streaked windshield her hands were on the wheel the way his had been on the towel — holding a job so it would not forget itself.

He did not know her name. He had not asked for it, and he did not ask afterwards, and it did not occur to him that afternoon or at any point in the following week that somebody probably should have.

On the counter the key lay half in its towel. He wrapped it fully. Parallel was Freya’s habit; he left it slightly crooked on purpose, and then corrected it anyway.

The door did not comment.

The apartment smelled of rain, peas-frost, and wet penny fading.

4

THE RUDE LADY

Iris came home sick on Friday.

Margot stood in the doorway with one hand pressed against Iris’s forehead and the other gripping the strap of her purse. Organic bag at her feet; celery again, or something that wanted to be. The landing’s bare bulb made Iris look greyer than fever alone.

“She was fine this morning.”

“She looks like a Victorian orphan.”

“She has a fever, Lyle.”

“I know. Victorian orphans were famously feverish.”

Margot did not laugh.

She handed him the overnight bag. The strap left a red line across her palm.

“Children’s ibuprofen at six. She needs fluids. Not soda pretending to be fluids.”

“I know how to care for our child.”

Margot looked past him into the apartment.

The hollow-core door still leaned against the kitchen wall. Copper edge dull. Groove dark where water had been and would be again.

“What is that?”

“A future desk.”

“It’s moldy.”

“It has character.”

“It has tetanus.”

“You can’t get tetanus from a door.”

Margot looked at him. Left foot slightly forward on the threshold — her not-rushed stance that always meant she was rushed.

“Please do not treat that sentence as a challenge.”

She knelt and hugged Iris. Coat hem puddled. Iris endured the hug with the patient misery of the ill, face against Margot’s shoulder for the inspection-length of three seconds, then released.

When Margot left, Lyle locked the door and carried Iris to the couch. She was lighter and hotter than she should have been. The linoleum under his socks still held faint ghosts of older water routes if you knew where to look.

By seven, the fever had climbed. The humidifier ticked and breathed in the corner.

By eight, Iris had rejected soup, toast, crackers, apple slices, and one medicinal spoonful of honey on the grounds that it “felt suspicious.”

At eight-thirty, Lyle called a sitter service because they were out of fever reducer and the delivery app had marked his address “temporarily unavailable” after an incident involving Harold’s ceiling.

The sitter arrived at nine.

Her name was Bethany. She was nineteen or twenty, wore a university sweatshirt, and smelled strongly of vanilla. She had brought a developmental psychology textbook and the expression of a person confident she could handle one mildly sick child for forty minutes.

Before she arrived, Lyle had filled a glass with water and placed it beside the wrapped key on the kitchen counter. The single good bulb put a hard little star in the water’s surface.

He had not submerged the key.

He had only wanted the option.

Then Iris coughed from the bedroom, and Lyle went to check her temperature. By the time he remembered the glass, Bethany was already washing her hands in the bathroom sink. Vanilla and soap.

The key sat on the counter beside the humidifier.

Steam condensed along its prongs. Mineral teeth went bright, then dull.

Lyle saw it.

He looked at Bethany through the open bathroom door. Water still ran.

Nothing happened.

He wrapped the key in a hand towel — not the good dish towel; a thin one — and shoved it into the medicine cabinet. The metal shelf was already sweating.

Then he left for the pharmacy. Hands empty in his coat pockets, still feeling grit that wasn’t there.

He told himself the danger had passed.

At the store, there was no strawberry electrolyte.

He bought grape.

When he returned, the apartment smelled of vanilla, fever, and wet penny under the humidifier’s breath. Bethany sat on the couch with Iris’s rabbit in her lap.

The developmental psychology textbook lay closed on the floor, spine up.

Iris was awake in the bedroom.

Lyle could hear her humming to herself — a thin, unwell line of sound.

Bethany looked at the grocery bag.

“You bought grape.”

He stopped. The bag’s plastic handles cut into his fingers.

Bethany lifted one eyebrow.

The gesture was not familiar.

The judgment was.

“No,” Lyle said.

“Excellent opening.”

“You need to leave.”

“I only just arrived.”

“You know what I mean.”

Bethany stood.

She was shorter than Freya’s previous hosts, and Freya seemed irritated by the altered distance to the floor. She adjusted the shoulders of the sweatshirt. The rabbit hung from one hand by an ear.

“You left the key beside a humidifier.”

“I put it away.”

“You put it in a metal cabinet full of condensation.”

“That sounds less responsible when you say it.”

“It was less responsible when you did it.”

Lyle set the pharmacy bag down. Grape bottles knocked softly together.

“How long have you been inside her?”

Freya looked at the wall clock. Second hand hitch, same as always.

“Six minutes.”

“Did she agree?”

“No.”

“Get out.”

Freya’s expression changed.

Not anger.

Disappointment.

Lyle felt it like a personal accusation and resented himself for that.

“Iris is awake,” Freya said.

“I know.”

“She has been asking for you.”

“I was buying medicine.”

“She thinks you left because she threw up on the rug.”

Lyle’s eyes went to the rug without meaning to. A pale scrubbed place. He looked away.

“That is absurd.”

“She is seven.”

Lyle moved toward the bedroom.

Freya stepped into his path. Vanilla and fever-heat and the shape of attention that was not Bethany’s.

“Don’t tell her you were worried.”

“Why not?”

“Because you express concern as blame.”

“I do not.”

“You told her the thermometer was expensive.”

“She bit it.”

“She had chills.”

“She still bit it.”

The bedroom door opened.

Iris stood in the hallway wrapped in her blanket, rabbitless and furious. Damp hair stuck to her forehead. Cheeks flushed the wrong colour for anger alone.

“You took him.”

Bethany looked down at the stuffed rabbit in her hands.

“I borrowed him.”

“You didn’t ask.”

Lyle closed his eyes briefly.

Freya noticed.

Iris walked closer. Bare feet on linoleum. She looked at Bethany for a long time — face, hands, shoulders, the placement of her feet. The same order she would one day use on her mother.

Then she looked at Lyle.

“Is she weird?”

“Yes,” Lyle said.

Freya said, “Rude.”

Iris looked back at Bethany.

“You’re the rude lady.”

Freya stopped.

Lyle felt the room tilt — door in the kitchen edge of vision, humidifier breathing, rabbit ear in Freya’s grip.

Iris pointed.

“You were the package lady.”

“Delivery driver,” Lyle said automatically.

It had been another Tuesday. She had stood on his step in somebody else’s uniform with a parcel for 2B, looked him up and down once, and said, wear the green one more often, you look less like a man selling his own furniture — and then handed him the wrong parcel and left.

“She said your apartment smelled like wet socks.”

“I said damp plaster.”

“You said he should burn the couch.”

“I maintain that position.”

Iris stepped nearer. The blanket trailed.

“You hold your hands the same.”

Bethany’s hands had curled slightly at her sides — Freya’s inventory habit in a student’s fingers.

Freya looked down at them.

“You look at stuff before people,” Iris continued. “And you do this.”

She tapped the doorframe.

Two fast.

One slow.

Two fast.

Freya stared at her.

Lyle waited for a joke.

None came. The humidifier exhaled.

“Yes,” Freya said.

Iris considered that.

“Are you sick too?”

“No.”

“Then why are you in Bethany?”

Freya glanced at Lyle. Object first was not available; the child was the object.

“Because I don’t have my own body.”

“Did she give you hers?”

“No.”

Iris’s face tightened in the way it did when she was working something out.

“Does she know?”

“Not all the way.”

“Will she be mad?”

“Yes.”

Iris looked at Lyle. His hands were still full of nothing useful.

“Are you going to pay her extra?”

“Yes.”

“Double?”

“Yes.”

Freya said, “That is not how ethics work.”

Lyle looked at her.

“Thank you, haunting conscience.”

Iris coughed. A wet, small sound.

Freya moved toward her.

Lyle put out one hand.

Freya stopped immediately.

The obedience surprised him. His hand stayed in the air between them, then lowered.

“I only want to check her forehead,” she said.

“You can ask.”

Freya looked at Iris.

“May I?”

Iris thought about it. Blanket up to her mouth.

“Yes.”

Freya knelt.

Bethany’s knees cracked.

Her face twitched in discomfort.

Iris noticed.

“You’re hurting her.”

“A little.”

“You should sit.”

Freya sat on the floor. The vanilla sweatshirt puddled. From there she was closer to Iris’s height than to Lyle’s.

Iris placed one hand against Bethany’s forehead instead.

“You’re colder than Dad.”

“Most objects are.”

Lyle retrieved the medicine. Cap stubborn; his thumb found the crescent habit of bottle lids.

Iris refused the grape drink but accepted the fever reducer after Freya told her it tasted terrible and would not improve with negotiation.

They ended up on the couch.

Iris sat beneath the blanket with the rabbit returned to her lap. Freya remained on the floor because Bethany’s knees hurt less there. Lyle sat in the armchair with the medicine bottle. The hollow-core watched from the kitchen without moving.

Nobody spoke for a while.

The apartment pipes clicked.

Iris leaned against the couch cushion.

“Tell me a story,” she said.

Freya looked at her.

“What kind?”

“One with a monster.”

“Why?”

“Because Dad’s stories don’t have any.”

“My stories have tax auditors.”

“They are not scary.”

“You have never been audited.”

Iris waited. One bare foot swung and stopped.

Freya studied the hollow-core door in the kitchen. Copper edge. Dark groove. Beige under landlord white.

“There was once a city built inside another city,” she began.

Lyle looked at her.

Freya ignored him.

“Every time a wall cracked, the people made a doorway. Every time a pipe leaked, they planted something beneath it. They stopped throwing broken things away because broken things had openings.”

“Was there a monster?” Iris asked.

“Eventually.”

“What did it eat?”

Freya’s fingers went still against Bethany’s knee.

“People who wandered where they weren’t invited.”

“That sounds like their fault.”

“Sometimes.”

“What did the monster look like?”

“Different depending on who survived seeing it.”

Iris frowned.

“That doesn’t make sense.”

“Good stories rarely do at first.”

“What happened to it?”

Freya looked toward the kitchen door — not the front door; the hollow-core.

“I don’t know.”

“You’re making it up.”

“Yes.”

“Then decide.”

Freya laughed softly. Bethany’s laugh, poorly fitted.

“I haven’t yet.”

Iris shifted beneath the blanket.

After a moment, she rested her head against the couch just above Freya’s shoulder, close enough that a centimetre would have been contact. Fever heat crossed the gap without touch.

“Tell it next time,” Iris said.

Freya did not answer.

Lyle watched her look at the child.

The sitter’s face was young and unfamiliar. Freya’s expression inside it was neither.

“I can’t stay much longer,” she said.

“Because Bethany hurts?”

“Yes.”

Iris nodded. Eyes half closed.

“Then go.”

Freya looked almost wounded.

Iris continued:

“And come back in somebody who said yes.”

The room became very still. Humidifier. Fridge. One drip somewhere Harold would hear if he were listening.

Lyle looked down at the medicine bottle. Label soft under his thumb.

Freya looked at Iris.

“Is that an order?”

“Yes.”

“You’re extremely small.”

“I’m sick.”

“That is not authority.”

“It is tonight.”

Freya smiled.

Not the dangerous smile she had worn through Harold.

Something quieter.

“I’ll try,” she said.

Iris closed her eyes.

“Strawberry next time.”

Lyle said, “They were out.”

Freya and Iris said, at the same time:

“You didn’t ask.”

Freya looked at Lyle.

Iris did not open her eyes.

For one strange second, the apartment arranged itself around the three of them as though it had always expected this shape — couch, floor, armchair; door in the kitchen; key sweating quietly in the cabinet.

Then Bethany’s smallest finger twitched.

Freya noticed.

She stood carefully. Knees loud.

“I have to leave.”

Lyle followed her to the bathroom. Vanilla stronger in the small space. Medicine cabinet still closed.

“Wait.”

Freya stopped beside the sink. Chrome spotted with old toothpaste.

“What?”

“When you come back…”

He lost the sentence. His hands found the edge of the sink and stayed there.

Freya waited.

Lyle looked toward the living room where Iris had begun to drift asleep. Rabbit ear over the blanket.

“Come back because you want to,” he said.

Freya’s face changed.

Not enough for him to understand.

“I do very little for only one reason,” she said.

Then she left.

Bethany collapsed against the bathroom wall.

Lyle caught her before she struck the tile. Cold tile smell. Her sweatshirt bunched under his hands.

When she woke, she remembered sitting on the couch and hearing the beginning of a story she had never learned.

She also remembered Iris asking whether she had agreed to something.

Bethany looked at Lyle with frightened suspicion. Young face. Wrong evening behind the eyes.

“What happened?”

Lyle opened his mouth.

The truth sounded impossible.

The lie arrived easily.

“You fainted.”

Bethany stared at him.

He paid her double. Bills from the kitchen drawer; his hands counted without looking.

She took the money.

Neither of them pretended that settled anything.

After she left, Lyle returned to the couch.

Iris was awake. Eyes open in the dim.

“You lied to her,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Are you going to tell Mom?”

“No.”

“Are you going to do it again?”

Lyle looked toward the bathroom.

The key lay dry inside the medicine cabinet — or as dry as a metal box of steam would allow. He could feel the cabinet’s presence without opening it.

“No,” he said.

Iris studied him. Face. Hands on his knees. Shoulders. Feet flat on the floor.

Then she closed her eyes.

The next morning, Lyle prepared a new glass of water under the single good bulb.

This time, before lowering the key, he wrote three rules on a sheet of paper. The pen dug. The counter was cool.

The first was:

ASK THE BODY FIRST.

He underlined it twice.

Then he waited to see whether Freya would come back.

The hollow-core leaned. The copper did not shine. The apartment smelled of grape he hadn’t poured, vanilla fading, and the ordinary dust of a radiator that had worked all night.

5

SCHOOL TOAST

Iris came back on the Friday.

Margot’s car double-parked outside the launderette because Margot did not come up any more, and Lyle went down for the handover in socks and shoes and no coat, which Margot noted without saying anything, in the way she had. The launderette’s steam ghosted the pavement. October air went straight through his shirt.

“Her reading log’s in the front pocket.”

“Got it.”

“It has to be signed. Not initialled. Signed.”

“I know.”

“You initialled it.”

“I know.”

Margot looked at him for a second longer than the exchange needed. Grocery bags in the back seat; left foot still angled toward the car as if the engine might leave without her.

“She’s had a week,” she said.

“What kind?”

“Ask her.”

Then she was gone, and Iris was standing on the kerb with her bag and her jacket over one arm, looking up at the building the way she did when she was deciding what her mood was going to be.

“There’s a smell,” she said.

“That’s the launderette.”

“It’s not.”

It was, in fact, not. Wet penny, faint, riding the stairwell down to meet them.

He had planned to say something about the door.

The door was leaning against the kitchen wall exactly where it had been for eleven days, beige, copper-edged, with a shallow groove around it that held water even when nothing had put water in it, and a knob hole he had begun to avoid looking directly at. The single good bulb made the copper look dirtier than it was.

Iris stopped in front of it, put down her bag, and considered it with her hands on her hips.

“Is it a desk?”

“It’s going to be a desk.”

“It’s a door.”

“It’s between things.”

“Like a caterpillar.”

“Exactly like a caterpillar.”

She accepted this completely and went to get the cereal, and Lyle stood in his kitchen and understood that he had prepared four separate explanations for a seven-year-old who had needed none of them. He found the back of a chair and held on.

She was angry with him on Saturday.

It came out of nowhere the way it did at that age, over a coat, and then not over a coat, and then over the fact that he had said he would come to the thing at the school in October and had then said the word probably, which she had heard, and had been carrying around since Tuesday. The coat still hung on the chair she was not using as a chair.

“You said probably.”

“I did say probably.”

“That means no.”

“It doesn’t always mean no.”

“It means no when you say it.”

That landed somewhere under the ribs and stayed there.

The old version of this — and he could feel it available to him, warm and ready, like a coat by the door — was to get down on her level and tell her how much he loved her, at length, with feeling, until the size of the feeling made the original complaint impossible to keep holding.

He had done that maybe forty times.

It worked every time, which was the problem.

He put the plates in the sink instead. Water on. Soap. His hands occupied.

“Okay,” he said.

“Okay what?”

“Okay, you’re right. Probably means no when I say it.” He turned the tap on. “I’ll tell you Wednesday. Yes or no, not probably. And if it’s no I’ll say why.”

Iris stood in the doorway with her arms crossed, waiting for the rest of it. Socks mismatched. Face set.

There wasn’t any rest of it.

After a while she said, “Fine,” in the tone of somebody who had been braced for a speech and had gone over the top into an empty field, and went to watch television, and was still faintly annoyed with him at bedtime, and he let her be.

He was proudest of that and could not have said so to anybody.

The chair had been broken since February.

It was Iris’s chair, the one at the end of the table, and one of the back struts had gone, and she had been sitting on a stool for eleven weeks and mentioning it approximately twice a week in the tone of a woman reporting a pothole to a council that had stopped answering.

Sunday morning he took the door outside.

The fire escape was cold through his socks before he remembered shoes. Eleven days of kitchen air left the hollow-core; alley orange was gone in daylight — just grey brick and a neighbour’s plant that had given up.

He had, briefly, a vision of himself as a man who owned a saw.

He owned a bread knife and most of a hacksaw blade with no handle, and he wrapped the blade in a dish towel and worked at a corner of the door for fifty minutes on the fire escape, and the veneer split badly, and inside the hollow core was a lattice of cardboard that came apart in his hands like something’s ribs. Cardboard dust on his wrists. Copper edge against his knee when he braced the slab. The groove had dried to a darker line, history more than water.

He cut two struts out of the ugliest part.

He glued them, clamped them with a bungee cord and a paperback, and let it set. The paperback was one of Margot’s she had never come back for.

The repair was visible from every angle in the room. Two pale wooden ribs, wrong-coloured, sanded badly, against forty years of dark varnish. His knuckle had a raw nick from the veneer that would scab and that he would stop noticing until something caught it.

Iris sat in it and rocked twice, testing.

“It’s ugly,” she said.

“It is.”

“Are you going to paint it?”

“No.”

“Good,” said Iris, and pulled the chair in, and that was the end of it.

The chair outlived a great many other things.

On Sunday afternoon Margot called about the reading log.

It went badly for about ninety seconds and then, for reasons Lyle could not afterwards reconstruct, it stopped going badly. He stood in the hall. Wallpaper scar where a picture had been.

“I signed it,” he said. “Actual signature. You can check.”

“I will check.”

“I know you will.”

“That’s not—” She stopped. “I’m not trying to catch you out.”

“You are a bit.”

“Yes.” A pause on the line. Traffic somewhere on her end. “All right, yes.”

And instead of the thing he normally did next — the joke, the small wound dressed up as wit, the sentence that made him the one being reasonable — Lyle stood in the hall and looked at the mark on the wallpaper where a picture had hung for six years and said:

“She had a hard week.”

“She did.”

“Do you want to tell me, or do you want me to ask her?”

There was a longer pause than the question needed.

“There’s a girl in her class,” Margot said.

They talked for eleven minutes. Nobody scored anything. At the end of it Margot said “Thank you” in a voice that had not had anything to do for a while, and Lyle put the phone on the counter afterward and did not feel clever, which was unfamiliar and not unpleasant. His hand stayed on the phone a second after the call ended.

He filled the glass at ten past nine on Sunday night.

He had told himself all weekend that he wasn’t going to, and then Iris was in bed, and the flat had that specific silence it had — fridge thinking, radiator dust, the door a quiet rectangle in the kitchen — and he got the key out of the towel and filled the glass and stood at the counter with the key in his hand about four inches above the water. Mineral teeth. Dull gold under the bulb. The nick on his knuckle white against the stem.

He had questions. Genuinely useful ones, that he had been writing on the back of the electric bill all week. What she was. Whether Harold was all right. Whether it hurt. Whether she could hear him now.

Whether she had been in the room on Friday.

The floor creaked in the hall.

Iris appeared in the doorway in the pyjamas with the fox on them, one hand on the frame, entirely unsurprised to find her father standing at the sink at night doing something.

“There’s a noise in the wall,” she said.

“That’s the pipes.”

“It’s a knocking one.”

“That’s what pipes do.”

She looked at the glass in front of him. She looked at the object in his hand, which was brass or bone and had little pale mineral teeth around the joints and which he had not, at any point, put down.

“What’s that?”

And there it was.

He could feel exactly how easy it would be. He had watched the water do it twice. Whatever came through was fast, and funny, and interested in him, and knew things, and Iris was seven and would have found it extraordinary and would have kept it secret if he asked her to, and he could have both — could have the woman and the daughter in the same room on a Sunday night, and told himself it was fine because nobody had said it wasn’t.

“It’s a tool,” Lyle said. “It came in the door.”

“What’s it for?”

“I don’t know yet.”

He put it down on the counter. Not quite parallel. Close enough that he noticed and left it.

Then he picked the glass up and poured it into the sink, and rinsed the glass, and put it upside down on the rack, and this took about four seconds and cost more than anything else he did that year. Water took the ordinary path. His hands did the ordinary work.

“Come on. Bed.”

“The knocking’s still going.”

“Then we’ll listen to it from your room.”

He sat on the end of her bed in the dark with his back against the wall, and the pipes did knock, twice fast and once slow and twice fast again, and neither of them said anything about it, and Iris was asleep before the fourth round. Her breathing evened. The fox on the pyjamas was a darker shape on darker cloth.

Lyle stayed a while.

Nobody saw him do it. His hands empty on his knees.

He went to bed pleased with himself, and lay there for some time arranging the story of it — the glass, the four inches, the sink — into the shape he would tell her when she came back, and how he would say it, and where in the telling he would stop and let her work out what it had cost him.

Then he went to sleep, and in the morning he initialled the reading log, because he had already signed it once and could not remember whether that counted. The pen was the kitchen pen. The nick on his knuckle caught the paper once and did not bleed.

6

STRONG CIRCULATION PREFERRED

The first paid exorcism began because Jared Bell drank from the wrong paper cup.

Lyle had gone to the basement of Saint Bartholomew’s Community Annex looking for a person who knew what the laryngeal key was.

The stairs down smelled of floor wax and old coffee. The handrail was metal, cold, sticky in patches where tape had been pulled off and never cleaned. Overhead, a fluorescent panel buzzed at a pitch that lived behind the eyes. At the bottom the air changed — warmer, drier, furnace-fed, with a faint damp under it as if the concrete never fully forgot rain.

The basement was longer than it was wide. Folding chairs had been set in two uneven rows facing a folding table. The floor was sealed concrete painted a sad institutional grey, scuffed into pale paths where feet had learned the routes to the coffee urn and the exit. Along one wall: a mural of smiling cartoon fish from a previous tenant, the paint bubbled near the baseboard. Along the other: the furnace behind a low rail, ticking itself toward usefulness. Acoustics were bad in the useful way — voices carried, secrets did not stay secrets, every chair-leg scrape arrived twice.

The event was called THE VEIL IS THIN: AN EVENING OF SPIRITUAL EXCHANGE, which sounded promising until he discovered that spiritual exchange meant twelve folding chairs, supermarket cookies on a paper doily, and a woman named Denise selling crystals beside the furnace on a card table with one leg shorter than the others.

The church had not organized the gathering. The church merely rented the basement to anyone willing to stack the chairs afterward. Maintained: the exit signs, the fire extinguisher with a current tag. Improvised: everything human.

Lyle had found the event through a flyer taped inside a launderette — the same launderette steam that ghosted the pavement under his building.

He brought the key wrapped in a dish towel. The good towel. It still held a faint kitchen smell under the copper that lived in the key now.

He also brought the hollow-core door because the key behaved differently near it, although carrying an entire door down those sticky-railed stairs made him feel less like an investigator and more like a contractor experiencing a private emergency. The copper edge knocked the doorframe once with a familiar tok. His knuckle nick from the fire escape caught the towel and did not bleed.

Donna Bell noticed him immediately.

She sat in the front row wearing a purple cardigan, three silver necklaces, and the attentive expression of someone who had spent years waiting for the universe to stop being coy. She had claimed the chair closest to Denise’s table — maximum proximity to whatever might happen.

“That door has an attachment,” she told him.

Lyle leaned it against the basement wall, copper groove facing the room, swollen bottom on the grey paint. Under the fluorescents the beige looked cheaper than it did in his kitchen.

“It came that way.”

Donna smiled.

“I meant spiritually.”

“So did I.”

This delighted her.

Donna had brought her son Jared because her car was in the shop and because Jared, despite being twenty-six, unemployed, and philosophically opposed to mornings, still drove her places when asked.

He occupied a chair in the back row with his hood pulled up and both legs stretched into the aisle — maximum distance from his mother and from sincerity. He had a thick beard, expensive sneakers, and the soft heaviness of a man whose primary exercise was explaining why a business had not launched yet. Cheap body spray traveled farther than he did.

He was recording something on his phone. The screen lit his face from below, annex-horror lighting for free.

Donna twisted around.

“Put that away.”

“It’s content.”

“You said you were between seasons.”

“I’m collecting bonus material.”

“You have nine listeners.”

“Nine confirmed listeners.”

Lyle sat two chairs away from him — close enough to watch, far enough to deny association — and placed the wrapped key beneath his seat. The dish towel made a small white flag against the grey floor. From here he could see the door, the exit stairs, and Donna’s necklaces catching every flicker of the bad fluorescents.

Jared glanced at the door.

“You carrying that around for spiritual reasons?”

“It’s complicated.”

“It’s a door.”

“That’s where the complication begins.”

At the front of the basement, Denise rang a brass bowl.

It produced a dull clunk that the concrete took and returned smaller.

Denise frowned at it and rang it again. Same clunk. The fish mural did not care.

“Tonight,” she announced, “we are gathering in a space of radical openness.”

The furnace turned on with a low industrial inhale. Warm air pushed the crystal table’s incense sideways. Heat found the backs of necks in the front row first.

Someone’s coat fell from the back of a chair. The scrape of the hanger-hook on metal echoed twice.

Denise closed her eyes.

“We invite any benevolent presences to make themselves known.”

Nothing did.

Lyle waited.

The key remained still beneath his chair.

For the next twenty minutes, the group performed breathing exercises, discussed recurring dreams, and passed around a gallon jug of water Donna had ordered from a website called Angel Springs Direct.

The label promised the water had been “vibrationally blessed at a frequency of 963 Hz.”

Lyle read it twice.

“That’s not how water works,” Jared whispered.

“You came willingly,” Lyle said.

“I drove my mom.”

“That is how cults acquire most men.”

Jared grinned.

The jug reached them.

Donna poured water into three paper cups and handed one to Lyle.

“You should hold your object near it.”

“My object?”

“The attachment.”

“I don’t think it likes being called that.”

Jared took a cup.

“I don’t think the plumbing part has feelings.”

The key hummed beneath Lyle’s chair.

He stopped. Wet-penny, faint under incense and body spray — his kitchen’s weather in the wrong building.

The sound was faint, nearly hidden by the furnace.

Jared lifted the cup. Paper rim wet. Blessed water catching the fluorescent stutter.

“Don’t drink that,” Lyle said.

Jared drank it.

The key vibrated hard enough to strike the underside of the chair. A sharp metallic complaint against metal legs. The dish towel jumped.

Jared lowered the cup.

His face emptied. Not peaceful — vacated.

Donna leaned toward him.

“Sweetheart?”

Jared’s shoulders pulled back.

His spine straightened.

He seemed to discover his full height one vertebra at a time.

One hand rose to his jaw, and Lyle — who had watched a pensioner do this in his kitchen and a delivery driver do it in a car park — stood so quickly his chair folded beneath him.

Jared was four inches taller than either of them and the movement had further to travel.

Jared looked around the basement.

His gaze passed over Donna, Denise, the table of crystals, the fish mural, the door against the wall — object-first, then the room’s exits, then the living.

Then he looked at Lyle.

“There you are,” Lyle said. The phrase left his mouth before he could sand it down for public.

Jared’s mouth tightened.

“You brought the door to church.”

“Technically, it’s an annex.”

“What is wrong with you?”

“A lot, according to several written evaluations.”

Freya looked down at Jared’s body.

She flexed his hands.

Rolled his shoulders.

Touched the beard.

Her fingers disappeared into it.

“This is appalling.”

Donna gasped.

“Jared.”

Freya tugged the beard experimentally.

“He’s hidden half his fucking face under an animal.” She rolled the shoulders, then the hips, and something in the lower back answered in a way she clearly had not expected. “Twenty-six,” she said, with real wonder. “Twenty-six, and every fucking thing in here already aches. What have you been doing to it?”

“Nothing,” Donna said.

“Yes. I can tell.”

“Don’t pull it,” Donna said. “He’s sensitive about the beard.”

Freya looked at her.

Jared’s face focused.

Donna went quiet.

“Who are you?” she asked.

Freya looked at Lyle.

Lyle looked toward the wrapped key under the collapsed chair.

He had not prepared the water.

Not intentionally.

The blessed water had touched Jared, the key had been near, and somehow that had been enough.

“Freya,” he said.

Donna’s eyes widened.

“You know her?”

“Not well.”

Freya scoffed.

Lyle corrected himself.

“Better than I’d like.”

“That is not what you said last time.”

Jared’s voice was still Jared’s, but the rhythm belonged entirely to Freya.

Donna stood.

“Jared, can you hear me?”

Freya tilted her head.

Beneath her expression, something moved.

Jared’s fingers tapped once against his thigh.

Then twice.

A fast, nervous beat.

Donna saw it.

“That’s his panic thing.”

Freya looked down at the hand.

It stopped.

“He’s frightened,” she said.

Donna pressed both hands to her mouth.

Denise approached from the front of the room, brass bowl held protectively against her chest.

“Is this a demonstration?”

“No,” Lyle said.

Freya said, “Yes.”

They looked at each other.

Denise brightened.

“I knew the energy had shifted.”

“The furnace came on,” Jared said from somewhere inside Jared.

Freya’s face twitched.

Lyle heard it too.

The words had come through Jared’s mouth, but not in Freya’s cadence.

Donna rushed forward.

“Jared?”

Freya staggered back.

Her hand clamped over his mouth.

For one second, genuine alarm crossed his face.

Then she regained control.

“He’s present,” Lyle said.

“I am aware.”

“Can he get out?”

“Can you stop asking that as if I’m a locked bathroom?”

Donna grabbed Lyle’s sleeve.

“You have to help him.”

“I’m working on it.”

“You said you knew her.”

“Knew is doing aggressive work in that sentence.”

Freya walked toward him.

Jared was four inches taller than Lyle and considerably broader. Freya seemed pleased by both facts.

“You should not have activated the key in public,” she said quietly.

“I didn’t.”

“You brought it into a basement full of water and suggestible strangers.”

“The water came in a sealed jug.”

“It was vibrationally blessed.”

“That’s not real.”

Freya glanced toward Donna.

“Apparently it was sufficient.”

Donna was crying now.

Not loudly. Tears slid down her face while she watched her son stand differently inside his own body.

Lyle’s stomach tightened.

“Leave him,” Lyle whispered.

“I just arrived,” Freya said through Jared’s mouth.

“He didn’t agree.”

“No one here did.”

“That doesn’t improve it.”

Freya’s expression hardened.

Behind her, Denise whispered something about spiritual warfare.

Donna heard her.

“Is that what this is?”

“No,” Lyle said.

“Yes,” Denise said.

Freya said, “It might be.”

Donna seized on the answer she could use.

“Can she be removed?”

Lyle opened his mouth.

The correct answer was that Freya would eventually be forced out by Jared’s body.

The honest answer was that Lyle did not fully understand how any of this worked.

The safest answer was to call an ambulance.

Then he noticed the envelope Donna had placed beneath the donation bowl.

Cash protruded from it.

He thought of the overdue electric bill.

He thought of Iris asking whether Bethany had agreed.

He thought of Freya disappearing into absolute silence.

“No,” he said.

Donna’s face collapsed.

Then Lyle heard himself continue.

“Not without a controlled extraction.”

Freya looked at him.

Very slowly, Jared’s eyebrows rose.

“A what?” Donna asked.

“A controlled extraction.”

Denise nodded as though this was a familiar term.

Lyle continued because stopping would have required courage.

“The entity has established a resonant pathway through your son’s…” He searched the basement for vocabulary. “Upper system.”

Freya stared at him.

Donna touched Jared’s chest.

“His heart?”

“Broader than the heart.”

“The soul,” Denise whispered.

Lyle pointed at her.

“Adjacent.”

Freya turned away so Donna would not see her smile.

“What do you need?” Donna asked.

Lyle surveyed the room.

A refreshment table.

Paper cups.

Salt packets.

A smoke detector on the wall.

Denise’s brass bowl.

A whisk resting beside a tray of cookie dough dip.

“I need copper,” he said.

Donna began removing her necklaces.

“Not jewelry.”

Lyle crossed to the wall and unplugged an extension cord.

Denise protested.

“That belongs to the church.”

“It belongs to the annex.”

“That’s still theft.”

“It is being requisitioned. Sit down, Denise.”

He stripped the cord with a butter knife.

Freya watched him wind copper around the whisk.

“What is that?” she asked.

“A conductive cross.”

“It is visibly a whisk.”

“Not to the spirit realm.”

“I am the spirit realm.”

“Then cooperate.”

Donna heard only enough to become frightened again.

“Is she resisting?”

“Violently.”

Freya folded Jared’s arms.

Lyle lowered his voice.

“You need to make this look difficult.”

“It is difficult. You’re holding a whisk.”

“React to it.”

“No.”

“I’ll give the money back.”

“What money?”

“Future money.”

Freya glanced at Donna.

Understanding dawned.

Her smile disappeared.

“You’re charging her?”

“I didn’t say that.”

“You’re building a pricing structure with your eyes.”

“Help me get you out of Jared before he gets hurt.”

“And afterward?”

“We discuss it.”

“You mean you improvise a moral defence.”

“I’m excellent under pressure.”

“You are sweating into a church extension cord.”

Donna touched Lyle’s shoulder.

“What should I do?”

“Dim the lights.”

“They don’t dim,” Denise said.

Freya looked up at the fluorescent panels.

Jared’s body reached overhead, unscrewed one bulb, then another.

Half the basement dropped into shadow.

Donna screamed.

Freya handed the hot bulbs to Denise.

“There.”

Denise accepted them reverently.

Lyle placed the wrapped key in the brass bowl and poured the remaining blessed water over it.

The bowl began to hum.

Everyone heard it.

The joke thinned.

Water trembled around the instrument. The copper wire tightened against the whisk as though contracting.

Freya’s amusement faded.

“What did you do?” she asked.

“The same thing as usual.”

“No. You added copper.”

“You told me copper carried the field.”

“I said it carried pressure.”

“Those sound related.”

“They are related in the way a bath and drowning are related.”

Donna gripped the back of a chair.

“Is it working?”

Freya’s head snapped toward her.

Jared’s body shuddered.

The hand against his thigh began knocking again.

One-two.

One-two.

Faster.

“Jared?” Donna said.

Freya’s teeth clenched.

Lyle saw the blood before she did.

A dark line slid from Jared’s left nostril into his beard.

His eyes had begun to water.

“Freya.”

“I know.”

“Leave.”

“I can stabilize him.”

Jared’s chest hitched.

His left hand curled inward.

Donna rushed forward, but Lyle held out the whisk.

“Stay back.”

He had no idea whether that mattered.

It sounded authoritative.

Freya wiped the blood with the back of Jared’s hand.

“He does this.”

“Does what?”

“Bleeds.”

“Not like that.”

“You don’t know him.”

“Neither do you.”

Freya glared at him.

Jared’s body swayed.

Lyle put down the whisk.

He caught Jared’s face between both hands. The nick on his knuckle pressed into beard and heat.

“Get out.”

Freya went still.

The beard was wiry and damp at the edges. There was a warm animal smell coming off the man that was entirely Jared’s — cheap body spray, sweat, the inside of a hood worn all day. Under Lyle’s right palm a jaw he had never touched in his life worked once, slowly, like it was chewing on a decision. Behind Freya’s eyes the annex fluorescents made two small cold rooms.

And behind all of it, looking out, furious at him for ordering her away and frightened that he might be right, was her.

The room went away for about half a second.

Freya’s gaze dropped to his mouth.

Lyle’s did the same.

He was going to. That was the appalling part — he had time to know it. He was going to kiss Jared Bell full on the mouth in a church annex in front of the man’s mother, and he was going to mean it, and none of the meaning was going to be for Jared.

Then Donna threw a handful of salt.

It struck Jared in the side of the face.

Freya recoiled.

“What the fuck, Donna?”

Donna crossed herself.

Denise gasped.

Lyle blinked salt from his eyelashes.

Freya looked back at him.

“Do that again,” she said.

Lyle stared.

Donna whispered, “It’s taunting you.”

Freya smiled with Jared’s mouth.

“For the ritual.”

Jared’s knees buckled.

The smile vanished.

Lyle caught him beneath the arms.

“Freya.”

“I can hold him.”

“No.”

“I have him.”

“He is not something you have.”

The words reached her.

Freya’s expression changed.

Jared’s body trembled against him.

His hand struck Lyle’s side once.

Twice.

A frightened signal from somewhere behind her.

Lyle lowered his voice.

“Leave him.”

Freya looked at Donna.

Then at the key vibrating in the bowl.

Then back at Lyle.

“Catch his head,” she said.

“What?”

“Catch it.”

Freya left.

Jared’s entire weight collapsed.

Lyle barely stopped his skull from striking the concrete floor. The grey paint was gritty under his knees. Salt stuck to his palms.

Donna screamed his name.

Jared coughed.

His eyes opened.

He looked at the ceiling.

Then at Lyle above him.

“Why are you holding me?”

“You fell.”

Jared swallowed.

His hand went to his bloody nose.

“What happened?”

Donna dropped beside him and wrapped both arms around his shoulders.

“You were possessed.”

Jared looked at Lyle.

Then at the whisk wrapped in copper wire.

“By a cooking show? Piss off.”

Donna laughed and sobbed at the same time.

Even Denise looked disappointed that the crisis had ended before she could ring the bowl again.

Lyle sat back on his heels.

Freya was gone.

He could no longer feel her anywhere.

One moment, she had been looking at him through Jared’s eyes.

The next, the body contained only Jared.

Lyle looked down at his empty hands.

Donna helped her son into a folding chair.

Denise brought tissues.

Someone found a cold bottle of water and pressed it to Jared’s neck.

The group gathered around him, speaking softly.

Nobody looked at Lyle for nearly a minute.

He stood beside the humming bowl, alone.

Donna approached him after Jared’s nose stopped bleeding.

She held the cash envelope.

“I don’t know how to thank you.”

“You don’t have to.”

“You saved my son.”

Lyle glanced at Jared.

Jared was conscious, pale, and trying to convince Denise that he did not need his aura combed.

“I didn’t exactly save him.”

Donna pressed the envelope into Lyle’s hand.

“You brought him back.”

Lyle felt the thickness of the bills.

He thought of handing them back.

He nearly did.

Then the basement lights flickered.

His electricity had been scheduled for disconnection that morning.

He closed his fingers around the envelope.

“Aftercare is important,” he said.

Donna nodded urgently.

“Of course.”

Lyle hated himself a manageable amount.

He slipped the envelope inside his coat.

The basement door opened.

A woman stepped inside wearing black work pants, heavy boots, and an employee badge from a twenty-four-hour pharmacy. Cold stairwell air came in with her and made the incense look stupid.

She was a little older than Jared, perhaps thirty. Her dark hair was pinned up with two pens. She smelled like hand sanitizer and night shift. She looked tired before she saw the room.

Then she saw:

the blood on Jared’s shirt,

Donna crying,

salt scattered across the floor,

two missing fluorescent bulbs,

the copper-wrapped whisk,

and Lyle standing beside a bowl of brown water.

Her tiredness became something sharper.

“Why is Jared bleeding?”

Donna rushed toward her.

“Cora, thank God.”

Cora did not take her eyes off Lyle.

“What happened?”

“He was possessed.”

Cora looked at Jared.

“By what, unemployment?”

Jared lifted his head.

“There was a woman.”

The joke left Cora’s face.

She crossed the room and crouched in front of him.

“Did you hit your head?”

“I don’t think so.”

“Do you know where you are?”

“Church basement.”

“Community annex,” Lyle said.

Cora looked over her shoulder.

“Who are you?”

Lyle considered several identities.

“Consultant.”

“With the church?”

“No.”

“With him?”

“Temporarily.”

Cora checked Jared’s pupils.

“Did he take anything?”

“No,” Donna said.

“The water,” Jared murmured.

Cora looked at the gallon jug.

Then the bowl.

Then the key submerged inside it.

“What is that?”

Lyle moved between her and the bowl.

“Specialized equipment.”

“That is a bone in soup.”

“It is not bone.”

“You don’t know what it is, do you?”

“I know several things it isn’t.”

Cora stood.

She was not tall, but she had the rare ability to make height seem administratively irrelevant.

Her eyes settled on the envelope bulging inside Lyle’s coat.

“How much did she give you?”

Lyle touched the pocket.

“That is not the important part.”

“That is a sentence people say when the amount is important.”

Donna stepped between them.

“Cora, stop. He helped.”

“He has a whisk wrapped in electrical wire.”

“It conducted the entity.”

Cora closed her eyes.

“Mom.”

“You weren’t here.”

“I was at work.”

“I called you.”

“I cannot leave a pharmacy because Jared starts speaking in bloody tongues.”

“It wasn’t tongues,” Jared said. “She was British or something.”

Lyle said, “Not British.”

Cora turned on him.

“You spoke to her?”

“Briefly.”

“How briefly?”

“Long enough to establish rapport.”

“You were flirting,” Jared said.

Lyle stared at him.

Donna stared at Jared.

Cora stared at Lyle.

“I was not.”

Jared touched his nose.

“You grabbed my face.”

“It was part of the extraction.”

“She liked it.”

“You were unconscious.”

“I wasn’t all the way unconscious.”

The room quieted.

Cora crouched again.

“What do you remember?”

Jared looked at his hands.

“Being crowded.”

Donna made a small sound.

Jared continued carefully.

“She was looking through me. I could feel her looking. She hated my beard.”

“That could have been anyone,” Cora said.

“She knew things.”

“What things?”

Jared rubbed his left wrist.

The wrist he had been pretending did not hurt.

“She knew about this.”

Cora looked at Donna.

Donna looked away.

Jared’s voice lowered.

“She wasn’t trying to hurt me.”

Lyle listened.

Jared stared at the floor.

“She just didn’t think I counted enough.”

Cora stood slowly.

She walked toward Lyle.

“Give my mother her money back.”

Lyle glanced at Donna.

Donna shook her head.

“No.”

“Mom.”

“He provided a service.”

“He electrocuted a whisk.”

“No electricity was applied to Jared.”

“That is not the defence you think it is.”

Lyle removed the envelope.

He looked inside.

Two hundred and forty pounds.

Donna’s car repair money, most likely.

His electric bill was two hundred and eleven.

He handed the envelope to Cora.

She counted it.

Then handed it to Donna.

Donna immediately tried to return it to Lyle.

Cora intercepted her wrist.

“No.”

“But he helped.”

“If he helped, he can send an invoice.”

Lyle said, “I can do that.”

Cora looked at him until the idea died.

As Lyle returned the envelope to his coat, a folded piece of yellow paper slipped from the inner pocket and fell to the floor.

Cora picked it up.

Lyle reached for it.

She stepped away.

The flyer read:

PAID HOST NEEDED

Consciousness-sharing experiment.

One hour maximum. Cash on completion.

No drugs.

Possible nausea, memory disturbance, emotional residue, temporary loss of motor control.

No sexual activity unless separately discussed and approved.

Must be over twenty-one.

Strong circulation preferred.

At the bottom was Lyle’s phone number.

Cora read the flyer twice.

Her eyes returned to one line.

“Strong circulation?”

Lyle cleared his throat.

“We had an issue with an older gentleman.”

“You have done this before?”

“Not for money.”

“This was for money.”

“This became for money.”

Donna peered over Cora’s shoulder.

“What does ‘host’ mean?”

“Nothing,” Cora and Lyle said together.

Jared held out his hand.

“Let me see.”

Cora gave him the flyer.

He read it.

“You have a sex clause?”

“It is a prohibition,” Lyle said.

“It says unless separately discussed.”

“Consent requires specificity.”

Cora looked at him.

“That may be the most upsetting correct thing you’ve said.”

Jared handed the flyer back.

“Who wrote this?”

“I did.”

“The formatting is terrible.”

“It’s a draft.”

Cora folded the paper along its original crease.

“You’re looking for people to let the woman inside them.”

“Yes.”

“Willingly.”

“Yes.”

“So what happened to Jared?”

Lyle looked at Jared’s bloodied shirt.

“An exception.”

Cora’s expression hardened.

“No. It happened first. The ethics came after.”

Lyle had no answer.

From the brass bowl, the key gave one quiet hum.

Everyone turned toward it.

Cora looked from the key to Jared.

Then to Lyle.

She tucked the flyer into her pocket.

Lyle blinked.

“Are you taking that?”

“Evidence.”

“Of what?”

“I haven’t decided.”

Donna touched Cora’s arm.

“You believe us?”

Cora looked at Jared.

He had begun tapping his fingers against his thigh.

One-two.

One-two.

His own rhythm — and then, with no change at all in his face, two fast, one slow, two fast, and then his own again.

Jared did not notice.

Cora did not know what she had just watched, only that she had watched it.

“I believe something happened,” she said.

Then she looked at Lyle again.

“And I believe this man should not be allowed to manage it unsupervised.”

Lyle almost smiled.

Cora saw that too.

“Do not mistake that for interest.”

“I wasn’t.”

“You were.”

“I find competence reassuring.”

“You met me forty seconds ago.”

“It has been a very efficient forty seconds.”

Cora pointed toward the bowl.

“Pack up your bone.”

“It isn’t bone.”

“Pack up your haunted cutlery and get out before I call the police.”
