Lyle retrieved the key from the tea-dark water in the bowl. Mineral teeth. Older smell lifting once, then gone under incense.

He wrapped it in the dish towel. Hands steady enough.

Then he lifted the hollow-core door. Copper edge cold. Weight he knew in his wrists from the alley stairs at home.

At the basement exit, he stopped beside Cora. The fluorescent buzz followed them to the door and lost.

She still had the flyer in her pocket.

“You seem responsible,” he said.

Cora stared at him.

Behind her, Jared sat with dried blood in his beard while Donna argued that spiritual services should be tax deductible.

Lyle nodded toward the flyer.

“The opening became available unexpectedly.”

Cora’s hand closed into a fist.

Lyle carried the door up the stairs before she could hit him.

Three nights later, his phone rang.

He answered on the second ring. Kitchen bulb. Door against the wall. Ordinary radiator dust.

“Lyle Mercer.”

Cora said, “Before we discuss anything, my mother gets every pound back.”

Lyle looked at the groceries on his counter. His hands were empty except for the phone.

“And after that?”

“One hour,” she said.

Behind her voice, he could hear Jared objecting.

Cora continued:

“He watches. We write down the rules. I choose the signal that ends it. She leaves immediately when I use it.”

Lyle sat down.

The key lay wrapped in front of him.

It had begun to hum.

“And if Freya says no?”

“Then you two can go back to exorcising each other with kitchen appliances.”

Lyle smiled.

“When can you come over?”

“I’m not coming to your apartment.”

“Why?”

“Because my first impression of you involved blood, fraud, and a whisk.”

“That’s fair.”

“I’ll send an address.”

“Cora?”

“What?”

“Why are you doing this?”

Silence.

When she answered, the humor had left her voice.

“Because Jared said she didn’t think he counted.”

Lyle looked at the key.

“And?”

“I want to see whether she can count when someone makes her.”

The line went dead.

The key hummed once beneath its towel.

Two fast.

One slow.

Two fast.

7

TERMS AND CONDITIONS

Cora chose the basement beneath Bellini’s because it had one door, no windows, and a floor drain.

The stairs down were concrete, painted the same grey as the annex but worn through to aggregate in the centre of each tread. Oil and tomato and bleach lived in the walls in layers. Sound went soft the moment the upstairs door shut — not silent; padded, as if the bulk cans and shelving absorbed argument before it could travel. One bare bulb on a pull chain at the landing. Everything else was clamp light or phone light or the freezer’s occasional interior flash when someone opened it for nothing.

She worked upstairs five nights a week, on top of the pharmacy, serving veal to people who believed snapping their fingers was a form of communication. The pharmacy was days and the restaurant was nights, and on Thursdays it was both, and she had explained this exactly once, to Lyle, in the tone of a woman reading out a meter reading. The restaurant closed at eleven. By eleven-thirty, the cooks had gone, the owner was asleep in his office, and the basement belonged to industrial shelving, bulk tomato cans with dust crowns, a floor drain that never fully dried, and a freezer that groaned every six minutes like something reconsidering death. Between groans: the drip of a pipe into a shallow pan someone had placed and never emptied.

Lyle arrived carrying the hollow-core door. Copper edge cold. The tok against the stairwell jamb was quieter here than in his building, swallowed by stock.

Jared followed him down the stairs. Expensive sneakers careful on the wet-looking paint that wasn’t wet.

“Why is he bringing that?” Cora asked.

“The key behaves better near it,” Lyle said.

“The key behaves?”

“Not in a way I can prove.”

Cora stood beside a folding table wearing jeans, a black sweater, and the expression she usually reserved for customers who discovered gratuity on the bill. Two pens already in her hair — pharmacy habit, restaurant permanence. The table was metal, cold enough that paper stuck slightly when laid flat. She had wiped it once with a bar towel; the towel hung on the chair back, damp, tomato-pink at one corner.

Three clamp lamps had been attached to an overhead pipe.

Their bulbs pointed upward. Hard white against industrial cream paint. Everyone’s eyes would sit in shadow; every page on the table would be bright.

Cora looked at them.

Then at Lyle.

“Why?” Jared asked.

“Visibility,” Lyle said.

“They’re lighting the ceiling.”

“It creates dimensionality.”

“It creates shadows under everyone’s nose.”

Lyle adjusted one lamp. The pipe ticked as the clamp bit. Heat began to gather under the metal shade.

“You complained about the basement.”

“I said it was dark.”

“Yes.”

“I didn’t ask you to make it look like an illegal surgery.”

Jared descended behind the door carrying two folding chairs and a paper bag of takeout containers. Grease had already mapped the bag. The smell of fried dough cut the tomato-bleach air.

“It looked worse before the lamps.”

Cora pointed at him.

“You’re here to observe,” Lyle said.

“I brought mozzarella sticks,” Jared said.

“You’re here to observe quietly.”

“I can do two things.”

“You once burned ramen.”

“The instructions were deceptive.”

Lyle leaned the door against the wall between a stack of 10s tomato and the floor drain. Its copper-edged channel was dry — darker line only, history. Under the clamp light the beige looked almost honest.

The laryngeal key sat wrapped in a dish towel on the folding table beside a plastic basin, a bottle of water, a roll of paper towels, and a first-aid kit Cora had brought herself. Surfaces arranged like a procedure: paper left, water center, body tools right. Space between each object large enough that nothing could brush by accident.

She had also brought:

a blood-pressure cuff,

a kitchen timer,

a legal pad,

two pens,

an instant cold pack,

a bottle of electrolyte drink,

and three copies of a six-page document titled:

TEMPORARY VOLUNTARY POSSESSION AGREEMENT

The paper was thick enough to hold a crease. Staples precise. Each stack squared to the table edge. Silence after the freezer’s groan made the rustle of Lyle picking up a copy sound like an admission.

Lyle picked up the contract. The page was cool. Ink black, dense, no generous leading.

“You made copies.”

“One for me, one for you, one for Jared.”

“Why does Jared get one?”

“Because the last time your ghost entered somebody, he was the somebody.”

Jared opened the paper bag.

“Do I sign?”

“No.”

“Can I eat?”

“Not over the forms.”

Lyle turned the first page. The staple caught his thumb. Single-spaced. Numbered sections. Subsections. Initial boxes like small empty mouths.

A handwritten addendum had been stapled to the back — different pen pressure, Cora still thinking after the printer stopped.

“This is longer than my divorce agreement.”

Cora pulled out a chair. Metal legs scraped once on concrete; the padded air took the scrape and kept it short.

“That explains your divorce.”

Lyle sat. The chair was too low for the table. Contract height became negotiation height: eyes slightly up to meet hers if she stood.

Jared made a sound that might have been a laugh and disguised it as a cough. Grease on his fingers; he kept them off the paper.

Cora remained standing. Advantage of paper author.

“Before we start, your mother’s money.”

Lyle reached into his coat.

He placed an envelope on the table.

Cora counted the bills.

Two hundred and forty pounds.

All of it.

She returned the envelope to her bag.

“You didn’t spend any?”

“I considered several purchases.”

“You bought those lamps.”

“They were returnable.”

“You kept the receipt?”

Lyle looked toward Jared.

Jared said, “He did not keep the receipt.”

Cora wrote something on the legal pad.

“What did you write?” Lyle asked.

“Pattern of poor documentation.”

“This is not an audit.”

“It became one when you brought props.”

“They’re equipment.”

Cora sat across from him.

“Read the agreement.”

“All six pages?”

“Out loud.”

“Why?”

“Because I want to hear which parts make you hesitate.”

Lyle looked at her.

Cora folded her arms.

He began.

“‘The host, hereafter Cora Bell, knowingly and voluntarily permits the occupying consciousness known as Freya, surname unknown—’”

“Deceased surname unknown,” Cora corrected.

“That feels unnecessary.”

“It establishes identity.”

“She’s dead.”

“That establishes status.”

Lyle wrote the word deceased above the line.

Jared ate a mozzarella stick.

“‘The permitted occupation shall begin only after verbal confirmation by the host and shall last no longer than sixty minutes.’”

“Fifty,” Cora said.

“The contract says sixty.”

“I changed my mind.”

“You can do that?”

“Yes.”

Lyle crossed out sixty and wrote fifty. Pen dig. The line through sixty was darker than it needed to be.

Cora initialed beside it. Her initial was small, exact, no flourish. The ink took a second to dry on the cheap photocopy stock; she did not smudge it with her sleeve.

“Keep going.”

“‘The host may terminate permission at any time, for any reason, including reasons not stated, understood, justified, or communicated.’”

Lyle looked up.

“That’s comprehensive.”

“It means no arguing.”

“I don’t argue.”

Both siblings stared at him.

Lyle returned to the page.

“‘Withdrawal shall be communicated by either the spoken phrase blue morning or three consecutive curls of the smallest finger on the host’s left hand.’”

He demonstrated with his own finger.

“That?”

Cora watched.

“Slower.”

He did it again.

“Why that signal?”

“Jared’s fingers kept moving when she controlled the rest.”

Jared stopped chewing.

Lyle lowered his hand.

Cora continued:

“If I can speak, I say blue morning. If I can’t, I move the finger. If neither works, Jared ends the session.”

“How?”

“He removes the key from the water.”

Lyle looked toward the wrapped instrument.

“That may not force her out.”

“Then he hits you with the door.”

Jared nodded.

“I accepted that responsibility.”

Lyle read on.

“‘Freya shall not access, repeat, investigate, or disclose private memories unrelated to the immediate operation of the host’s body.’”

He paused.

“How is she supposed to know whether a memory is private before accessing it?”

“She can ask.”

“She’d have to encounter it first.”

“Then she backs away.”

“From a memory?”

Cora tapped the contract.

“That is the rule.”

“This is metaphysically vague.”

“So is she.”

Lyle could not dispute that.

“‘Freya shall not contact any person by telephone, letter, electronic message, or in person while using the host’s body without specific permission.’”

“Reasonable.”

“‘Freya shall not consume alcohol, medication, tobacco, or recreational substances.’”

“Also reasonable.”

“‘She shall not cut, dye, shave, pierce, mark, injure, photograph, undress, or otherwise alter the host’s body or appearance.’”

Lyle glanced at Cora’s hair.

Two pens held it in a loose knot.

“Photograph?”

“I don’t want pictures of my face that I didn’t take.”

“Fair.”

He turned the page.

Then stopped.

Cora watched him.

“Read it.”

Lyle cleared his throat.

“‘There shall be no sexual activity.’”

“Continue.”

“‘This includes kissing, genital contact, breast contact, intentional arousal, undressing for erotic purposes, insertion, penetration, simulated sexual conduct, or use of the host’s body to solicit sexual attention.’”

Jared lowered the mozzarella stick.

Lyle looked at Cora.

“You wrote intentional arousal.”

“Yes.”

“How would we determine intent?”

“By asking whether you did it on purpose.”

“What if it happens accidentally?”

“Then nobody acts on it.”

“This could have been one sentence.”

“It was one sentence. Then I met you.”

Jared covered his mouth with one hand.

Lyle continued.

“‘Any future sexual activity requires a separate written agreement executed while the host is unoccupied.’”

He set the page down.

“That phrase is unpleasant.”

“Which one?”

“Unoccupied.”

Cora looked toward the hollow-core door.

“You’ve been calling people hosts.”

“That was her word.”

“It was yours on the flyer.”

“It was shorter than temporary corporeal provider.”

“That’s worse.”

“I know that now.”

The laryngeal key hummed beneath the towel.

Everyone became quiet.

Cora looked at it.

“Can she hear us?”

“No,” Lyle said.

“You’re sure?”

“When she isn’t in a body, she can’t hear, see, or feel anything.”

“Nothing?”

“Nothing.”

Cora looked at the contract.

Jared looked at his hands.

The freezer groaned.

Cora said, “Then we explain everything again when she arrives.”

Lyle reached for the water bottle.

“Wait.”

Cora rolled up her left sleeve.

She checked her pulse.

Then fitted the blood-pressure cuff around her arm.

Jared watched the numbers.

“Hundred eighteen over seventy-six.”

“Write it down,” she said.

He did.

Cora removed the cuff and placed both hands flat on the table.

“Before we do this, I want one thing clear.”

Lyle waited.

“I’m not helping you run exorcism scams.”

“We haven’t discussed employment.”

“You handed me an advertisement at my brother’s fake exorcism.”

“It was an exploratory offer.”

“I’m here to find out what happened to Jared. I’m here because another person who agrees is better than another person who doesn’t. And I’m here because I need the money.”

Lyle nodded.

“Those can all be true.”

“They stay separate.”

“What does that mean?”

“It means you don’t turn the money into gratitude. You don’t turn curiosity into permission. And you don’t decide that because I agreed once, I agreed next time.”

Lyle’s expression lost its humor.

“Okay.”

Cora studied him.

“Say it properly.”

“I understand.”

“No.”

Lyle looked at the contract.

“You agreeing tonight does not mean you agree next time.”

“Good.”

“And if you tell her to leave, she leaves.”

“If I tell her.”

“If you signal.”

“And if she doesn’t?”

Lyle looked at the door.

“Jared uses the door.”

Jared raised one hand.

“With enthusiasm.”

Cora opened the water bottle. Plastic crack. Cap set down on paper towels, not on the contract.

She poured it into the plastic basin.

The stream sounded too loud in the basement — a clean domestic sound in a place built for bulk and cold storage. Everyone listened to water as if it were a third party speaking.

Lyle unwrapped the key. Dish towel folded back with both hands. Under the clamp lamps, the brass-and-bone surface looked damp despite being dry. Mineral deposits clung to the prongs like little pale teeth. The pointed end caught the light. Wet-penny waited under tomato-bleach and fryer grease, not yet released.

Cora’s confidence flickered.

Only once.

Jared saw.

“So did mine,” he said.

Cora looked at him.

“The first second,” Jared continued. “Before she came in. It felt like missing a stair.”

Cora nodded.

“Were you scared?”

“Yes.”

“Did she know?”

“Yes.”

“What did she do?”

Jared rubbed his wrist.

“She stayed.”

Lyle lowered the key toward the basin.

Cora held up one hand.

“Not yet.”

He stopped.

She looked at Jared.

“You don’t leave.”

“I won’t.”

“You don’t let him explain anything away.”

“I’ll try.”

“Don’t try.”

“Fine.”

Then she looked at Lyle.

“When she comes in, don’t touch me.”

“Unless you fall.”

“Unless I fall.”

“No face grabbing.”

Lyle’s ears warmed.

Jared looked between them.

“What face grabbing?”

“Nothing,” Lyle said.

Cora said, “He grabbed your face.”

“I remember.”

“It was medical.”

“It was horny,” Jared said.

Lyle lowered the key into the water. Four inches, then contact. His knuckle nick white for a second against the stem.

The basin answered with a low vibration that ran into the metal table and into their wrists if they were touching it.

Cora inhaled. Hands still flat where she had left them — terms held in posture.

The water darkened around the prongs.

Thin brown threads unfurled from the metal and spread across the surface. Older smell, brief, wrong for a restaurant, familiar to Lyle alone until the others had no choice.

“Last chance,” Lyle said.

Cora looked at the basin.

Then at Jared.

Then back at Lyle.

“I consent to fifty minutes under the written terms.”

The hum rose.

Cora touched the water.

Her fingers stiffened.

She gasped.

Her chair scraped backward.

Jared stood.

Lyle gripped the edge of the table but did not reach for her.

Cora’s head bowed.

Her shoulders tightened.

For several seconds, nothing else happened.

Then her left hand rose to her jaw.

Her mouth opened.

Shifted left.

Then right.

“There you are,” he said, before her eyes had finished lifting.

Cora’s face turned toward him.

Freya looked first at the key.

Then at Lyle.

Then at Jared.

Her gaze dropped to the contract.

“What is this?”

Cora’s voice, Freya’s cadence.

Lyle smiled despite himself.

“Terms and conditions.”

Freya picked up the first page. Cora’s fingers, Freya’s care with paper — edges aligned, no bend.

Her eyes moved rapidly down the text. Clamp-light glare on the dense ink.

“You’ve unionized. Christ.”

Jared sat again.

“She did.”

Freya looked at him.

For an instant, Jared’s face tightened.

Freya saw it.

Her attention returned to the document.

“Cora volunteered?”

“Yes,” Lyle said.

“Why?”

“She has questions.”

“Everyone has questions. Most people lack stationery.”

Freya looked down at Cora’s body.

She flexed the hands carefully.

Tested each finger.

Rolled the shoulders.

She reached toward the pens in Cora’s hair.

Lyle said, “No alterations.”

Freya stopped.

Her eyes narrowed.

“You remembered the rule.”

“It’s been active fourteen seconds.”

“And already oppressive.”

She continued reading.

When she reached the sexual-activity section, she stopped.

Jared watched her.

Lyle pretended not to.

Freya read aloud:

“‘There shall be no insertion, penetration—’”

She stopped. Her gaze lifted to Lyle, then travelled down Cora’s own body, unhurried, taking inventory of the premises.

“A little late for that.”

“You know what it means.”

“I know what it says.” She turned the page back and read the line again, apparently for the pleasure of it. “Somebody has drafted a clause forbidding me to enter a body, inside a document whose entire purpose is to let me enter a body, and nobody in this basement has noticed except me.”

Jared choked on mozzarella.

Lyle rubbed his forehead.

“You know what it means.”

“I know what she hopes it means.”

Cora’s smallest finger twitched.

Once.

Not the signal.

A warning.

Freya looked down at it.

“Oh, she’s very present.”

“Can you hear her?” Jared asked.

“Not as speech.”

“What, then?”

“Pressure. Images. Objections.”

The finger twitched again.

Freya smiled.

“Mostly objections.”

Lyle pointed at the contract.

“No intentional arousal.”

“I am reading.”

“The way you’re reading is provocative.”

“I’m using her eyes.”

“You’re doing something with her mouth.”

“This mouth naturally appears skeptical.”

Cora’s finger curled once.

Freya flattened the hand on the table.

“Fine.”

Jared leaned toward her.

“Can you feel me?”

Freya’s humor faded.

“Yes.”

“Do you remember being inside me?”

“Yes.”

“Everything?”

“No.”

“What do you remember?”

Freya set down the contract.

Lyle glanced at the timer.

Forty-seven minutes remained.

Freya looked at Jared.

“Your left wrist hurt.”

“It still does.”

“You hated the fluorescent lights.”

“Everyone hates fluorescent lights.”

“You were afraid your mother would see you frightened.”

Jared’s jaw tightened.

“You kept thinking about Cora.”

Cora’s body reacted before Freya could control it.

A sharp intake of breath.

Jared noticed.

“What about her?”

“You wanted her to come.”

“She always comes.”

Freya looked down at Cora’s hands.

Something passed across her face.

Not memory.

Proximity to memory.

“She took you to the emergency room when you were fourteen.”

Jared stood.

Cora’s smallest finger curled once.

Twice.

Freya recoiled from the thought.

“I’m not looking.”

“You just said it.”

“It surfaced.”

“That’s private.”

“I didn’t ask for it.”

Cora’s third curl began.

Freya released the memory so quickly that Cora’s entire body jerked.

The finger straightened.

Nobody spoke.

Freya looked at the contract again.

“Private memories unrelated to operation,” she said.

Her voice had changed.

Less amused.

“I understand.”

Jared sat slowly.

“Did I ask you to leave?”

Freya did not answer.

“Last time,” he said. “Did I?”

“Yes.”

“Did you know I was asking?”

“Yes.”

“Why didn’t you?”

Freya looked toward Lyle.

He did not rescue her.

Cora’s body held very still around her.

“Because I had just arrived,” Freya said.

Jared’s face went blank.

Freya continued before she could soften it.

“I had been nowhere. Then I was in a body. I could hear. I could feel the room. I could see Lyle. You were frightened, and I knew that, but I had just arrived.”

Jared looked at his hands.

“So you stayed.”

“Yes.”

“Until he told you.”

Freya glanced at Lyle.

“Yes.”

Cora’s mouth tightened under her.

It was strange to watch two women disagree through one set of muscles.

“What is she saying?” Lyle asked.

Freya’s eyes remained on Jared.

“She thinks that is worse.”

“She’s right,” Jared said.

Freya looked at him.

For once, she had nothing sharp ready.

The kitchen timer ticked.

Forty-three minutes.

Lyle picked up the contract.

“Cora wants to know whether you agree to the rules.”

“I’m already here.”

“That isn’t an answer.”

Freya’s irritation returned, but thinner.

She read the withdrawal clause.

“Blue morning or three curls.”

“Yes.”

“And I leave immediately.”

“Yes.”

“What if leaving quickly harms her?”

Cora’s finger moved once.

Freya paused.

Then said, “She says that is her risk to choose.”

“Do you agree?”

Freya looked at the water.

The key vibrated softly inside the basin.

She could leave now.

Lyle could see the thought.

If she refused, Cora would end the session.

If she agreed, someone else would decide when Freya returned to nothing.

“I agree,” she said.

Jared wrote the time on the legal pad.

Lyle said, “Cora wants verbal confirmation.”

Freya looked annoyed.

Then:

“I agree to leave immediately upon the phrase blue morning or three consecutive curls of the smallest finger. I agree that tonight’s permission applies only tonight. I agree not to access private memories deliberately. I agree not to alter, injure, intoxicate, undress, photograph, contact others through, or sexually use Cora’s body.”

Her eyes shifted to Lyle during the last phrase.

Cora’s pulse jumped visibly in her throat.

Freya felt it.

So did Lyle.

Nobody mentioned it.

Jared did.

“Her heart rate went up.”

Cora’s eyes closed.

Freya said, “Bodies react.”

“That’s in the contract,” Jared said.

Lyle checked.

“It is.”

Freya leaned closer to read over his shoulder.

Cora’s hair smelled faintly of coffee and restaurant soap — the stamp the annex hadn’t had yet, earned here under lamps.

Lyle noticed.

Freya noticed him noticing. Space between collar and hovering fingers held like a clause.

Her voice lowered.

“You wore the shirt.”

Lyle looked down.

It was the dark green button-down she had once approved through somebody else’s uniform.

“You told me to.”

“I didn’t think you listened.”

“I listen.”

“To compliments.”

“Those are still words.”

Freya reached toward his collar.

Stopped before touching him.

Her fingers hovered beside the third button.

It sat in the fourth hole.

Freya smiled.

“You’ve done it again.”

Lyle glanced down.

“Done what?”

“Buttoned it wrong.”

Jared groaned.

“You dressed yourself in the basement.”

“It looked correct upstairs.”

Freya’s hand remained near his chest.

Not touching.

Cora’s pulse quickened again.

Freya went still.

Her expression changed.

“What?” Lyle asked.

“She can feel me.”

“We established that.”

“No.”

Freya looked down at Cora’s body.

“She can feel what I feel.”

Jared stopped eating.

“What does that mean,” he said. “Practically.”

Freya did not take her eyes off Lyle.

“It means that when I looked at him a moment ago, her mouth went dry and her stomach dropped and there is heat in this body that she did not put there and cannot get out of, and she is lying underneath all of it perfectly still, hoping nobody in the room can tell.”

Jared set the mozzarella stick down on the table.

“Can we tell?”

“Her brother can’t.” Freya looked away at last. “Her brother is the only one.”

Cora’s smallest finger twitched, but did not curl.

Freya looked at Lyle.

Somewhere under the black sweater, Cora’s heart did something that every person in the basement could see in her throat, and that only one of them had chosen.

“You’re attracted to me,” Lyle said.

Freya laughed.

Cora’s voice made it brighter than usual.

“That is an ambitious interpretation.”

“You’re using her pulse as a smoke screen.”

“It is her pulse.”

“But your feeling.”

“And her body.”

Jared raised the contract.

“No intentional arousal.”

Freya did not look away from Lyle.

“Nothing intentional has occurred.”

“Your hand is still near his shirt,” Jared said.

Freya lowered it.

Cora’s shoulders relaxed.

Lyle said, “Are you attracted to me?”

Freya’s eyes narrowed.

“You don’t know what I look like.”

“That wasn’t the question.”

Freya stared at him.

Then Cora’s finger curled sharply.

Once.

Lyle stopped breathing.

Twice.

Freya’s face changed.

The flirtation vanished.

“Cora?”

The third curl came slowly.

Freya stepped backward from Lyle.

“Blue morning,” she said herself.

Then she left.

Cora collapsed sideways.

Jared caught her before her head struck the table. Contracts skidded; one page drank a drop of brown water at the corner and bloomed.

The chair tipped. Metal scream short in the padded air.

Lyle grabbed the basin and moved it out of the way as water sloshed across the floor toward the drain that had justified the room. The key rang once against plastic.

Cora opened her eyes almost immediately.

She shoved Jared’s hand from her shoulder and rolled onto her knees.

“Timer.”

Jared checked it.

“Thirty-eight minutes left.”

Cora sat against the freezer.

Her breathing was rapid.

Lyle crouched several feet away.

He kept his hands visible.

“Are you hurt?”

Cora looked at him.

Her face was hers again.

That distinction was already frighteningly easy to make.

“No.”

“Why did you signal?”

She swallowed.

Jared opened the electrolyte drink and handed it to her.

Cora took it.

“She was enjoying it.”

Lyle looked toward the key.

Freya could not hear them.

Cora continued:

“She was enjoying being here. The body. The room. You.”

Jared sat beside her.

Cora pressed the cold bottle against her neck.

“And I could feel her wanting you.”

Lyle looked away.

“No — ” Cora stopped, and started again, because she had tried the easy sentence first and it had not been accurate. “It isn’t that I could tell she wanted you. It’s that I wanted you. In here.” She put two fingers flat against her own sternum, without any tenderness whatsoever, the way you point at an error on a form. “With my body. Using my parts. And I have met you twice, and I don’t, and there was nowhere in me to put it.”

“That violated the contract?”

“No.”

Cora’s answer surprised him.

“She stopped before touching you. She was trying to follow it.”

“Then why end the session?”

“Because I wanted to.”

Lyle nodded.

Cora watched him accept them.

“She left immediately,” Jared said.

Cora looked at her smallest finger.

“Yes.”

“Before you finished the signal,” Lyle said.

“After the third curl.”

“She said the phrase herself.”

Cora’s expression shifted.

“She knew.”

Lyle looked at the empty place where Freya had been.

“She always knows more than she admits.”

“That isn’t what I mean.”

Cora drank.

“She felt me want out. She didn’t make me prove it.”

Jared stared at the floor.

The freezer groaned behind them.

After several minutes, Cora stood.

Her knees nearly failed.

Lyle moved instinctively.

Cora held up one hand.

He stopped.

She steadied herself on the table.

“Payment.”

Lyle blinked.

“Now?”

“Possession is work.”

He took out the envelope he had prepared.

Two hundred pounds.

Cora counted it.

Jared watched.

“You’re keeping it?”

“Yes.”

“I thought you wanted Mom’s money back.”

“That was Mom’s money.”

“This is mine.”

She put the cash in her pocket.

“Dinner is friendship. Possession is work.”

Lyle frowned.

“Nobody offered dinner.”

“You brought restaurant food into a restaurant basement,” Jared said.

Cora pointed toward the mozzarella sticks.

“Those were marked waste at closing.”

“Upcycling.”

Cora gathered the contracts. Squared the stack. Damp corner isolated face-out so it would dry without sticking.

She wrote the actual end time beside the clause and signed beneath it. Pen pressure firm. No flourish.

Then she added:

HOST TERMINATED WITHOUT EXPLANATION. EXIT WAS IMMEDIATE.

Lyle read it.

“You’re documenting that positively?”

“I’m documenting what happened.”

“Will you do it again?”

Cora capped her pen.

“I don’t know.”

Lyle looked at the basin a second too long.

She saw that.

“Don’t make that face.”

“What face?”

“The one where you confuse access with affection.”

Lyle looked toward the key again.

“I barely know you.”

“That has not protected anyone so far.”

Jared stacked the folding chairs.

Cora picked up her bag.

At the basement stairs, her knees buckled once more.

She caught the railing.

“Sit down,” Jared said.

“I’m fine.”

“You’re shaking.”

“I have to close the register.”

“You’re off the clock.”

“I’m never off the clock.”

A small voice came from the top of the stairs.

“Dad?”

Lyle looked up.

Iris stood in the doorway wearing pyjama pants beneath her winter coat. Mrs. Alvarez stood behind her, holding Iris’s overnight bag and looking furious.

“She woke up,” Mrs. Alvarez said. “You were not upstairs.”

“I left a note.”

“You left a note that said basement.”

“It was accurate.”

“I am going home.”

Mrs. Alvarez handed Iris the bag and left before Lyle could thank her.

Iris descended slowly.

She saw Jared.

Then Cora.

Then the basin of brown water.

Her eyes moved to the contracts.

“Did she come?”

“Yes,” Lyle said.

Iris looked at Cora.

“Is she still in there?”

“No,” Cora said.

Iris studied her face.

Then her hands.

Cora let her.

After a moment, Iris nodded.

“You’re Cora.”

“I’m Cora.”

“You look sick.”

“I feel sick.”

Iris opened her overnight bag.

She removed a bottle of strawberry electrolyte drink.

“I saved this.”

Lyle stared.

“You said you drank it.”

“I said I opened it.”

“That was strongly implied.”

Iris handed the bottle to Cora.

Cora accepted it.

“Thank you.”

“It tastes better cold.”

“I believe you.”

Iris looked toward the key.

“Did Freya listen?”

Cora glanced at Lyle.

“Yes.”

“All the way?”

Cora considered the question.

“When I told her to leave, she left.”

Iris smiled.

Not widely, but enough.

“Good.”

She climbed onto one of the folding chairs Jared had not yet stacked and pulled her coat tighter around herself.

Cora opened the strawberry drink.

She sat beside Iris.

Jared found another mozzarella stick.

Lyle began collecting the wet paper towels.

For ten minutes, nobody spoke about possession.

Iris asked Cora why restaurants put parsley on things nobody intended to eat. Cora explained that adults decorated food when they wanted to charge more for it. Jared disputed the economic model. Lyle found four unopened packets of marinara and warmed them on top of the freezer motor.

Cora stayed until her hands stopped trembling.

Freya perceived none of it.

The next time she entered, she would find that Iris knew Cora’s name.

That Cora had a preferred drink.

That Jared had begun calling the basement “the office.”

That Lyle had bought a fourth folding chair.

She would find a room that had continued arranging itself after she was gone.

For now, Cora drank strawberry electrolyte under the crooked lamps while Iris explained that grape tasted like medicine pretending not to be medicine.

On the folding table, beside the dry key, the contract waited — cool again, edges soft from handling, denser with the night than with ink.

At the bottom of the final page, beneath Cora’s signature, Lyle wrote one additional rule. His hand was not quite steady; the letters stayed clear anyway.

Nobody is furniture.

He stared at the sentence.

Then he underlined it.

Once.

Not twice. The freezer groaned. The pipe dripped into its pan. Upstairs, nothing knew.

PART II

HOUSE RULES

8

THE DINOSAUR PROJECT

Iris’s teacher had assigned a family tradition.

“Not a holiday,” the rubric specified. “Something your family does that is only yours.”

Iris sat at the kitchen table with construction paper the colour of cheap construction-site orange, scissors sticky at the hinge, and the expression of a child who had realised too late that her family’s primary tradition was fraudulent spiritual extraction. Evening through the plastic blinds was that thin hospital blue that made glitter look like something medical. The hollow-core leaned in its usual place; the copper groove held no water tonight, only the darker memory of it.

“We could do pancakes,” Lyle said. “We do pancakes.”

“Everyone does pancakes.”

“We do them on Tuesdays.”

“That’s not a tradition. That’s just when you remember to buy batter.”

Lyle was washing dishes. He had washed the same plate three times. Foam dying on the rim. His hands red at the knuckles, the nick from the fire-escape pale against soap.

Freya was in Cora, who sat across from Iris with a cup of tea that was mostly honey — amber skin on the surface under the single good bulb. Cora’s body wore a cardigan she had explicitly agreed Freya could borrow from Lyle’s closet, provided nothing underneath was removed. The cardigan was too large and smelled like Lyle’s detergent. History on sleeves: dishwasher steam, someone else’s shoulders.

“What do we do that nobody else does?” Iris asked.

Cora’s finger tapped the table.

Two fast.

One slow.

Two fast.

“We tell stories,” Freya said.

“Everyone tells stories.”

“Not like mine.”

“Your stories are scary.”

“Your stories are boring. The one about the turtle who learned to share made me want to remove my own ears.”

“Freya,” Lyle said.

“What? It was pedagogical torture. The turtle had no interiority.”

Iris considered this.

“What’s interiority?”

“The part where you know what someone wants but they can’t say it.”

“Like when Dad wants to go to bed but stays up because he’s sad?”

Lyle dropped the plate.

It did not break.

He picked it up and resumed washing.

“Exactly like that,” Freya said.

Cora’s hand reached across the table and adjusted Iris’s scissors so the blades aligned properly.

“What about the key?”

“What key?” Iris asked.

“The door key. The one your father found. The one that opens—”

“Freya,” Lyle said, sharper.

“The closet where we keep the good cereal,” Freya finished.

Iris was not fooled, but she was distracted.

“We don’t have a cereal closet.”

“We should. Secret cereal is an excellent tradition.”

Iris returned to her paper. She had drawn a rough outline of their apartment — linoleum suggested by grey scribble, the door a tall rectangle with a copper crayon line — but added rooms that did not exist: a library, a greenhouse, and a room labeled DO NOT ENTER beneath a skull and crossbones.

“What’s this one?” Freya asked, pointing to the greenhouse.

“That’s where Cora grows her tea.”

Cora’s expression surfaced briefly through Freya’s.

“I don’t grow tea.”

“In the picture you do.”

“Why?”

“Because you like it. And you’re always cold. The greenhouse is warm.”

Cora looked at the drawing through Freya’s eyes.

Iris had drawn a radiator shaped like a dragon.

“That’s very accurate,” Cora said quietly.

“I know. I’m good at accurate.”

Lyle dried his hands and sat beside Iris.

“What if we do the tooth fairy?”

“That’s baby.”

“It is supernatural commerce. A nocturnal entity exchanges currency for biological material. That is disturbing.”

“Dad.”

“What about the hollow-core door? We could call it an art project.”

“We could call it a door.”

“It is a door.”

“Then it’s not a tradition. It’s furniture.”

Freya leaned back and stretched Cora’s arms above her head, an expansive gesture Cora herself rarely made.

“What about the tapping?”

Iris looked up.

“You tap when you’re thinking,” Freya said. “Two fast, one slow, two fast. I do it. You learned it. Your father pretends he doesn’t notice, but he counts.”

Lyle said nothing.

“It’s a family code,” Freya continued. “Nobody else knows what it means.”

“What does it mean?”

Freya smiled.

“Nothing. That’s the point. It means we’re paying attention to each other.”

Iris wrote in large careful letters:

MY FAMILY TAPS

Beneath the words, she drew three figures:

A tall man with too many buttons.

A small person holding a rabbit.

A medium person with a teacup.

The medium person had Freya’s posture, though Iris was drawing Cora’s body.

“Is that me?” Cora asked.

“It’s all of you,” Iris said. “When you’re here.”

Lyle looked at the drawing.

Something gathered beneath his ribs. Not happiness exactly. The anticipation of happiness, perhaps. The upward climb before the drop.

“I need glue,” Iris said.

“We don’t have glue.”

“Then how do I attach the glitter?”

“What glitter?”

Iris pointed to a bag of silver glitter on the counter.

Lyle did not remember buying it.

Cora did not remember bringing it.

Freya adjusted the cardigan and said nothing.

“You bought glitter,” Iris accused.

“I bought supplies,” Lyle said.

“You bought glitter because Freya likes shiny things.”

“Freya likes many things.”

“She likes glitter and your shirts and making me laugh when I’m supposed to be sleeping.”

Cora’s face went carefully neutral.

Freya was hiding, or trying to.

Lyle stood.

“I’ll get glue. The hardware store is open until nine.”

“It’s eight-forty,” Cora said.

“Then I’ll run.”

He put on his coat.

At the door, he stopped.

“Field-trip permission slip. Museum. Due tomorrow.”

“You forgot?” Iris asked.

“I remembered at eight-forty-seven instead of two in the morning. This is growth.”

He ran to the hardware store.

He bought glue that dried clear.

He bought a small cactus because Iris had mentioned wanting a plant six weeks earlier, on a Tuesday, while eating pancakes.

He got exact change for the museum fee.

He completed the permission slip on the bonnet of his car using the store’s pen.

When he returned, Freya and Iris were covered in glitter.

The teacup had been knocked over. The borrowed cardigan carried a silver streak along one sleeve.

“Bird poop,” Iris announced, holding up her project.

The three figures were encrusted in silver.

“It’s beautiful,” Lyle said.

“It’s accurate. We’re shiny.”

He produced the permission slip, the exact change, and the cactus.

Freya looked at them.

Cora surfaced in the surprise.

“You remembered,” she said.

“I’m trying to.”

The kitchen timer rang.

Freya’s posture shifted.

“My hour.”

Iris did not look up.

“Stay for dinner.”

“I can’t.”

“You stayed two minutes last time.”

“And Cora wrote it on the contract.”

“She writes everything.”

“That is why civilization continues.”

The prepared water sat in its basin near the hollow-core door. Tea-dark already at the edges where the key had waited. Wet-penny under honey and glue.

Freya preferred leaving beside it. The water gave the departure somewhere to pull, reducing the violent backward snap through the host. It was a courtesy, not a requirement — plumbing ethics.

Freya stood beside the basin. Cardigan cuff silver. Timer’s plastic tick the loudest honest thing in the room.

“The cactus needs sun,” she said. “The window without the draft.”

Then she withdrew.

Cora returned seated at the kitchen table, the glue stick still in her hand.

She blinked at Iris.

At Lyle.

At the three silver figures.

“What did I miss?”

“You helped with the project,” Iris said.

“Did I do a good job?”

“You did a Cora job. That’s the best kind.”

Cora smiled.

“I should go. I have class in the morning.”

“Stay for dinner,” Lyle said.

“You already paid me for the hour.”

“Stay anyway.”

Cora looked at Iris, who was placing the cactus in the correct window.

“I’ll stay. But I’m washing the glitter off first.”

They ate pasta.

Cora told a story about a restaurant customer who tried to pay with a coupon from another restaurant.

Iris laughed hard enough to snort milk.

Lyle did not check his phone once.

After dinner, Cora helped Iris pack her backpack. She found the permission slip taped visibly to the front and the museum fee sealed in a labeled envelope.

“You really did this,” she said.

“I told you. Growth.”

After Cora left and Iris fell asleep, Lyle sat alone with the project propped on the coffee table.

Three figures.

One drawing.

All of them silver.

He touched the medium figure — teacup person, Freya’s posture in Cora’s outline.

Glitter came away on his finger. Silver on the nick. He did not wipe it off right away.

9

THE NEGOTIATION

It happened on a Thursday.

The apartment without Iris had a different pressure — not empty, held, like a room waiting for a form to be filled. Cheap blue through the blinds. Fridge thinking. Door against the wall like a third party that had not been invited to the negotiation.

Cora arrived at seven wearing rust-coloured trousers the shade of dried blood if you were unkind and of autumn leaves if you were not, and a blouse she hated, purchased for a cousin’s wedding and resented ever since. The fabric whispered when she moved. Restaurant soap under something sharper she had put on for courage.

“You look nice,” Lyle said.

“I look like a garnish.”

“You look like someone who owns a blouse.”

“I don’t want to own a blouse. I want to own rent money.”

Iris was spending the night with Mrs. Alvarez. The rabbit was gone with her. The fort cushions still held the shape of a child on the couch.

Cora reviewed the session terms on the kitchen table. Paper under the bulb. Pen uncapped. Hands flat once, then not.

One hour.

No phone use.

No private memories.

No sexual contact without an additional agreement made while Cora was fully herself.

Freya entered at seven-fifteen.

Cora’s face settled into Freya’s focus.

Freya looked down at the blouse.

“This is tragic.”

“You chose it,” Cora said through the shared mouth.

“I did not choose it. I merely have to inhabit the consequences.”

They had become better at sharing speech. Not effortlessly. Cora could surface in short bursts if Freya allowed room.

The fact that they argued more often comforted Lyle and unsettled him in equal measure.

Cora was no longer afraid to push.

Freya was no longer careful enough to avoid needing the push.

Freya crossed the room.

“We need to talk.”

“About what?”

“About the fact that you look at me like you want to kiss me. I look at you like I want to be kissed. Cora is inside this with us, feeling everything, while we pretend not to breathe at each other.”

Lyle sat heavily on the couch.

“I don’t—”

“You do. I do. Innocence is becoming humiliating.”

Freya sat beside him. Distance measured in couch cushions — close enough for breath, far enough for a clause.

The blouse whispered against the upholstery. Cheap fabric doing expensive work.

“Cora,” Freya said aloud. “Are you listening?”

Cora’s expression surfaced unevenly through Freya’s focus.

“I’m always listening. That’s the contract.”

“I want to kiss him.”

A pause.

“Do I have permission?”

Cora breathed through the shared body before answering.

“One kiss. I stay present. You do not push me down.”

“Agreed.”

“And it ends when I say it ends.”

“Yes.”

Freya turned to Lyle.

Cora’s face was flushed.

Whose reaction caused it, Lyle could not tell.

Freya touched his jaw with Cora’s fingers.

The touch was light.

Exploratory.

“This is strange,” he said.

“Everything about us is strange. This is simply the first part we’re admitting is romantic.”

She leaned toward him.

Lyle met her halfway.

Their lips touched.

Cora’s mouth tasted of honey tea — the same amber as the dinosaur-project cup, history in a kiss. Warmth. A pulse under skin that was not only one woman’s.

Freya kissed with an insistent curiosity, slightly desperate and slightly amused by its own desperation. Lyle’s hand found the back of the couch, not her, and stayed white-knuckled on the fabric.

Lyle pulled away first.

“Are you okay?”

He did not know whom he was asking.

Cora surfaced.

Her eyes focused entirely as her own.

“That felt like being present at someone else’s first date.”

Freya laughed from somewhere within the same expression.

A startled, almost young sound.

“I’m sorry,” Lyle said.

“Don’t be sorry. Be aware.”

Cora touched her chest.

“I felt my lips. My breath. My heart speed up. I don’t know whether that was her or me or both. I don’t like not knowing.”

Freya’s focus returned.

She looked at Lyle.

“Again.”

“No,” Cora said immediately.

Freya’s mouth tightened.

“One kiss,” Cora continued. “You honored it. Now I’m ending early because I need to sit in my body without the two of you breathing through it.”

Freya closed her eyes.

She withdrew without argument.

Cora returned sitting beside Lyle.

Her face was pale but composed.

“I’ll come back,” she said. “But we need new terms.”

She stood.

“That was not nothing.”

After she left, Lyle poured a glass of tap water. No key near it.

He drank it in the kitchen under the single bulb and tried to remember what his mouth had tasted like before honey and contract and rust-coloured trousers in the doorway. The dish towel hung dry on the oven. The door did not comment.

10

THREE JOBS

The business learned its own other people’s front rooms, salt on carpet that would never fully vacuum out, the case handle warm from Lyle’s hand.

Job One: The Cleansing

Mrs. Patterson wanted her brownstone cleansed before listing it for sale.

She was seventy, widowed, and convinced her husband’s ghost was preventing buyers from making offers.

“He sits in the study,” she told Lyle. “He sighs. He was a great sigher.”

Freya used David, a retired actor who missed audiences.

David was tall, silver-haired, and possessed the kind of bone structure that made supernatural occupation appear aristocratic.

They staged the cleansing in the study. Brownstone light through heavy drapes the colour of old wine. Dust motes turning in the shaft. The husband’s chair still held the hollow of a man who had sighed for decades — leather cracked at the arms where thumbs had worried.

Lyle sprinkled salt. It caught in the carpet’s long pile and would outlast the listing photos.

Freya sighed extravagantly through David. David’s lungs made melancholy sound expensive.

Mrs. Patterson frowned.

“That isn’t my husband’s sigh.”

“Your husband’s sigh was depressed,” Freya said. “I’m providing the deluxe version. Melancholy with a trace of regret.”

Mrs. Patterson laughed.

She paid them four hundred pounds and a jar of preserved peaches. The glass was sticky. The peaches floated in syrup the colour of late sun through kitchen curtains. Lyle carried the jar home like evidence of a kinder job than the one they had done.

Job Two: The Punishment

Mr. Kessler hired them because he believed his wife was possessed.

“She’s become disobedient,” he said.

His wife, Anna, sat beside him with a fading bruise around one wrist — yellowing at the edges, the colour of a lie that had almost healed. The living room smelled of lemon polish and something scorched under it. Chairs too formal for the hour.

Freya was in Cora under strict terms.

Thirty minutes.

No physical contact with the client.

Immediate withdrawal if Cora felt unsafe.

Mr. Kessler described Anna’s symptoms.

She had stopped cooking properly.

She asked where he went at night.

She no longer wanted to attend his office parties.

Freya listened.

Cora listened.

Then Freya turned Cora’s body toward Anna.

“What is your name?”

“Anna.”

“Do you want to be here?”

Mr. Kessler stood.

“That isn’t part of the evaluation.”

Freya kept her eyes on Anna.

“Do you want to be here?”

Anna looked at her husband.

Then at Cora’s body.

“No.”

Cora’s smallest finger curled once.

Permission.

Freya turned toward Mr. Kessler.

“The infestation is not inside your wife.”

He frowned.

Freya pointed at his watch.

“It is standing three feet to her left and overdressed for the occasion.”

Mr. Kessler’s face darkened.

Lyle moved between him and Anna.

Freya continued before he could speak.

“The attached presence appears territorial, controlling, and resistant to female autonomy. Removal requires isolation from its preferred target.”

Mr. Kessler stared.

“What does that mean?”

“It means you come into the hallway with Lyle while I prepare your wife.”

Lyle caught on.

Barely.

He ushered Kessler outside.

“Masculine entities often resist public extraction,” Lyle said.

Inside, Freya placed the client’s fee into Anna’s hands. Cash warm from Kessler’s wallet, colder in purpose.

“Fourth Street shelter,” she told her. “Back entrance. Use a different cab company from the one he knows.”

Cora’s finger tapped agreement against her thigh — two fast, one slow, two fast, too quiet for the hall to hear.

Anna left through the kitchen. Screen door. Evening air. The lemon polish stayed behind with the man.

By the time Kessler realised there had been no extraction, she was gone.

Cora withdrew early.

When she returned, her hands shook.

“We chose to intervene,” she said. “She asked. I said yes.”

Lyle nodded.

“But I don’t want to do that again. I don’t want to feel that afraid in my own skin.”

Lyle paid her double.

She accepted the money because possession was work.

Her hands were still shaking when she left. Lyle watched them from the porch light’s cheap yellow cone until the dark took her. Possession was work. Fear was also work. The double pay sat folded in her pocket like a receipt that could not cover everything.

Job Three: The Rejection

Marcus had hosted Freya twice before.

Both sessions had gone smoothly.

The third did not.

Repeated possession had consequences.

His body recognized her now.

Recognition did not produce familiarity.

It produced defence.

The moment Freya entered, Marcus’s spine arched. The client’s spare bedroom had floral wallpaper and a window unit that dripped into a stained pan — domestic weather, wrong for what was happening.

His throat constricted.

His hands clawed at his chest. Nails short. Panic exact.

Freya tried to settle.

The body pushed harder.

“Lyle,” she gasped through Marcus’s mouth. “He’s rejecting.”

Marcus screamed.

Lyle seized the basin and threw the prepared water across the floor. Tea-dark slap on laminate. Wet-penny and vomit rising together. The key rang against the tub rim.

The pathway broke.

Freya was expelled so violently Marcus vomited into the plastic tub. The body knew its history better than the contract.

The client stared in horror.

“The entity was too powerful,” Lyle said, breathless. “We had to abort for the host’s safety.”

He refunded the client.

Then he sat with Marcus in the bathroom until the shaking stopped.

Marcus looked at him with eyes entirely his own.

“I could feel her trying to fit.”

“I know.”

“I kept pushing.”

“That was your body protecting you.”

“I didn’t mean to.”

“You didn’t have to mean to.”

Marcus swallowed.

“I just didn’t want her there.”

Lyle nodded.

“That’s your right.”

He paid Marcus triple.

Afterward, Lyle sat among the fake instruments in the boot of the car. Car-park sodium light the colour of weak tea.

Salt in a bag that had held groceries once.

Food-coloured water in bottles that still wore sports-drink labels under the marker.

Copper wire that remembered the annex whisk.

The business was becoming successful.

Repeat clients.

Referrals.

Enough money to pay bills before the red notices arrived.

He could not stop seeing Marcus’s face.

A body could agree twice and refuse the third time without changing its mind.

The body had its own history.

He called Cora.

“I want better contracts.”

Cora was quiet.

Then:

“That’s a very good idea, Lyle.”

11

TAKINGS

The problem with the business was that it kept working.

Not the money. The money was fine and then good and then, briefly, in July, alarming — eleven hundred in a fortnight, which Lyle handled by buying a coat he did not need (navy, too formal, hung in the closet smelling of shop plastic for weeks) and then feeling strange about the coat.

The problem was the effects. Rooms that remembered the fourth line. Hands that itched for the laminated card the way they used to itch for the key.

They had a system by then. Freya went in the day before and did the groundwork — the cold spots, the smell, one drawer that would not stay shut. Lyle arrived on the day with the case. The case contained a censer he had bought at a garden centre, four bottles of water, a stole from a theatrical costumier, and a laminated card in a language he had assembled from a Latin hymn and the ingredients list on a bottle of Polish vodka.

The card was the joke. The card had always been the joke. He read it out in a voice Freya described as a man doing the shipping forecast during a hostage situation, and she made the body do something appalling, and he said the last line and slapped his hand on the forehead, and she came out.

That was the trick. Say the words, hit the mark, she leaves.

In July the words started arriving early.

The first time, he assumed he had misjudged the timing.

They were in a semi in Ferndale with a woman named Pat and Pat’s two sisters, and the host was a nephew who had agreed to it for a hundred and twenty, and Lyle got to the fourth line of the card — not the last, the fourth — and the nephew went down like a dropped coat.

Two beats early.

Lyle covered it. He did the rest of the card over the body, which looked committed, and Pat cried, and one of the sisters said “he’s got the gift” to the other one, and they paid in cash and gave him a Tupperware of something.

In the car — Martin’s car, because the new man on the roster drove, which was most of why Lyle had taken him — Freya said, “That wasn’t me.”

“You went out early.”

“I went out because I was put out.”

“Don’t.”

“I’m telling you what happened in the room I was in.”

“You lost your grip. It happens. He’d been drinking, you said he’d been—”

“Lyle.” She turned Martin’s head. “I have been leaving bodies for twenty-six years. I know the difference between falling out of a window and being shown the door.”

He put the radio on.

The second time was three weeks later and he could not put the radio on, because the second time it was a stranger.

A man had come to a job. Not a host. Not part of it. A neighbour who had wandered in through an open door to see what the noise was, and had stood at the back of somebody’s front room while Lyle read the fourth line of the laminated card.

The neighbour’s knees went.

He was fine. He was up in ten seconds, embarrassed, apologetic, blaming his blood pressure, and everybody in the room was so completely inside the story by then that they took it as a bonus.

Lyle went out to the car and sat in it.

Freya, when she got there, did not make a joke, which was how he knew she had been thinking about it too.

“He wasn’t hosting,” Lyle said.

“No.”

“There was nothing in him.”

“No.”

“So what went out of him.”

Freya was quiet.

“Nothing went out of him,” she said. “Something arrived at him. That’s not the same shape.”

He tested it once, on his own, which he did not tell her about for four months.

He sat in the kitchen at eleven at night with the laminated card and no host and no water and no key anywhere near him — door behind him beige and copper-dark, single bulb, ordinary silence of a flat that had learned too many kinds of quiet — and he read the whole thing through in his ordinary voice, at his ordinary volume, to an empty room.

Nothing happened.

He read it again with the voice on.

The extractor fan over the hob, which had not worked since March, turned over twice and stopped. A mechanical throat clearing. Lyle’s hands flat on the table so they would not reach for anything.

Lyle sat looking at it for a long time. Then he got up, put the card in the drawer under the tea towels, and went to bed with the light on, and in the morning he told himself the wiring in this flat had always been a joke, which was true, and had nothing to do with anything, which was not.

“How does it work?” he asked her, in August.

“How does what work?”

“Any of it. Yours. Why does the water do it? Why the key? Why is there a procedure at all — you’re a dead woman, why do you need a shape to come through?”

Freya, in Martin, was doing the crossword badly on purpose. Martin’s silver hair in the afternoon light through the windshield; ink on a callused actor’s finger.

“Because that’s what a door is,” she said. “You don’t get in because you want in. You get in because someone made a place where getting in is possible, and then it stays made.”

“Made by who?”

“Somebody who needed it. Usually somebody who needed it very badly.” She filled in a wrong answer with satisfaction. “That’s most of the supernatural, in my experience. Somebody wanted something so much that they wore a groove in the world, and then everybody afterwards walks in the groove and calls it a law.”

Lyle thought about that.

“We’ve done sixty-one jobs,” he said.

“Sixty-three.”

“Sixty-three rooms where between four and eleven people watched me say a set of words and something happened.”

Freya’s pen stopped.

“Yes,” she said.

“That’s — Freya, that’s about four hundred people.”

“Four hundred and six, if we count the ones at Donna’s twice, and we should, because they’ve told everyone.”

He waited for her to make it small.

She did not make it small. She put the pen down and looked at the crossword and did not make it small, and after a while she said:

“You should stop asking me questions you already have an opinion about. You’re better at this than you think and it makes you frightening in a way I like and don’t.”

He did not stop.

He got worse, if anything, over the rest of that summer — added a second card, changed the stole, worked out that the fourth line landed harder if he took a breath before it. He would say afterward, to himself, in the specific hours, that he had been refining the act, and that a man refining an act is not a man building an instrument.

There is a version of him that put the case in a skip that August.

That version does not have the money. Iris does not get the coat, and the school trip, and the four hundred pounds in an envelope when Margot’s boiler went, and the year of her father being a man who could produce four hundred pounds.

Lyle knew that. He had it ready, in the front of the drawer, for anyone who ever asked.

Nobody asked.

12

THE TOOTH

Iris’s tooth had been loose for a week.

She moved it with her tongue during breakfast (milk and cereal dust on the table), cartoons (blue flicker on her cheek), and the elaborate nightly fiction in which she claimed she had already brushed her teeth. The loose tooth made a tiny wet click when she smiled too hard.

Lyle offered to pull it.

“No.”

“Why?”

“You’ll do it wrong.”

“How does someone pull a tooth wrong?”

“You’ll use pliers.”

“They’re clean.”

“They’re pliers.”

Freya was in Cora that evening.

Cora had arrived in her waitress uniform — black, coffee and restaurant soap, a grease ghost near the hem — and spent the first half of the session working on accounting homework while Freya complained about compound interest. Pens in the hair. Timer on the table. Basin near the door, dark at the rim.

Lyle sat at the far end of the kitchen table assembling invoices. Paper, pen dig, the business learning its own paperwork weather. His hands smelled faintly of salt from the last job.

Iris emerged from a couch-cushion fort and opened her mouth.

Freya looked up.

“Yes, monster?”

Iris pointed at the tooth.

Freya leaned closer.

“It is hanging by a thread.”

“Don’t say thread. Thread is scary.”

“What terminology has been approved?”

“Ready to graduate.”

Freya examined it again.

“Your tooth appears academically prepared.”

Iris nodded.

“Do you want me to do it?” Freya asked.

“Yes.”

“Why me?”

“Because you won’t pretend it won’t hurt.”

Freya’s expression softened.

“It will hurt briefly.”

“How briefly?”

“Shorter than being afraid.”

Iris thought about that.

“Don’t count.”

“I wasn’t planning to.”

“And no thread.”

“I possess no thread.”

Freya washed Cora’s hands and dried them with a paper towel, Cora’s preference — hygiene as contract. Kitchen light on wet knuckles.

Then she crouched beside Iris. Eye level. No pliers in the room, though Iris checked Lyle’s face for them anyway.

“May I touch your face?”

“Yes.”

Freya steadied Iris’s jaw.

“Ready?”

“No.”

Freya waited.

Iris took one breath.

Then another.

“Do it.”

Freya pulled.

The tooth came free with a small wet sound — plumbing of the body, brief and honest.

Blood spread along Iris’s gum, bright under the bulb.

Her eyes watered immediately, less from pain than from the body’s offense at having opened.

Freya placed the tooth in Iris’s palm.

Tiny.

White.

Blood shining at the root.

“That was inside me.”

“Most of you is inside you.”

“That’s disgusting.”

“Correct.”

Lyle brought a folded paper towel.

Iris pressed it to her gum.

Freya remained crouched.

“You moved,” Freya said. “But you didn’t run.”

“I almost did.”

“Almost is not an action.”

Iris studied the tooth.

“Will you put it under my pillow?”

“Is that the custom?”

“The fairy brings money.”

“How much?”

“One pound. Two if the pull was hard.”

“The pull was easy. You were difficult.”

“That should count.”

Freya carried the tooth to Iris’s room and placed it beneath the pillow.

Then she checked Cora’s pocket.

Her hand stopped at the opening.

Cora’s smallest finger tapped once.

Freya waited.

It tapped again.

“Permission granted,” Freya said.

She removed a pound coin and placed it beside the tooth.

“The note,” Iris said.

“What note?”

“The fairy writes a note.”

“Why?”

“To prove she came.”

“That is a troubling evidentiary standard.”

“Curlicue handwriting.”

“I cannot promise curlicues.”

“Fancy printing.”

Freya wrote on the back of a grocery receipt — milk, eggs, the glitter long since paid for:

Dear Iris,

Your tooth has been received and added to the great tower. Your contribution has been noted. Future structural defects will be monitored.

THE MANAGEMENT

Iris smiled through the blood.

“Will you be here when I wake up?”

Freya checked the timer.

“No.”

“Why?”

“Because tomorrow belongs to Cora.”

Iris nodded.

The session ended twelve minutes later.

Cora returned sitting beside the couch, the pen still balanced between her fingers.

Iris was asleep with the paper towel against her mouth.

Cora read the note.

Then she looked beneath the pillow.

One pound.

She added four from her own tip money and wrote beneath Freya’s message:

P.S. Bonus for bravery. No tower administration was consulted.

CORA

Lyle saw her do it.

“That is going to destroy the tooth-fairy economy.”

“She earned hazard pay.”

In the morning, Iris counted the bills at the kitchen table.

“Five pounds,” Lyle said. “For one tooth?”

“It was two fairies.”

“That sounds expensive.”

“They split it.”

PART III

EXCEPTIONS

13

CORA’S AFTERNOON

It was Saturday.

Freya was not scheduled. The basin stayed dry under the sink. The key slept in the drawer under the tea towels — with the school letter and the other things Lyle meant to deal with — and the apartment’s light was only the ordinary cheap blue of a weekend morning, not the charged bulb of session hours. After the tooth, Iris’s smile still had a careful gap; nobody mentioned the two fairies.

Cora came over anyway with bagels and a library book about dinosaurs because Iris had requested “something with teeth.” The bagels left a greasy print through the paper bag onto the kitchen table. Cora wiped it with her sleeve without comment. No pens in her hair for work. Jacket over the chair. Shoes would come off later, and that would mean staying.

Lyle was in his bedroom repairing a lamp he had already repaired twice. The socket smelled faintly of burned dust every time he tried it.

From the kitchen, he heard Cora asking deliberately incorrect questions.

“So the tyrannosaurus ate salad?”

“No!” Iris’s voice went high with delight. “That’s ridiculous.”

“Perhaps he was watching his weight.”

“He had teeth like knives.”

Cora, deadpan: “For chopping lettuce.”

Iris laughed.

Lyle stopped working. The screwdriver stayed in his hand a second longer than it needed to.

He sat on the edge of the bed and listened to his daughter laugh with someone who was not him and not Freya.

Just a person who had chosen to arrive.

After lunch, Cora helped Iris build a fort from every blanket and cushion in the apartment. A chair leg stuck out at an indecent angle. Iris declared it the triceratops horn and would not be corrected.

Cora crawled inside with the dinosaur book.

Lyle stood outside the sheet wall. His shadow made a slow lump on the fabric.

“You can come in,” Cora said, “but you have to be a triceratops.”

“I don’t know how.”

“Then you can be a rock.”

“I can do rock.”

He crawled inside and sat in the corner while Iris and Cora debated whether rocks had feelings.

It was hot in there almost at once. The blankets held the whole smell of the apartment — laundry powder, radiator dust, the bagels — and the light coming through the sheet was a weak orange that made both of them look slightly ill and slightly holy. Cora read lying on her stomach with her chin on her fists. Somewhere around the ankylosaurus she took her shoes off, and nobody remarked on it, and Lyle understood that she had decided she was staying a while.

At four, Cora collected her coat. One sleeve was inside out; she fixed it at the door.

“Thank you for the bagels,” Lyle said.

“Thank you for not attempting the triceratops.” She smiled with half her mouth. “You would have embarrassed the species.”

After she left, Iris poked her head from the fort. A curl of hair stuck to her cheek with fort-heat.

“Dad?”

“Yes?”

“Can Cora be my aunt?”

Lyle looked toward the closed apartment door. The deadbolt still showed the small scrape Cora’s keyring had made on the way out.

“She can be whatever she wants to be.”

“Good. I already told her.”

Later, at the sink, Lyle worked out that the afternoon had run to five hours and eleven minutes. The plate in his hand had a dried mustard edge he kept missing with the sponge. Hot water. Ordinary path. No tea-dark, no hum.

None of it had happened anywhere Freya could reach. The wet-penny smell was only the coins in his pocket. The copper on the door across the room was just metal in weekend light.

He stood there with the plate and could not decide whether that was a betrayal or the first ordinary day his daughter had had in a year. The gap in Iris’s smile when she laughed at Cora had not looked careful at all.

14

THE MUG

Patricia arrived on a Wednesday, three days after Cora’s shoes-off Saturday. The yellow mug was already in the dish rack. Iris was asleep. Session light was back — single bulb, bills out.

Patricia understood the process.

She had signed the terms.

She had permitted ordinary movement, conversation, and nonsexual contact.

Freya entered at seven.

Lyle was paying bills at the kitchen table. The pen had already chewed a dark spot through the electric bill where he’d paused too long on the total.

Freya’s gaze settled on the dish rack before it settled on him.

A yellow mug sat upside down beside the sink.

WORLD’S MOST OKAY WAITRESS

The rim was chipped.

Freya picked it up. A dark ring of dishwater remained on the laminate where it had sat.

“Where did this come from?”

“Cora,” Lyle said, without looking up yet.

“She brought one mug?”

“She brought several.” He capped the pen. “She said ours were depressing.”

Freya opened the cabinet.

A blue mug with a cat.

A green one reading COFFEE FIRST.

A red one repaired along a crack with glue.

The shelf had stopped looking temporary.

“How often does she come here?”

“Sometimes.”

Freya shut the cabinet hard enough that the cat mug ticked against the green one. “How often is sometimes?”

“Freya.”

“Where does she sit?”

Lyle looked up.

“What?”

“When she comes here.” Patricia’s hand — Freya’s will — stayed on the cabinet edge. “The couch? The table? My chair?”

“There is no your chair.”

Freya turned sharply.

Patricia’s shoulders tightened beneath the movement, a nurse’s body catching an angle it would not have chosen.

“Does Iris miss me when Cora is here?”

“Iris misses you when you’re gone.” Lyle kept his hands flat on the bills. “She also loves Cora. Those things are not opposed.”

“They feel opposed.”

“That’s jealousy.”

“I’m not jealous of Cora.”

“What are you jealous of?”

Freya sat on the couch and pulled Patricia’s knees against her chest.

The posture was too young for Patricia’s body and too tight for her joints. The cushion took her on its soft left side — the side that already knew a habitual weight.

“The fact that she can stay.”

Lyle waited.

“She has a mug,” Freya said.

“She has several.”

“She has a place here that doesn’t require water or a key.” Freya’s voice stayed even while Patricia’s fingers whitened on her own shins. “She can walk in. Sit down. Leave without becoming nothing.”

Lyle sat beside her but did not touch Patricia without permission.

“You have a place here.”

“I have a pathway. A contract. An hour.”

“You have Iris. You have me.”

“For an hour.”

Lyle looked toward the yellow mug still in Freya’s hand, logo facing him like a small stupid verdict.

“You could have more if you stopped treating every visit like it might be the last.”

Freya looked at him through Patricia’s kind, settled face.

“I have to treat it like that because it might be. One day Cora will stop hosting. One day you’ll stop preparing the water. One day Iris will be old enough to decide that what I am is not a person but a habit.”

Freya released Patricia’s knees.

Too late.

The joints had already stiffened.

“Where does Cora sit?” she asked again.

“Wherever she wants,” Lyle said.

Freya withdrew.

Patricia returned aware that the session had ended early.

She extended her legs carefully, one and then the other, as if inventorying damage.

“She held my knees too tightly.”

“I’m sorry.”

“And she was angry in my muscles.”

“She’s going through something,” Lyle said.

Patricia stared at him.

“I do not care what she is going through. My knees hurt.”

Lyle heard himself.

He paid Patricia the full hour plus extra.

She accepted the money but declined future sessions.

After she left, he washed Cora’s yellow mug and put it back in the cabinet. Hot water. The chip on the rim caught the sponge. He dried it harder than it needed. On the shelf it sat among the others like a vote Freya could read and he could not take back.

15

THE ACTOR

Martin arrived at eight. The apartment still had Patricia’s refusal in it if you knew where to look — the full-hour pay envelope gone, the yellow mug back on the shelf, Freya’s last question about chairs unanswered. Recognition was the weather now, not jealousy’s sharp inventory.

He was seventy-one, silver-haired, and still carried the posture of a man who had once played kings before learning to tolerate smaller roles. His tweed jacket smelled faintly of dry cleaning and cough drops.

He had hosted Freya during Mrs. Patterson’s cleansing and found the experience strange but manageable.

His agreement allowed:

conversation,

ordinary movement,

hand-holding,

and nonsexual gestures of greeting if separately approved during the session.

Lyle opened the door and felt no attraction.

Only the courteous neutrality he might have felt toward a distant uncle at a funeral.

Martin entered. He wiped his shoes once on the mat out of habit that belonged to a lifetime of other rooms.

Then the smile changed — not broader; differently aimed.

“Well,” Freya said through him. “This is different.”

“What is?” Lyle asked.

“Your face.”

Freya moved Martin’s body into the apartment.

There was uncertainty in the shoulders, as if the jacket were a costume still finding its marks.

“You’re not looking at me the way you look at Cora.”

“I’m looking at you.”

Freya tilted Martin’s chin a degree. “You’re looking at Martin.”

“I’m looking at you in Martin.”

Freya sat.

She crossed Martin’s legs and adjusted the tweed jacket. The fabric whispered. One knee cracked, Martin’s joint, not hers.

“Cora is young. David is elegant. Patricia was sturdy. Martin is…”

“Seventy-one,” Lyle said.

“Yes.”

“And?”

Martin’s hands adjusted the jacket buttons.

Again.

Then again.

“You don’t want to touch me,” Freya said.

It was not accusation.

It was examination. Martin’s eyes held still while she said it, which Martin’s eyes rarely did.

Lyle sat opposite her. The chair between them was the one with the loose caster; it turned a half-inch under his weight and stopped.

“You tapped your foot when you sat down.”

Freya stopped.

“Two fast, one slow, two fast,” he continued. “Martin drums his fingers. I watched him at Mrs. Patterson’s.”

Freya stared. Martin’s throat worked once around a swallow she hadn’t ordered.

“You checked the jacket pockets because you like how deep they are. You found a cough drop. It was cherry. You wanted lemon.”

Martin’s mouth opened slightly. A red foil edge showed between two fingers and disappeared again into the pocket.

“You’re here,” Lyle said. “I know you’re here. I do not need to want the body to know the person.”

Freya stood.

Martin’s gait was stiffer than hers, forcing urgency into careful steps. The loose board by the rug clicked under him.

“Why do you keep recognizing me?”

“Because you keep coming back.”

“It would be safer if you forgot.” She said it looking past his shoulder, then forced Martin’s eyes back. “You could treat me like a dream.”

She stopped in front of him.

Martin’s hand rose toward Lyle’s face but did not touch. The fingers trembled on the last inch and held.

“You look at me like I am continuous.”

“You are.”

“Like I deserve to be remembered.”

“You do.”

Freya made a sound halfway between laughter and grief. It used Martin’s lungs poorly; he had to catch a breath after.

“Kiss me.”

Lyle’s expression changed. He did not step back. He also did not step forward.

“Martin didn’t agree to that.”

“Not my mouth.”

She held up Martin’s hand.

“Kiss it as if you are glad I came.”

Martin’s fingers opened in Freya’s grasp.

A deliberate movement.

Permission.

Lyle took the hand in both of his.

The skin was dry and warm. A thin actor’s callus rode the side of the middle finger where pens and props had lived.

He kissed the knuckles.

“Hello, Freya.”

“Hello, Lyle.”

They sat holding hands like actors in a play neither had rehearsed.

The gesture was not sexual.

It was intimate enough to frighten her. Martin’s pulse jumped once against Lyle’s thumb and then steadied into something that was neither man’s alone.

Freya stayed the full hour.

When she withdrew, Martin returned slowly.

He flexed his fingers.

Looked at Lyle.

“She was crying,” Martin said.

“I know.”

“No.” Martin touched under his own eye, found nothing wet, frowned. “You saw her face.”

Martin touched his chest.

“I felt it in my lungs.”

Lyle walked him to the door.

Martin paused on the landing. The stairwell light made his silver hair look thinner and more honest.

“She seems very sad,” he said. “And very interesting.”

16

WEDNESDAY

The thing at the school was on a Thursday in October and cost four pounds and required a parent to be physically present in a hall for ninety minutes.

Iris had asked about it in May.

Lyle had said probably, and she had said that means no when you say it, and he had said he would tell her on the Wednesday, yes or no, and if it was no he would say why.

That had been in May — coat fight, plates in the sink, no speech.

It was now the tenth of September and he had the letter in the kitchen drawer under the tea towels, where he put things he intended to deal with, which was also where the key lived, wrapped, dry, meaning different things than the letter and stored the same way. A coincidence he did not examine. Morning light through the blinds was ordinary. His hands flat on the table with the biro were the hands that had held Martin’s knuckles and Anna’s cab money and Iris’s tooth-blood towel; today they only had to mean yes.

The problem was that the thing was at two in the afternoon.

Two in the afternoon was a Donna referral in Yardley, three hundred pounds, and the woman had already moved it twice for him.

He worked it out at the kitchen table with a biro and the back of the letter. Ink through soft paper. Two hours in Yardley, forty minutes back if the ring road behaved, which it would not. The school hall at ten past three, forty minutes after it finished, in a car park with nobody in it.

He looked at that for a while.

Then he rang Donna and moved the job to the Friday and lost sixty pounds of it, because the woman in Yardley had a sister visiting on Friday and would only do the evening.

He did not tell Freya he had done that.

He did not tell Iris either.

A thing you have arranged is not the same as a thing you have arranged at somebody.

On the Wednesday he told her at breakfast, which he had thought about and decided was better than after school, when she would be tired and would take it wrong whatever it was.

“The school thing in October.”

Iris looked up with her spoon halfway.

“Yes,” Lyle said. “I’ll be there. Two o’clock, in the hall, and I’ll be there at ten to because you said last time everyone’s dad came in late and it was embarrassing.”

Iris did not say anything.

“That’s a yes,” he said. “That’s not a probably.”

“Okay.”

“You can write it down if you want.”

“I don’t need to write it down.”

She went back to the cereal, and Lyle got up to do the toast, and about ninety seconds later, from behind him, at ordinary volume, Iris said:

“You moved something.”

“What?”

“To do it. You moved something.”

He turned around.

She was not looking at him. She was doing the thing where she pushed the cereal to one side of the bowl and then the other, which she did when she was working out how to say something without it being a big thing.

“A bit,” he said.

“Okay,” said Iris, and that was the end of it, and she talked about a girl called Bea for four minutes and then went to find her shoes.

He told Freya on the Thursday because he could not help himself.

“Four hundred and ten, less the sixty.”

“Three fifty.”

“You didn’t even—”

“Lyle, it’s three fifty.” Martin’s hand turned over. “Why is it less sixty.”

“I moved the Yardley one.”

“Why?”

And he told her, and he heard himself telling her, and understood about four words in that he was telling her so that she would say something.

Freya did not say anything for a moment.

Then: “Ten to two.”

“Ten to.”

“Because of the dads coming in late.”

“She mentioned it in July. Once.”

“In July,” Freya said, and there was something in Martin’s voice that Lyle had heard perhaps three times.

“Don’t.”

“I’m not doing anything.”

“You’re being nice at me. It’s four pounds and a hall.”

“It’s the tenth of September and the thing is in October and you have moved a job you cannot afford to move.” She put the pen down. “I’m allowed to notice. I notice everything, it’s practically the only thing I’ve got.”

Lyle looked at the invoices.

“Say the number again.”

“Three hundred and fifty pounds,” said Freya, “you enormous coward,” and did the rest of them out loud while he wrote, and they got through the whole stack in eleven minutes, and at the end of it she made a joke about Donna’s handwriting that was so unkind and so accurate that he had to put his head on the table.
