That was one of the good evenings. There were a lot of them that autumn.

Halfway through the second page Freya put Martin’s reading glasses on without thinking about it, and got four lines further before she noticed, and stopped.

“That’s his.”

“It’s a pair of glasses.”

“It’s his pair of glasses.” She took them off and folded them and set them back where they had been, square to the edge of the table. “He’d have said yes. That’s not the same as having asked him.”

Then she did the rest of the column without them, badly, at arm’s length, complaining the entire way.

She stayed the full hour and left at the hour.

Lyle washed up.

Then he took the letter out of the drawer — key still wrapped beneath the towels, undisturbed — wrote THURS 9 OCT — 2pm — BE THERE 1:50 on the front of it in capitals, and stuck it to the fridge with a magnet shaped like a slice of watermelon, where it stayed, at Iris’s eye level, for twenty-nine days. Paper as record. Hands as refusal to be only the man who said probably.

17

SMALL DELAYS

The first delay lasted two minutes.

Cora signaled.

Freya finished her sentence before leaving.

Cora recorded the overage on the contract.

SIGNAL GIVEN. EXIT DELAYED TWO MINUTES.

Freya apologized at the next session.

The second delay lasted less than a minute.

Iris asked Freya to finish a bedtime sentence.

Freya looked at Cora’s curling finger.

Then finished:

“And the monster found the door unlocked.”

She withdrew immediately afterward.

Cora recorded it.

SIGNAL GIVEN. EXIT DELAYED THIRTY-SEVEN SECONDS. CHILD PRESENT.

The third was negotiated.

Freya asked for five more minutes.

Cora said yes.

Iris did not understand why one delay was permitted and another was not.

“Because asking changes it,” Cora told her.

Iris nodded.

She began watching the clock.

18

THE ADDRESS

Keith Ruddock had eleven storage units and a way of standing.

Lyle had cleared four of them over three days, in August, in a metal corridor that held the heat like an argument. The agreement had been six hundred. On the third afternoon Keith walked the units with a clipboard he did not write on and found a scuff on a wall that had been there when Lyle arrived and had photographs proving it.

He paid four hundred.

That was not the part.

The part was that Iris was there, because it was Lyle’s week and there had been nowhere to put her, and she had spent three days being extremely good in a plastic chair with a colouring book and a warm juice, and Keith counted the four hundred out into Lyle’s hand slowly, in twenties, and then looked over at her and said:

“Bring your kid to work, do you.”

“She’s fine.”

“I’m not saying she isn’t fine.” He put the clipboard under his arm. “I’m saying there’s men who’d have had this done Tuesday and been home.”

Lyle said nothing.

He said nothing all the way to the car, and while he got the seatbelt, and while Iris asked twice whether they were getting food.

Freya was in Martin that Thursday.

Lyle told the story badly, the way he told all stories where he came off worst — too many hand gestures, too much scene-setting, the joke arriving early and dying.

She listened the whole way through without interrupting, which should have warned him.

“Four hundred,” she said.

“Four.”

“For three days.”

“In August.”

“In front of Iris.”

“That’s not the—” He stopped. “Yes.”

Martin’s fingers went still on the arm of the chair.

“I could visit him,” Freya said.

“No.”

“I haven’t said what I’d do.”

“You don’t have to.”

“I’d like to be clear that some of the things I could do are quite small.”

“No, Freya.”

She let it go so easily that he noticed, and then thought about it for a moment, and then chose not to notice.

Three days later he was on the phone to her — to Martin, to the arrangement, to a voice in a rented throat at nine on a Sunday night — and they were talking about the school run, and the fridge, and whether the Donna referral was worth the drive.

And then Lyle said:

“Twenty-two Denholm Road. The yellow gate.”

There was a pause on the line of about a second and a half.

“Whose is that?” Freya said.

“Nobody’s.”

“Lyle.”

“I don’t know why I said it.”

He did know. He had looked it up on Friday. He had sat in the car outside the school with his phone and found the man’s road in under four minutes and then put the phone face-down on the passenger seat like it had done something.

“All right,” Freya said.

“I’m not asking you to—”

“I know.”

“I said no. On Thursday. I said no.”

“You did,” she agreed. “You were very clear.”

She changed the subject to the fridge.

What happened to Keith Ruddock happened on a Wednesday and was, by any reasonable measure, nothing.

He was not hurt. Nothing was stolen. There was no fire.

At some point between eleven at night and six in the morning, every one of his eleven units was opened, and their contents were moved — carefully, without damage — into different units. Not emptied. Redistributed. A woman’s forty years of photographs into a unit belonging to a bathroom-fittings company. Sixteen boxes of a dead man’s papers into a unit rented by a woman who was in Spain. Somebody’s dismantled bed into three separate places.

Nothing was broken. Everything was in the wrong life.

No lock was forced. Whoever did it had keys. That was in the police report, and it was the part Keith could never get anybody to take seriously.

It took Keith Ruddock nine weeks and cost him two customers, four solicitors’ letters and, eventually, the units.

Lyle heard about it from a man at the hardware shop who thought it was funny.

He laughed.

Then he went out to the car and sat in it for a while with his hands on the wheel.

He never asked her.

That was the whole of it, in the end. Not a decision — a series of small refusals to make one. He did not ask on the Thursday, or the Sunday after, or in October when it came up at a table and she said, “Mm,” and looked at the ceiling, and he let the subject move on the way you let a door swing shut behind you.

He told himself several things over the following months. That she probably hadn’t. That it was a coincidence, that a man like that had enemies, that eleven units is a lot for one person. That he had said no out loud and had been on record about it.

The record was the thing he kept going back to.

He had, in fact, said no.

He had said no, and then he had said twenty-two Denholm Road, and both of those things had come out of the same mouth in the same week, and he had spent the intervening time being a man who had said no.

It was October before she said it.

They were in the car outside Donna’s with the engine off, waiting out a downpour — the same cooling-car weather as after Harold’s daughter, hands without a job — and he was complaining about something — the wipers, the referral, the fact that Donna kept feeding them — and Freya was in a host called Anneke and had been quiet for a while. Rain on the roof. Wet-penny nowhere; only wet glass and the smell of heater dust.

“Do you remember Keith Ruddock,” she said.

Lyle stopped talking.

The rain did the thing rain does on a car roof.

“I said no,” he said.

“You did.”

“I said no, and then—”

“You gave me an address instead of forgiveness.”

He did not answer that.

He had a great many things available to say and every single one of them was a version of that’s not fair, and he sat in a cooling car with his hands in his lap and could not get any of them to a state where he would have been able to hear himself say it.

“You’re not angry,” he said eventually.

“No.”

“You should be angry.”

“Why?”

“Because I used you.” His voice came out strange. “I used you and I kept my hands clean and I’ve been walking round for two months being a man who said no.”

“Yes,” Freya agreed.

She watched the rain.

“Everyone does that with me,” she said. “That’s the thing nobody warns you about being what I am. People don’t ask me for things. They just stand near me and say something true out loud.”

The rain did not stop. Lyle’s hands stayed in his lap. Paper would have been cleaner — a contract, a no on a page. He had given her an address instead.

19

WHAT HE HUGGED BACK

Iris started waking up in September.

Not nightmares, or not exactly. She would surface around two and be entirely awake, and would lie there for a while doing the arithmetic children do at that hour, and then she would come and stand in his doorway and not say anything until he noticed her. Hallway light cut a cheap yellow bar across the carpet. After Keith — after the address that was not forgiveness — the apartment’s night weather had a new edge: work tiredness, four jobs a week, the key closer to sleep than it should have been.

He got good at noticing.

Then he got worse at it, because the nights had turned, and because the Donna referrals had turned into four jobs a week, and because he had started keeping a glass of water on the bedside table with the key beside it so that Freya could come and go without the whole production of the kitchen. Ordinary glass. Ordinary water. The mechanism moved into the bedroom. Intimacy of access, violation waiting in the shape of convenience.

The glass was the thing.

He put a door in his own bedroom and left it unlocked. Curiosity to access, completed without a ceremony.

On the fourteenth he did not wake up.

He would learn the shape of it later, in pieces, from a seven-year-old who reported it the way she reported everything — accurately, in the wrong order, with the important part buried in the middle.

She had come to the doorway. She had stood there for a while. She had said Dad twice, at ordinary volume, and had then done the thing she did not usually do, which was to say it a third time, loudly.

He had not moved.

And then he had.

He had sat up in one motion, and swung his legs off the bed, and come across the room and crouched down in front of her, and said, “Hey. Hey. What’s going on, little bird.”

And Iris — who was seven, and frightened, and who had been standing in a doorway for eleven minutes — had put her arms around his neck.

And he had hugged her back.

She told him about it four days later, in the car, apropos of a discussion about a coat.

“You hugged me back,” she said.

“When?”

“When I came in on the night.”

Lyle looked at her in the mirror.

“Which night?”

“The one where you sat up fast.”

He put the indicator on for a turn he did not take.

“Iris,” he said. “When did I sit up fast?”

“Tuesday. Or the other one.” She was looking out of the window. “It’s fine. She was nice about it. She got me the water and did the thing with the blanket where you tuck the bottom.”

“Iris.”

“I knew it wasn’t you,” Iris said, “because you hugged me back.”

The car in front moved off.

Lyle did not.

Somebody behind him used a horn and then, after a moment, went around.

“Say that again.”

Iris sighed, in the manner of a child asked to repeat the least interesting part.

“You do the one where you put your hand on my head,” she said. “And you say oof. Every time. You’ve always done it.” She pulled at a loose thread on her sleeve. “She did the arms one. Where you go all the way round and squeeze. Nobody does that in our house.”

He waited until Iris was at Margot’s.

He filled the glass. He put the key in. Tea-dark came. Wet-penny in the bedroom where it had never belonged — contamination of the place that was supposed to be only sleep. He sat on the edge of the bed with his hands between his knees and waited for the water to change the way water changed, and when Martin’s voice came out of Martin’s mouth in the front room and said, “Christ, it’s cold in here, do you not have heating,” he found that he had been rehearsing for two days and had nothing.

“You came in without asking.”

“Yes.”

That was all. No argument. No angle.

“You used my body to hug my daughter.”

“Yes.”

“Say something else.”

“What would you like?”

“I’d like you to fight me,” Lyle said. “I’d like you to make it complicated, because if you make it complicated I can lose, and I would very much like to lose this one.”

Freya sat down.

“She said your name three times,” she said. “The third one was loud. She’s never done a third one.” Martin’s hands turned over. “You were on your side with one arm off the bed and your mouth open, and you’d done four jobs and you’d been up at half five, and I want to be clear that I looked at you for a while first.”

“You could have woken me.”

“I tried.”

“You could have tried harder.”

“Yes,” Freya said. “That’s the true one. Well done.”

She let that sit for a moment.

“Your kid needed you,” she said. “You weren’t there, so I was.”

And there it was — the sentence he would carry around for the rest of his life, that was so nearly right that arguing with it made him the villain, and so completely wrong that agreeing with it made him nothing at all.

“You don’t get to do that,” he said.

“I know.”

“You don’t get to be right about it.”

“I’m not right about it,” Freya said. “I’m right about the eleven minutes. That’s a much smaller thing to be right about, and I’d like it back, please, because I’ve been holding it since Tuesday and it’s heavier than I expected.”

Lyle put his face in his hands.

“She noticed in two seconds.”

“Yes.”

“Two seconds, Freya. A stranger could have — ” He stopped. “She had two seconds of thinking her dad had finally got up, and then she did the maths.”

“I know.”

“What do I do with that?”

Freya was quiet for a long time.

“You could hug her the other way sometimes,” she said. “So it isn’t a test.”

“That’s not—”

“I’m aware it isn’t a solution. I’m aware I’ve made a thing she can only survive by being suspicious, and that I did it while being kind, and that she’ll be forty and still checking.” Martin’s voice had gone somewhere unfamiliar. “I’ve been sitting with that. It isn’t going well.”

That night he did the thing with his hand on her head and said oof, and Iris said “you always do that,” and he said “I know,” and went out and stood in the hallway for a while.

He never said why, and Freya never asked, and the glass stayed on the table for another five weeks before he moved it to the kitchen where it had been in the first place. Water retreats to infrastructure. The bedroom light went back to being only shabby, not an invitation.

20

MARGOT NOTICES

Margot picked Iris up on a Tuesday.

After the hug that was not his — after Iris learned the arms-all-the-way-round as a test — pickup weather had changed. Iris’s inspection was no longer only a game. Lyle’s hands found the door chain because they did not know where else to go.

She arrived at four holding organic groceries and wearing the expression of a woman prepared to discover that Lyle had again used a dish towel as a curtain. A celery top stuck out of the bag like a small green accusation.

Iris met her at the door.

“Mom.”

“Baby.”

Margot knelt and opened her arms. Her coat hem puddled on the scuffed threshold.

Iris did not move forward.

She examined Margot’s face.

Her hands.

The angle of her shoulders.

The placement of her feet — left slightly forward, Margot’s habit when she was trying not to look rushed.

“What?” Margot asked, still kneeling.

“How do I know it’s you?”

Margot laughed nervously. One grocery bag leaned against her knee and crackled.

“Because I’m your mother. You know my face.”

“Faces can change.”

“Not my face.”

Iris continued looking. Her own hands stayed in the pockets of her hoodie, fists probably.

Then:

“You’re Mom.”

“Yes.”

“Okay.”

She hugged Margot briefly and carefully, as if the body might still shift under the coat.

Margot stood and looked at Lyle over Iris’s head.

“What was that?”

“What?” Lyle said.

“The inspection.”

“She’s being careful.”

“Why does she need to be careful around me?”

Lyle did not answer. He found something urgent to do with the door chain that did not need doing.

At the park, the ducks already knew the bread bag. Iris stood at the water’s edge with her sneakers darkening at the toes.

“Can ducks be possessed?” she asked.

Margot tore a piece of crust. “Possessed by what?”

“People without bodies.”

“Who told you that?”

“Freya.”

Margot tightened her grip on the bread bag until the plastic squeaked.

“Freya is a story.”

Iris shook her head once, patient. “She’s real. She just doesn’t have her own face.”

Margot watched Iris scatter crumbs with measured precision — not child-flinging, something closer to a ritual she’d been taught not to waste.

“Has Freya ever been inside someone you know?”

“She was Cora.”

“Cora is real?”

“Cora is real.” Iris kept her eyes on the water. “Freya was inside her. Now Cora comes over when Freya isn’t there too.”

Margot knelt. Gravel bit through her tights; she ignored it.

She held Iris gently by the shoulders.

“If someone enters another person’s body, they are not allowed to be there unless that person says yes. Do you understand?”

“Freya asks.”

“Does she always?”

Iris looked at the ducks. One of them had a torn foot and still won the crumb.

“Sometimes she stays too long.”

Margot’s fingers tightened slightly.

“Has she ever been inside Daddy?”

“No.”

“Inside you?”

“No.”

“Inside me?”

Iris looked directly at her mother.

“No.”

Then she hesitated.

Iris had learned that no and not yet were different answers.

Margot saw only the pause.

She took Iris home early. The bread bag went back in the car half full.

At the car, Iris tapped the side of the bread bag while she waited for the door to unlock.

Two fast. One slow. Two fast.

She did it the way children do things with their hands, without hearing herself do it.

Margot stopped with the key halfway to the lock.

“Where did you learn that?”

Iris looked down at her own hand as though it belonged to somebody sitting next to her.

“I don’t know,” she said.

She sounded, for the first time that afternoon, like a child.

That night, Margot opened her notes app and wrote:

Tuesday, 4:12 p.m. Iris asked how she could know I was herself. She described a dead woman entering familiar people and said, “Sometimes she stays too long.” She hesitated when asked whether this woman had entered me. At the car she tapped out a rhythm — two quick, one slow, two quick — and could not tell me where she got it. It is not one of mine. It is not one of his.

Then Margot called Lyle. She stood at the kitchen counter with the phone on speaker and her notes still open.

“We need to talk seriously about what is happening in your apartment and what it is doing to Iris.”

Lyle listened.

Then he minimized.

He called Freya a game.

Cora a babysitter.

Possession an imaginative framework Iris had adopted.

Margot did not call a lawyer.

Not yet.

She opened a new document.

She began keeping a record.

21

THE NIGHT FREYA STAYED

A message appeared while Freya was using her body.

Freya read the preview.

Only the preview.

Only because it appeared in front of her.

Only because the message came from Jared and included Lyle’s name.

The message said:

Is the haunted raccoon paying you tonight or pretending friendship covers labor again?

Freya answered:

The haunted raccoon objects to your characterization.

Cora returned, saw the sent message, and ended the session schedule for one week.

Freya called the punishment disproportionate.

Cora said, “It isn’t punishment. You used my phone.”

Lyle said, “It was funny.”

Cora said, “That is why you are not in charge of consequences.”

The third violation lasted forty minutes.

It began with an animated fox.

Iris had chosen the movie.

She sat cross-legged on the floor in pyjamas, defending every song as though she had personally composed it.

Lyle sat at one end of the couch.

Freya sat at the other in Cora’s body.

Cora had agreed to one hour. No sexual contact. No phone. No food except popcorn. Exit at nine.

The contract lay signed on the coffee table.

Freya made it twenty-three minutes before objecting to the premise.

“Foxes do not sing.”

“They do in this,” Iris said.

“They scream like women being murdered.”

Lyle looked at her.

“Could we avoid that comparison during family entertainment?”

“It is zoologically accurate.”

Iris paused the movie.

“What do they scream about?”

“Territory. Mating. Fear.”

“Like adults.”

Freya looked at Lyle.

“Exactly like adults.”

Iris resumed the movie.

Freya criticized the animation, the songs, the implied economy of the animal village, and the mayor’s refusal to acknowledge obvious infrastructure failures.

Lyle disagreed with her mostly to keep her talking.

At eight-fifty, Cora’s smallest finger curled once.

Freya saw it.

“I have ten minutes.”

Lyle checked the clock.

“Nine.”

“The opening credits do not count.”

“They absolutely count.”

“No narrative occurred.”

“You inhabited her body during them.”

“Very little happened.”

Cora’s finger curled a second time, then straightened.

A reminder.

Freya looked toward Iris.

Iris had fallen asleep against the couch, one hand inside the popcorn bowl.

Lyle lifted her carefully.

He carried her to bed, removed the popcorn from her sleeve, and placed the stuffed rabbit beneath her arm.

When he returned, the movie had ended.

Blue light from the menu screen moved across Cora’s face — not the cheap blue of blinds, the colder blue of a screen that would not stop offering more. The apartment’s ordinary evening (popcorn, rabbit, fox) had become a place where exit could fail.

The timer read nine-oh-two.

Cora’s smallest finger curled once.

Twice.

Three times.

Freya did not leave.

Lyle stopped beside the couch.

“Freya.”

“She’s giving me a moment.”

“No. She gave you the signal.”

Freya stared at the television.

The fox danced silently beneath the menu options.

“She can feel that I’m not finished.”

“That isn’t the agreement.”

“I know the agreement.”

“Then leave.”

Freya looked at him.

Cora’s eyes held a brightness that belonged to neither woman alone.

“I hate it,” Freya said.

“Still.”

“No. Not still. Again.”

Her hand closed around the couch cushion.

“Every time is the first time. There is no transition. No fading. No sleep. One second there is a room, and then there is no room. No sound. No body. No distance between one thought and the next because there is nothing to measure distance with.”

Cora’s right foot tapped against the floor.

Not Freya’s rhythm.

A hard, arrhythmic beat.

Cora surfacing.

“Leave,” Lyle said.

Freya’s expression hardened.

“You say that easily.”

“I’m not saying it easily.”

“You get to remain here.”

“That doesn’t make her body yours.”

Cora’s hand jerked toward the contract.

Freya restrained it.

Only for a second.

Long enough.

Lyle saw.

“Don’t.”

Freya released the wrist.

Cora’s fingers scraped against the paper.

The withdrawal clause crumpled beneath them.

Freya’s voice lowered.

“Tell me to stay,” Freya said.

“No,” Lyle said.

“You want me to.”

“That doesn’t matter.”

“It matters to me.”

“It cannot matter more than her signal.”

Freya stood.

Cora’s legs trembled.

Her body had begun pushing against the possession, muscles tightening and releasing without coordination.

Freya crossed the room.

Lyle stepped backward.

“Tell me,” she said.

He could see how frightened she was.

Not theatrically frightened.

Not sharpened into anger.

Terrified of the nothing waiting beyond the room.

He thought of the first time she had returned.

There you are.

He thought of every goodbye that erased her from the world.

He thought of the apartment after she left.

The unoccupied chair.

The pipes.

The humiliating fact that he sometimes spoke aloud because silence felt like proof she had never existed.

Cora’s smallest finger began curling again.

Once.

Freya’s eyes closed.

Twice.

“Lyle,” Freya said.

The third curl came.

“Stay,” he whispered.

Freya inhaled.

Cora’s body convulsed once.

Then stilled.

It did not still the way a person settles. The foot stopped mid-beat, on the down, heel still raised. The hand went slack from the wrist first and then the fingers, in that order, like something being switched off in sequence by somebody working from a diagram.

For about a second afterward, Cora’s eyes were still Cora’s, and they were looking at him.

Then they weren’t.

Freya had pushed her down.

Wrong.

Freya opened Cora’s eyes.

Relief moved through her face.

“Thank you.”

Lyle felt sick.

He sat at the opposite end of the couch.

Neither of them touched.

Five minutes passed.

Then ten.

The menu music looped.

The same cheerful six notes returned again and again.

Freya did not do anything with the time.

That was what Lyle could not get past afterward. She had held a woman under in order to keep a body, and then she had used the body to sit on a sofa. She put Cora’s bare feet flat on the carpet and moved the toes. She ran Cora’s thumb slowly along the seam of the cushion, back and forth, the way you test fabric you are thinking of buying. She breathed in through Cora’s nose twice, deeply, for no reason he could see, and something in her face eased both times.

Once she reached over and laid Cora’s palm flat against the radiator and left it there past the point where it must have started to hurt, and then took it off, and turned the hand over, and looked at it.

At nine-fifteen, Lyle began crying.

He did not realise it until Freya turned toward him.

She lifted Cora’s hand.

“Don’t,” he said.

The hand stopped.

“Don’t what?”

“Don’t comfort me with her hands.”

Freya lowered it.

Cora’s fingers trembled in her lap.

“I stayed because you asked.”

“That’s worse.”

“You wanted me here.”

“She wanted you gone.”

Freya looked away.

The radiator knocked inside the wall.

Two uneven blows.

Then another.

“I can apologize,” Freya said.

“You cannot apologize while she is trapped behind you.”

“I’m not trapping her.”

Lyle looked at Cora’s motionless smallest finger.

Freya followed his gaze.

Her face collapsed around the lie.

“Then leave.”

Freya did not move.

“Leave,” Lyle repeated.

“You asked me to stay.”

“I was wrong.”

“You don’t get to make me vanish and call that morality.”

“I don’t get to make her vanish either.”

Cora’s breath came shallow and fast.

The body was fighting harder now.

Freya pressed one hand to the sternum.

“I can hold her.”

“She is not something you hold.”

Freya flinched.

The phrase had returned.

The one he had used with Jared.

This time it had nowhere to hide.

At nine-forty, Freya stood.

She staggered toward the bathroom.

Lyle followed without touching her.

At the sink, the prepared water waited in a glass bowl. Mechanism waiting to become ordinary mortality again. The key was not the crime. The ask was.

Freya gripped the counter.

“I’m going to keep wanting this,” she said.

“I know.”

“I’m going to want you to ask.”

“I know.”

“Will you?”

Lyle looked at Cora’s face.

At the sweat along her hairline.

At the body Freya was using to ask whether he loved her.

“No,” he said.

Freya almost smiled.

Not because she believed him.

Because she knew he was trying.

Then she left.

Cora collapsed.

Lyle caught her before her head struck the sink.

Her body was hot.

Her pulse raced beneath his fingers.

She opened her eyes and immediately pushed him away.

“What time is it?”

Lyle looked at the clock.

“Nine-forty.”

Cora stared at him.

“How long?”

“An hour and forty minutes total.”

“No. After I signaled.”

“Forty minutes.”

She touched her throat.

Then her chest.

“She pushed me down.”

Lyle said nothing.

“I was there.”

“I know.”

“I could see pieces. I could hear you.”

“I know.”

“You knew I was trying to come back.”

“Yes.”

Cora sat against the bathroom cabinet.

Her disappointment arrived quietly.

“You asked her to stay.”

“Yes.”

“And she listened to you instead of me.”

“Yes.”

Cora closed her eyes.

Lyle reached toward her.

She opened them.

He stopped.

“Payment,” he said weakly. “I’ll pay double.”

“I don’t want double.”

“You worked longer.”

“I did not work longer. I was held under.”

Cora stood using the sink.

Her knees shook.

Lyle moved aside.

She collected her bag and coat.

“Cora.”

She did not look at him.

“I won’t host her again.”

The statement seemed to change the pressure inside the apartment.

“For how long?” he asked.

Cora turned.

“That question is exactly the problem.”

“I meant—”

“I know what you meant.”

She pulled on her coat.

“I’ll still see Iris.”

Lyle stared.

Cora continued:

“I’ll help with school pickups when Margot agrees. I’ll bring groceries. I’ll come for dinner when I feel like it. I’ll help Jared with his forms. I might even help you make sure clients don’t die in church basements.”

“And Freya?”

“Freya doesn’t get to come with me.”

Lyle sat down on the arm of the couch.

Not because Cora was leaving.

Because she was staying without Freya.

“You’re part of her access,” he said.

Cora’s expression sharpened.

“No.”

Lyle heard the mistake.

Cora stepped closer.

“I am not her access. I am not her continuity. I am not the chair she sits in while you two have a relationship.”

“I didn’t mean it like that.”

“You did. You just didn’t hear it.”

She opened the apartment door.

“Tell Iris I’ll see her Sunday.”

Then she left.

Lyle sat alone in the bathroom.

The prepared water remained in the bowl.

Freya was somewhere beyond perception.

Cora was walking away in her own body.

For the first time, Lyle understood those as two separate losses.

He poured the water down the sink. His hands shook.

The key remained in his hand. Copper dull.

The next morning, he did not prepare more.

By afternoon, he had begun listening to the pipes — for weather that would not come back as Cora.

PART IV

WEARING MARGOT

22

WHY ARE YOU WEARING MOM?

After Cora would not host — after the water poured out and the pipes listened empty — the apartment’s night weather was grief and hunger sharing a room. The knock brought Margot holding a bottle of wine.

“Iris forgot her jacket in the car,” she said. “I texted you. You didn’t answer.”

The label was familiar in a way that hurt his teeth. The kind they used to drink in their first apartment, when arguments still ended in the same room they started in.

“She’s asleep,” Lyle said.

“Then we don’t have to be careful.”

Margot stepped inside before he decided whether to invite her. She moved through the living room touching things with a familiarity that felt both earned and expired—the back of the couch, the edge of the bookshelf, the frame of a photograph of Iris at six, at the beach.

“You kept this,” she said.

“You gave it to me.”

“I know. I didn’t think you’d keep it framed.”

She was wearing a dress he didn’t recognize. Dark blue, cut in a way that opened at the throat. He had never seen it before. Not on her. Not in the back of their closet during the divorce, when they had divided clothes like evidence.

“New dress?” he asked.

“Do you like it?”

She poured the wine without asking. Two glasses. She handed him one and her fingers stayed against his a half-second longer than necessary.

“Sit,” she said.

He sat.

She sat close enough that her knee touched his. She had crossed her legs at the ankle, not the knee—the way she used to when they watched movies, but there was a deliberateness to it now. A performance of old habits.

“I’ve been thinking,” Margot said. “About us. About how I left.”

Lyle felt the old hunger open in his chest, the one he had spent years learning to keep fed on smaller portions.

“You don’t have to—”

“I do. I was cruel. Not in the obvious ways. In the ways that made you feel like you were imagining it. I want you to know I knew. I knew exactly what I was doing.”

The wine was good. It tasted like their bathroom in the old place, the only room with a lock. Red in the glass under the cheap lamp. History as intoxication.

“I forgave you,” he said. It wasn’t entirely true, but it was true enough for the room.

“Did you?”

She leaned closer. Her hand found his thigh. It was a confident hand, surer than Margot’s had ever been with him in the last years of their marriage, when every touch carried apology. This hand claimed.

He looked at her. Her eyes were brown, the same brown, but the light moved through them differently. As though someone had adjusted the focus.

He did not know how she had entered Margot. There was no bowl on the table. The key was still in the kitchen drawer. That not-knowing stayed with him longer than the wine stain.

“Margot,” he said.

“Don’t say my name like you’re checking ID.”

She kissed him.

It was a good kiss. Better than good. It tasted like want without the buffer of history. Her tongue moved with an eagerness that felt borrowed, as though she were trying out an appetite she had heard described but never personally owned.

His hand moved to her waist. The dress was thin. He felt the heat of her through it, the ridge of her hipbone, the slight tremor in her stomach. She made a sound against his mouth—not quite a moan, more like approval.

Then, for less than a second, the mouth under his stopped.

Everything stopped. The tongue, the hands, the small greedy rhythm. And something came up hard behind the face and pressed against the inside of it, the way a person comes up against a window.

Lyle felt it, and did not understand it, and it was gone before he could.

Whatever it was went back down.

Then her fingers tapped against his shoulder.

Two fast. One slow. Two fast.

He pulled back.

Margot’s face was flushed. Her lips were parted. She looked beautiful in a way that made him angry.

“How long did it take?” he asked.

Her expression didn’t change. Not immediately. Then the corner of her mouth lifted, and it was Freya’s smile wearing Margot’s bones.

“Get out of her,” Lyle said.

“Which part upset you? The kiss, or that you liked it?”

“Freya.”

“I was inside her. I felt you decide.” She pressed Margot’s hand harder against his chest. “Right here. Your heart changed speed. You wanted her. You wanted me wearing her. Don’t lie. I was there for the whole thing.”

Lyle stood. The wine glass tipped, spilling red across the coffee table — the same table that had held contracts, popcorn, the signed hour. He didn’t look at it. Paper and wine both inadequate truth.

“Not tonight. Not to this. Not with you inside her.”

Freya made Margot’s body recline against the couch with a languidness that belonged to neither of them. She crossed Margot’s legs at the ankle, a gesture too deliberate to be Margot’s.

“You don’t get to refuse on her behalf,” Freya said. “She’s not here. She’s in the back seat, watching through the windows. And she’s seeing exactly what I’m seeing. That you still love the shape of this marriage. You just needed it to want you back.”

“Stop.”

“Make me.”

Lyle reached for her—Margot’s arm, Freya’s arm, he didn’t know anymore. He gripped above the elbow and pulled. For a second, Margot’s face surfaced. Genuine confusion. Genuine hurt. Then Freya covered it again.

“You’re hurting her,” Freya said calmly.

“You’re wearing her.”

“And you’re hard. Even now. Even knowing.”

He let go. He stepped back until his calves hit the armchair. He sat down heavily.

Freya watched him from Margot’s eyes. There was no triumph in the look. Only a terrible, exhausted satisfaction, as though she had proven something she wished were false.

“How long did it take?” Lyle asked again.

“For what?”

“For you to recognize that I’d fail.”

Freya was quiet. Then she said, “I didn’t know you would. I was hoping you wouldn’t.”

“Get out of her.”

“Not yet. I haven’t finished being disappointed.”

She stood up. Margot’s body moved toward him with a grace that was almost threatening. She straddled his lap before he could rise. Her hands framed his face. Margot’s hands. Warm. Familiar in their shape, alien in their intention.

“I could stay,” Freya whispered. “She wouldn’t know. You wouldn’t have to be alone. I could be her for you. I could be better than her. I already know what you like.”

Lyle’s hands found her wrists. He held them still.

“You’re not her,” he said.

“I’m not me either. Not anymore. So what does it matter which not-me I am?”

The door to Iris’s bedroom opened.

They both turned. Iris stood in the hallway in her pyjamas, clutching the stuffed rabbit she was supposedly too old for. She looked at her mother’s body sitting on her father’s lap. She looked at her mother’s face, which was wearing an expression of need and calculation that had never belonged to Margot.

“Why are you wearing Mom?” Iris asked.

Freya went still. Margot’s body became a statue. Then, slowly, Freya slid off Lyle’s lap and stood up. She adjusted Margot’s dress with Margot’s hands.

“Iris,” Freya said. “Go back to bed.”

Iris didn’t move. She looked at Lyle.

“Dad?”

“Go to your room, sweetheart.”

“That’s not Mom.”

“I know.”

“Then why is Mom’s body here?”

Freya turned. Margot’s face was pale, the makeup smeared at one corner of the mouth. She looked at Iris and did not seem to know what to do with her face.

“Your mother is fine,” Freya said. “She’s just… visiting.”

“You’re wearing her,” Iris said. “Like you wore Bethany. Like you wore Cora. But she’s Mom.”

Freya’s composure cracked. Margot’s lower lip trembled. For a moment, neither woman was in control—Freya too shaken to maintain the mask, Margot too buried to surface.

Then Margot’s breath stopped.

Her chest hitched. Her eyes rolled back, the whites showing, and her body went slack. Lyle lunged forward and caught her before she hit the coffee table. She was heavy in a way that felt like absence, like a building with the utilities shut off.

He looked around the room.

The kitchen doorway. The hallway. The dark corner by the bookshelf where Iris’s jacket hung.

Nothing. No shadow, no shimmer, no voice. But he felt the attention. The room was occupied by something he could not see or touch, something that had just been inside his ex-wife and was now listening to him hold her empty body.

Margot gasped.

Her eyes opened—focused, confused, entirely hers. She looked at Lyle’s hands on her shoulders. She looked down at herself, at the dress, at the unfamiliar blue fabric bunched between his fingers. She pinched it with two of her own fingers, pulling it away from her body as though it were wet.

“I don’t own this,” she said.

She touched her mouth and found it swollen.

“Lyle?” Her voice was hers. Thinner. Frightened. “What happened? Why am I…”

She looked at the spilled wine. At the couch. At his face.

“I was at the light,” she said. “Near your street. I had the jacket. And then…”

She stopped. Her hand went to her stomach, pressing against the skin as though feeling something move beneath it.

“Someone was here,” Margot said. “Inside. I could feel… she was angry at you. She was so angry. And I could feel…” She looked at Lyle with dawning horror. “I wanted you. I don’t want you. Not like that. Not anymore. But I wanted you. I could feel her wanting you through my hands.”

She backed away from him, from the couch, from the spilled wine.

“Iris,” Margot said. “Come here.”

Iris didn’t move. She was looking at her mother with the same precision she used now on every adult who came into the apartment, reading the body for its occupant.

“Mom?” Iris asked.

“Yes, baby. It’s me now.”

“How do I know?”

Margot’s face crumpled. She reached for her daughter, and Iris flinched.

The flinch was small, and Margot saw it.

Margot looked at Lyle. Her eyes were wet, fully hers, fully awake.

“You let her in,” Margot said. Not an accusation. A fact. “You let her use me. In our daughter’s home.”

“I didn’t know. Not at first.”

“But you knew. Before the end. You knew, and you didn’t…” She touched her mouth again. “You called me Freya. I heard you. I heard my name in your mouth and it wasn’t me. It was her. You were touching me and saying her name.”

She found her purse on the floor. She didn’t remember setting it down. She slung it over her shoulder with a jerky violence.

“I’m taking Iris,” Margot said.

“Margot—”

“Don’t.” The word was sharp enough to cut. “You don’t get to say my name tonight. You don’t get to pretend you know which one of us you’re talking to.”

She held out her hand to Iris. Iris looked at her father. Then she walked to her mother, slowly, but she didn’t take Margot’s hand. She walked beside her, maintaining a careful inch of space.

At the door, Margot stopped. She didn’t turn around.

“She left something,” Margot said. “Inside. I can feel it. Not thoughts. Not exactly. But the shape of them. She was jealous of me. She wanted to know if you still loved this body more than hers.” Margot’s voice cracked, then steadied. “I don’t think you know the difference between loving someone and missing what they used to give you.”

She opened the door.

“Iris, come on.”

Iris looked back at Lyle. She didn’t say goodbye. She didn’t say anything. But her eyes held the question she had asked her mother four minutes earlier, that nobody in the house had answered yet.

How do I know?

The door closed.

Lyle sat in the armchair. The wine soaked into the rug beneath the coffee table. He touched his shoulder where Margot’s—Freya’s—fingers had tapped the pattern.

Two fast. One slow. Two fast. The rhythm that once meant attention now meant invasion dressed as love.

“Are you still here?” he asked the empty room.

No answer.

He sat there until the stain dried.

23

THE NEUTRAL ADDRESS

The letter came on the Tuesday and the meeting was on the Friday, and in between Lyle did almost nothing, which he later understood to have been the week.

The apartment still knew what had happened. Wine ghost under the coffee table if you got low enough. Shoulder that remembered a rhythm. Iris’s empty room until Margot’s schedule said otherwise. No clean morning. The pipes did not need to speak; the letter spoke in their place.

The letter was from a firm on Colmore Row with three names in the title and a reference number that was longer than his phone number. It used the phrase your former partner four times and the word incident once, in a sentence Lyle read eleven times.

Following the incident of 3 October, our client seeks to regularise arrangements for the child by consent, in advance of any application to the court.

By consent.

He looked at those two words for a long time.

Cora read it at her kitchen table with her coat still on.

“That’s good,” she said.

“That’s good?”

“That’s her solicitor telling you she’d rather not go to a judge.” She turned the page. “It costs money and it takes eleven weeks and a judge would ask you both what happened on the third of October.” She looked up. “Do you want a judge asking you what happened on the third of October?”

“No.”

“Then it’s good.”

The school letter was still on the fridge, under the watermelon magnet, with THURS 9 OCT — 2pm — BE THERE 1:50 on the front of it in his own capitals. Promise paper beside the new legal paper. Same fridge. Different weather.

Thursday was the ninth.

At one-fifty Lyle sat in the car outside the flat with the engine off. He had decided that morning that he would go if Margot rang — a condition he had invented, and had told nobody, and which put the thing in her hands.

She did not ring.

He could have rung the school and asked whether parents came without their children. It would have taken four minutes.

At ten past two he went back inside.

He did not take the letter down.

They did it in a room above a building society because Margot’s solicitor had an arrangement with somebody.

There were six people. Margot and her solicitor. Lyle, with no solicitor, because he had rung two and neither would look at it without four hundred pounds up front. A duty mediator whose name Lyle did not retain. Cora, who had been asked to come and had come.

And Iris, for eleven minutes at the start, so that everybody could say hello to her and she could see that this was a room with biscuits in it and not a thing being done to her behind a door.

Then a woman from the building society took her downstairs.

The document was four pages. Cool thick paper. Staple catch. Initial boxes like the ones Freya had filled in a better basement, under a different kind of light.

It had numbered sections. It had subsections. It had boxes down the right-hand margin for initials.

Lyle got to the bottom of page one and had to stop, because his hands had gone strange and he could not immediately work out why. The pen would not dig. Desire was not what paper was trying to govern now. Custody was.

1.2 — The parties agree the arrangements set out below are temporary and shall be reviewed on or before 12 January.

2.1 — The child shall reside with the father, subject to the conditions at section 4.

3.1 — The mother shall have the child from Friday 18:00 to Sunday 10:00 on alternate weekends, until review.

3.2 — Exchanges shall take place at the address of Ms C. Bell, agreed by both parties as a neutral venue.

And then section four, which was a list of things he must not do, written in cool declarative sentences by somebody who had never met him.

4.1 — The father shall not permit the child to be present where any ritual, spiritual, or occult practice is conducted, whether or not for payment.

4.2 — The father shall not permit any person to be present with the child whose identity or condition he cannot vouch for.

4.3 — The father shall not use, prepare, or store water or implements associated with the practices described at 4.1 in any room used by the child.

(Water, which had been mechanism and intimacy and violation, was now a line item. The basin under the sink became illegal weather.)

4.4 — The father shall disclose to the mother, in writing and within 24 hours of any change, the address at which the child sleeps.

Lyle read 4.4 twice.

Then he read the whole page again from the top, more slowly, and this time he was not reading a legal document at all.

He was reading Cora’s contract.

Same shape. Same numbering. Same cool voice. The host may terminate permission at any time, for any reason, including reasons not stated, understood, justified, or communicated. Somebody sitting at a table with a pen, deciding in advance which parts of a life another person is allowed access to, and writing it down, because the alternative was trusting them.

He had thought that was a kind of poetry when Cora did it.

He had said this is longer than my divorce agreement and Cora had said that explains your divorce, and everyone had laughed, and it had been one of the happiest afternoons of his year.

“Mr Mercer?”

Lyle looked up.

“Do you want to take a minute?”

“No,” he said. “No. Sorry. I’m reading it.”

He argued about two things and won one.

He argued about 4.2, on the grounds that it was unworkable — whose identity or condition he cannot vouch for would technically cover a supply teacher — and the mediator agreed and it was amended to persons unknown to the mother, which was not much better and which he signed anyway.

He argued about the review date and lost.

He did not argue about 4.4.

Cora noticed, and mentioned it once, months later, in a stairwell.

He did not argue about 4.4 because it was the easiest one in the document. It cost nothing. It required no change to how he lived. All it asked was that when the place his daughter slept changed, he write it down and send it.

He thought, reading it: fine. That one’s fine.

Margot spoke twice in ninety minutes.

The first time was to say that she was not trying to take Iris away from him, and that she wanted the review in January to be the end of it, and that she had said so to her solicitor in exactly those words, and would he please believe her.

The second was at the end, when it was signed, and the solicitor was putting the caps back on things.

“Lyle.”

He looked up.

“On the third of October,” Margot said. “In my house. In my body.”

The mediator said her name.

Margot did not look away from him.

“I’m not going to be able to stop asking about it,” she said. “I want you to understand that now, so that when I ask you in six months you don’t act surprised.”

Cora agreed to the address in the room, out loud, on the record.

She did it in the flattest voice she owned, and afterwards, on the stairs, she said:

“I want to say one thing and then I’m never saying it again.”

“Okay.”

“It’s my flat. Every Friday at six and every Sunday at ten, it’s my flat, and I’ll have the kettle on and I will not have an opinion.” She was doing the thing with the pens in her hair. “I’m not a supervisor. I’m not a witness. I’m not part of your arrangement. I’m a place where two adults hand a child over without having a conversation.”

“Cora—”

“I’m doing it for her.” She got the door. “And if I ever have to be more than a place, I’m going to be a lot more than a place. Just so you know what’s in the room.”

Iris was in the car with a biscuit in each hand.

“Are you and Mom fighting?”

“No.”

“You’re a bit.”

“We’re a bit.”

Lyle put the seatbelt across her and did the clip.

“There’s a bit of paper now,” he said, “that says what happens when. So nobody has to guess and nobody has to argue about it.”

Iris considered that.

“Is it like Cora’s one?”

He stopped with his hand on the buckle.

“What?”

“Cora’s one. With the numbers.” She turned the biscuit over. “The one where Freya has to leave when Cora says.”

“…Yes,” Lyle said. “It’s like that one.”

“Good,” Iris said, and ate the biscuit. “That one works.”

The first exchange was that Friday.

Lyle got her there at ten to six with her bag packed and her reading log signed — signed, not initialled — and Cora had the kettle on, and Margot came up at six exactly and did not come in.

It took ninety seconds and nobody said anything they had to take back.

He did it the next week too, and the week after, and the week after that.

The system worked. Nobody afterwards would believe it, and it was true: for five weeks, the piece of paper did exactly what it was for. Iris went on Friday and came back Sunday. Margot stopped ringing at odd hours. Lyle put the dates in his phone and made them all, and one Sunday in early November Margot said “Thanks” through the gap in the door, and meant it.

Then the wall came down.

24

THE ROSTER

Without Cora, the business took eleven days to stop working.

Lyle had not understood how much of it she had been. He had thought of her as the host, which is to say the part in the middle, and discovered over a fortnight that she had also been the diary, the intake, the safeguarding, the pricing, the aftercare, and the reason people said yes twice.

Three jobs cancelled in the first week. Two of those were Donna’s referrals, and both cancelled for the same reason, which Donna relayed with enormous embarrassment on the phone: they’d heard there was a lady who did the paperwork and they wanted the one with the paperwork.

The Hendricks job went ahead without a host and was a disaster.

Lyle did it as a straight performance — no possession, just the card and the stole and forty minutes of theatre in a conservatory in Solihull — and it worked, technically, in the sense that nothing supernatural happened and nobody asked for a refund. He came out three hundred pounds up and sat in the car with his hands on the wheel in the cooling quiet — after Harold weather, after Keith weather — feeling something he did not immediately recognise as shame. No wet-penny. No true exit. Only the stole and the card and money that did not clean the third of October.

Freya, in Martin, said: “You did it without me.”

“There wasn’t anyone to put you in.”

“You did it without me and it went fine.”

“It went flat.”

“Yes,” Freya said. “It went flat and it went fine, and now you know.”

The roster, at the end of September, had four names on it.

Martin, who was seventy-one and could not do more than an hour and had a heart.

Bethany, who had not returned a call since May.

Patricia, who had said no in writing.

And Cora, crossed out, still legible, because Lyle had crossed it out with one line instead of scribbling it. One line, like a stay that could be reversed. He knew better. The pen still lied that way.

He put the ad back up in October.

He wrote it better this time. Two years of Cora’s influence had made him almost competent — he listed the risks properly, and the rate, and the fact that there was a contract, and he did not use the word host anywhere in it.

He got nine replies.

Seven were unusable. One was a man who wanted to know whether he could be possessed by somebody specific, which Lyle spent a bad evening thinking about.

The ninth was Desmond.

Desmond Ackroyd was thirty-four, wiry, sharp-faced, and worked nights at a distribution centre off the Coventry Road.

He arrived at the interview — Lyle had started calling them interviews, because Cora had — with a printed copy of the advert on which he had underlined four things and written ? beside two of them.

“‘Emotional residue,’” he read out. “What’s that mean.”

“You might feel something afterwards that isn’t yours.”

“For how long.”

“Hours. Sometimes a day.”

“Has anybody had longer.”

Lyle thought about Cora saying I don’t know whose those are, and about Jared rubbing a wrist that had stopped hurting in 2014.

“Yes,” he said.

Desmond wrote something down.

“Right,” he said. “Two hundred, and I want it in cash on the night, and I’m not doing anything where somebody’s mother’s in the room.”

“That’s most of the work.”

“Then I’m not doing most of the work.”

He had a reason and he gave it once, flatly, when Lyle did not ask.

“My sister’s got a thing with her spine. She’s on a list. The list is four years.” He put his hands on the table. “There’s a place in Germany does it in eleven weeks and it’s nineteen grand, and I’ve got about four, and I’m thirty-four and I work nights, so.”

“Desmond—”

“I’m not telling you so you’ll feel a certain way about it. I’m telling you so that when I say I’ll do two a week you understand that I mean two a week, and that I’m not going to get frightened and stop.”

He did not like Freya.

That was the other thing, and Lyle had never had it before, and it changed the temperature of everything.

Bethany had been curious. Martin had been delighted. Patricia had been professional until she wasn’t. Cora had been — whatever Cora had been.

Desmond was polite, punctual, extremely good at it, and did not like her at all.

“She talks to me like the van,” he said, in the car park after the third job. “Not like a bloke. Like the van.”

“She does that.”

“No, I mean specifically. Like I’m a vehicle she’s been assigned and she’s checking the mirrors.” He lit a cigarette he was not supposed to have. “She’s not rude about it. She’s rude about everything else. She’s careful with me because I’ve got terms.” He blew smoke at the ceiling of the car park. “I’d rather she was rude, if I’m honest. Careful means she’s decided something.”

The thumb was the thing nobody could do anything about.

Desmond cracked the knuckle of his left thumb. Constantly, unconsciously, at a rate of perhaps four times a minute, and had done since he was eleven, and had been told to stop by every partner, employer and dentist he had ever had.

Freya could not stop it.

She could stop everything else. She could still Martin’s hands and reset Patricia’s jaw and hold Cora’s breathing at any rate she liked. But somewhere in the wiring of Desmond Ackroyd there was a loop she could not get in front of, and every fifteen seconds, in the middle of whatever appalling thing she was performing in somebody’s front room, that thumb went click.

The first time it happened she stopped mid-sentence and looked at the hand as though it had spoken.

“What was that.”

“That’s him.”

“I’m aware it’s him, Lyle, I would like to know why it’s happening while I’m driving.”

“He does it. He’s always done it.”

She tried, twice, in the car afterwards, to hold the hand still and wait it out.

It went at fourteen seconds and then at nine.

“Well,” Freya said. “That’s fucking horrible.”

It bothered her for weeks, and she said so, and the reason she gave was the one Lyle could not stop turning over afterwards.

She could put a woman under in her own body and hold her there for forty minutes.

She could not stop a man cracking his thumb.

“They’re different rooms,” she said. “The one where you decide things is easy. Anybody could get into that. It’s the wiring underneath that won’t have me.”

Which turned out to be useful, in the way that things did.

On the fourth job — a bungalow in Yardley, a family who had wanted this very badly for a long time — Lyle came around the corner into a back bedroom and found Desmond standing at the window with his back to the room, absolutely still, and said, “You all right?”

And Desmond said, “Yeah.”

And Lyle got as far as good before he understood that the hand hanging at Desmond’s side had not moved in the whole time he had been standing there.

“Freya.”

“Mm.”

“You said you’d come out at nine.”

“It’s four minutes past nine.”

“You said you’d come out at nine.”

“I was looking at something,” Freya said, and turned around, and the face was doing the thing Desmond’s face did not do, and Lyle stood in a stranger’s back bedroom with his heart going and understood that he had spoken to her for eleven seconds believing she was somebody else.

He had never once been wrong before.

He had built an entire relationship on never once being wrong.

That night he wrote it in the book, under the date, in the column Cora had ruled:

4 mins over. D. did not signal. I did not know.

Desmond read it at the next job, because Desmond read everything.

“You wrote it down.”

“That’s what the book’s for.”

“Most people wouldn’t.” He handed it back. “My last job before this, bloke used to log the near-misses in a different book to the incidents. Two books. Everyone knew.”

“Is that a compliment?”

“It’s an observation.” He cracked the thumb. “Keep it in one book, Lyle. Two books is how it gets away from you.”

He did nine sessions between the eleventh of October and the twenty-second of November.

He was, by a distance, the best host Lyle ever worked with — punctual, unshakeable, unsentimental, and so precise about his own terms that Freya stopped testing them somewhere around the fifth job and never started again.

He never asked what she was.

He asked, once, in the car, whether it hurt her to leave.

Lyle said yes.

Desmond thought about that for a while, and cracked his thumb twice, and said, “Good,” and did not explain, and Lyle did not ask him to.

PART V

THE CITY OPENS

25

THE TRUCK

Three weeks after Cora stopped hosting Freya — three weeks after the wine, the rhythm on the shoulder, the letter, the section that made his kitchen illegal — a truck stole part of Lyle’s car.

It began outside a hardware store, and it began there because of section four. Aftershock carried in the glovebox: key in the dish towel, exile of the work from the child’s rooms, copper traveling like contraband.

Sections 4.1 and 4.3 had taken the business out of the flat. No ritual practice where the child could be present. No prepared water or associated implements in any room she used. Lyle had read those the first time as a description of a man he was not, and had discovered within a fortnight that they described his kitchen, his bathroom, his bedroom windowsill, and the cereal bowl.

So the work went outside. Jobs were done in other people’s houses now, and the key travelled in the dish towel in the glovebox, and Lyle had spent three weeks conducting his entire supernatural life in car parks and function rooms and one memorable pub cellar in Digbeth. He had bought a secondhand leather jacket for the outside work because the long-sleeve was not enough for the ring road at night. One cuff was more cracked than the other. It made him look like a divorced electrician who had wandered into neo-noir and was hoping nobody would ask him to smoke. It failed as armor and as fashion. He wore it anyway.

Which is why he was on the ring road on a Saturday afternoon with the key in the car, buying light bulbs, duct tape, and a replacement smoke detector because the original had been converted into a demon meter and never recovered professionally.

He came out carrying two paper bags, cracked cuff catching on the bag handle, and found a white box truck parked beside his sedan.

The truck had no company name.

No number plate on the back.

Its cargo doors stood open.

Inside were hollow-core doors.

Dozens of them.

They had been stacked upright and secured with chains. Each edge was capped in copper. Shallow channels ran around their faces. Some carried numbers carved into the veneer — long sequences of them, with measurements, and arrows.

One had a human shape outlined across it in faded red pencil.

Lyle stopped beside his car.

A woman in grey coveralls pushed another door up the truck’s ramp.

She was broad-shouldered, perhaps fifty, with a shaved head and a blue thread bracelet around one wrist.

The door moved past Lyle.

Water shone inside its copper groove. Wet-penny from a door that was not his, in a dry car park, in daylight that did not care. Discovery becoming threat at industrial scale.

The day was dry.

“Where did you get those?” he asked.

The woman kept pushing.

“Work.”

“What kind?”

“Door work.”

“That is aggressively unhelpful.”

She secured the door with a leather strap.

Then she looked at him.

Her gaze moved to his hands — the same hands that had held Iris’s head for oof, that had held Margot’s wrists, that had signed initials in a room above a building society.

To the faint mineral staining beneath his nails from handling the laryngeal key.

Her expression changed.

“Go home,” she said.

Lyle stared.

“Do I know you?”

“No.”

“Do you know me?”

“No.”

“That was a very personal ‘go home.’”

The woman climbed into the truck.

Lyle walked closer.

“What do the numbers mean?”

The driver shut her door.

Lyle knocked on the glass.

She looked straight ahead.

The truck started.

Water began leaking from beneath it.

Not engine fluid.

Clear water.

It spread across the pavement, paused at the gutter, then flowed uphill toward a storm drain behind the store.

Lyle watched it.

The truck pulled away.

A length of chain trailed from the open rear bumper.

Its hook dragged across the asphalt.

Lyle should have let it go.

Freya had once warned him about trucks.

It had happened during an argument so unrelated that he had assumed she was improvising.

She had been inside Martin.

They were discussing whether Lyle should advertise evening appointments when Freya suddenly looked toward the street.

“If you ever see a white truck carrying doors,” she said, “do not follow it.”

Lyle had waited for context.

None came.

“Why?”

“Because I said not to.”

“That has never persuaded me.”

“I know.”

Then she changed the subject.

Now the truck carrying copper-edged doors turned onto Fulton.

Lyle put the bags in his car.

He followed.

His sedan was twelve years old, dented along both sides, and held together by the shared optimism of zip ties and delayed maintenance.

It made a clicking sound during left turns.

Lyle called it a feature.

The truck drove slowly at first.

Lyle stayed two cars behind.

He told himself he was not following it. They were merely traveling in the same direction through a city with several million possible destinations.

The truck turned.

Lyle turned.

The truck changed lanes.

Lyle changed lanes.

At the third light, the driver looked at him through the side mirror.

Lyle pretended to inspect his dashboard.

The light changed.

The truck accelerated.

The trailing chain swung.

Its hook caught beneath Lyle’s passenger-side mirror.

There was a metallic snap.

The mirror vanished.

For half a second, it hung from the chain, bouncing against the truck’s rear bumper.

Then the attached panel began peeling away from Lyle’s door.

Lyle screamed.

Not from fear.

From offense.

“That was structurally fucking important!”

The truck kept moving.

The outer panel ripped halfway loose and flapped beside the car.

Lyle honked.

The horn produced one weak, asthmatic note.

The driver glanced back.

Then accelerated.

Lyle accelerated too.

The clicking in his front wheel became a continuous rattle.

He lowered the passenger window and shouted:

“Pull over!”

The window reached halfway down and jammed.

The truck turned onto a narrow side street.

Lyle followed.

The loose panel struck a parked car, folded backward, and tore free with a sound like a metal animal being disemboweled.

It remained attached to the truck’s chain.

Lyle watched part of his sedan disappear around the corner.

“You stole my car!”

Technically, most of the car remained with him.

Emotionally, he rejected this distinction.

The truck crossed an intersection against the light.

Lyle followed and nearly collided with a delivery bicycle.

The cyclist slapped his roof and shouted something anatomically ambitious.

“Emergency,” Lyle shouted back.

The street narrowed.

Buildings crowded closer together.

Storefronts gave way to shuttered garages, loading docks, and brick walls painted with old advertisements.

The truck turned into an alley.

Lyle entered seconds later.

The alley ended at a concrete wall.

No truck.

No open garage.

No turn.

Only brick on both sides and a shallow loading bay ahead.

Lyle stopped.

His car idled unevenly.

Water ran along the alley floor.

Uphill.

It disappeared beneath the loading bay door.

Lyle got out.

The passenger side of the sedan looked bitten.

Wires hung where the mirror had been.

A strip of metal curled outward like peeled bark.

He stared at it.

Then at the loading bay.

“You owe me a door,” he called.

His voice returned from the alley walls.

Nothing answered.

He approached the loading bay.

The metal shutter was rusted at the bottom.

Dust lay undisturbed along its track.

It had not opened recently.

Lyle crouched.

The water flowed beneath it.

He touched the stream.

His fingertips went numb.

The sensation moved up his hand in a narrow line, almost directional.

The same feeling as the rainwater inside the hollow-core door.

He stood quickly.

Beside the loading bay was a personnel entrance.

A cheap beige hollow-core door had been fitted directly into the brick wall.

Fresh screws gleamed in the hinges.

There was no frame around it.

No handle.

Only a copper-edged hole where a knob should have been.

Lyle stepped closer.

A piece of blue thread caught on one screw.

He pulled it free.

The thread was damp.

Warm.

Something knocked from the other side.

Two fast.

One slow.

Lyle stopped breathing.

Then came another pattern.

Not Freya’s.

Three evenly spaced impacts.

Metallic.

Measured.

A maintenance rhythm.

The first pattern answered.

Two fast.

One slow.

The second interrupted before it could finish.

Lyle reached toward the knob hole.

The key was at home.

Wrapped in Cora’s old dish towel.

The water at his feet rose.

Only half an inch.

Enough to cover the soles of his shoes.

From beyond the door came a sound like a heavy chain being drawn through a pipe.

Lyle stepped back.

The door bowed outward.

Not opening.

Breathing.

The veneer flexed around an enormous pressure from the other side.

A voice spoke behind him.

“You should have gone home.”

Lyle turned.

The woman in coveralls stood at the alley entrance.

No truck behind her.

Her blue thread bracelet had come loose at one end.

“That vehicle removed part of my car,” Lyle said.

The woman looked at the sedan.

“It improved it.”

“It has electrical components exposed.”

“It had electrical components exposed before.”

“That isn’t relevant.”

The door knocked again.

The woman’s expression hardened.

She walked past Lyle and placed one palm against the veneer.

The pressure stopped.

“What is behind that?” Lyle asked.

“Nothing you can afford.”

“I’m not paying.”

“You always are.”

She removed the blue thread from his hand.

Lyle tried to hold on.

The woman twisted his wrist with practiced ease.

He released it.

“Who are you?”

“Someone cleaning up after the woman you keep inviting into people.”

“Freya?”

The woman’s eyes moved toward the door.

“She has not told you.”

“Told me what?”

“Enough to stop asking.”

“That’s not an answer.”

“No,” the woman said. “It is an exit.”

She knocked once against the door.

The water reversed.

It flowed away from Lyle’s shoes and disappeared beneath the wall.

The hollow-core door settled flat.

The woman pressed her thumb into the copper-edged knob hole.

There was a click.

The entire door slid sideways into the brick, which is not what doors do.

Behind it was darkness and the smell of old stone, soup, hot metal, and standing water.

Far inside, amber bulbs burned along a descending corridor.

People moved across the opening.

A child carried a bundle of copper pipe.

An old man rolled a television set on a cart.

Someone laughed.

Then a siren sounded in the distance.

Low.

Mechanical.

The people stopped moving.

The woman stepped through.

Lyle followed one pace.

She turned.

“Not tonight.”

Something roared far below, and it was not an animal, and it was not entirely a machine either.

The corridor lights flickered.

A figure appeared at the bottom of the passage.

Too far away to understand.

Large enough to erase the light behind it.

A chainsaw engine coughed once.

Then again.

The woman shoved Lyle backward.

The door slid shut.

Brick covered the place where it had been.

No seam remained.

Lyle stood with one hand against a wall.

The chainsaw started behind it.

The sound moved downward, away from him, into an impossible depth beneath the city.

He returned to his car.

The sedan’s dashboard displayed four warning lights it had not possessed that morning.

Lyle drove home with no passenger mirror and half a door panel.

At the apartment, Iris sat at the kitchen table coloring a picture of a city made entirely of rooms.

Cora sat across from her with a stack of custody forms Margot had asked Lyle to complete two weeks earlier.

Freya was absent.

Cora looked up.

“What happened to your car?”

“A truck stole part of it.”

Cora waited.

“It was attached to a chain.”

“That raises more questions.”

“I know.”

Iris held up her drawing.

The city had pipes instead of roads.

Every building was connected by little doors.

A blue figure stood at the bottom.

“What’s that?” Lyle asked.

“The man who comes back.”

Cora looked at him.

Lyle’s hand closed around the copper stain on his fingertips.

“Who told you about him?”

“The rude lady.”

“What did she say?”

Iris returned to coloring.

“That the city loves him.”

Lyle sat across from her.

“And?”

“And it chains him.”

Cora stopped sorting papers.

Iris drew a line of red crayon through the blue figure’s hand.

“And every year he goes outside.”

“What does he do outside?”

Iris shrugged.

“Freya wouldn’t say.”

From inside the kitchen wall came three evenly spaced knocks.

Cora heard them.

Lyle saw that she had.

Iris continued coloring.

“She said he always comes back,” she said.

The pipes knocked again.

Two fast.

One slow.

Then three even blows answered before the pattern could finish.

26

THE VOCAL CROSS-TRAINING

Martin arrived at seven with a thermos and a scarf, which he wore in all weather because he had spent forty years being told a voice was an instrument and had never entirely recovered. The apartment still held truck dust and unsigned custody forms. Iris at Margot’s. The night of the blue dress not spoken. Lyle’s face already the conversation Martin had named.

“You said urgent,” he said.

“I did.”

“Urgent at my age means a fall.”

“Nobody’s fallen.”

Martin looked past him into the apartment. Iris was at Margot’s until Sunday. The custody forms were still on the kitchen table where Cora had left them, still unsigned, now nineteen days late. Paper waiting. The watermelon magnet still held a school date that had already passed without him.

“Where’s the young woman? Cora.”

“She’s not doing this any more.”

“Ah.” Martin set the thermos down. “And you didn’t want to sit with that for a week first.”

“No.”

“No,” Martin agreed. He unwound the scarf. “Terms?”

“Ninety minutes. No leaving the apartment. Nothing that damages you.”

“And the other thing.”

“There’s no other thing tonight.”

Martin gave him a look that had closed the second act of several regional productions.

“Lyle. There’s a look on your face I have only ever seen on men about to have a conversation they have been putting off. I am about to be the room it happens in. I would like to know that.”

Lyle got the key out of the towel. Kitchen only — section four’s ghost even with Iris gone. Tea-dark in the glass. He hated how ordinary the preparation looked now.

“Yeah,” he said. “That’s about right.”

“Then I’d like an hour and a half and my fee and one condition.”

“Name it.”

“If it goes badly, you say the word and she comes out. Not one more sentence. Not the last word.” He sat down and put his hands on his knees like a man settling into a dentist’s chair. “I have been in a great many domestic scenes. Somebody always wants the last word. It will not be had through me.”

“Agreed.”

“Good.” Martin closed his eyes. “Right. Off we go.”

The change was not dramatic.

It never was, which Lyle had stopped finding reassuring some months ago. Martin’s shoulders came down two inches. His head tipped. The hands on the knees rearranged themselves into a shape Martin’s hands did not make.

“He’s got a lovely instrument,” Freya said. “It’s like driving a car with a full tank for once.”

“We need to talk.”

“Oh, hello. Straight in.”

“A truck took half my car.”

“Did it.”

“On Saturday. Outside the hardware place.”

Freya crossed Martin’s legs.

“Was it white?”

Lyle stopped.

He had rehearsed four openings on the drive back and none of them had accounted for her simply answering.

“Yes.”

“Was it carrying doors.”

“Yes.”

“Then I told you,” she said, “explicitly, in this room, that if you ever saw a white truck carrying doors you were not to follow it.”

“You didn’t tell me why.”

“Because the why is a door, Lyle, that’s the entire problem with the why, the why is a door and you are the single most curious idiot I have ever loved.”

“There was a woman in coveralls.”

Freya went still.

Martin’s hands stopped moving. That was how Lyle always knew — not the face, which she managed, but the extremities, which she forgot.

“Describe her.”

“Fifty. Shaved head. Blue thread round her wrist.”

“What did she say.”

“She said she was cleaning up after the woman I keep inviting into people.”

The room did a thing rooms do when a fridge cycles off.

“Right,” Freya said.

“Right?”

“What would you like instead?”

“An argument. Denial. Something.”

“You want me to tell you she was lying.”

“I want you to tell me anything.”

Freya stood Martin up and walked him to the window, and the walk was wrong for Martin, too fast, too short in the stride, a woman’s pace in a man’s legs.

“There’s a place,” she said. “It’s under the district around Fulton and Kerr, and it’s not under it exactly, it’s — through it. Behind it. It’s about eleven hundred people and it’s been there a long time and it takes in the kind of people the rest of the city has finished with.”

“Eleven hundred.”

“Give or take.”

“There are eleven hundred people living inside the city and nobody—”

“That’s the whole point of it, love.”

“And you know about it.”

“Yes.”

“How.”

A pause. Lyle could see the shape of the pause. He had seen her take exactly that long before, sitting in Cora, being asked what she wanted for her birthday, a question that should not have needed four seconds.

“I lived there,” she said.

“When.”

“Before.”

“Before what.”

“Before the part you already know about.”

He put both hands flat on the back of a chair.

“There is a woman in a boiler suit,” he said, “who looked at my hands and told me to go home. She knew what the mineral staining was. She knew what I’d been doing with the key. She has been keeping a list, Freya, she has been cleaning up after you, and you are standing in Martin telling me you lived somewhere.”

“Yes.”

“That’s the whole answer.”

“That’s the whole answer you’re getting tonight.”

“Why?”

And here she did something he had not expected and did not have a defence prepared for.

She turned around and told him the truth about the shape of the lie.

“Because if I tell you the next part, you’ll go,” she said. “Not tomorrow. Tonight. You’ll put your shoes on and drive to Fulton with a torch and that idiot expression you get, and they will let you in, because they let people in, that’s what the place is, and then you’ll be inside it, and once you’ve been inside it you don’t get to be a man with a daughter and a bad car and an unsigned custody form any more.”

“That’s not your decision.”

“No,” she agreed. “It’s yours. That’s why I’ve been not making it for eight months.”

“You’ve been lying for eight months.”

“I have been omitting for eight months, and you have been extremely comfortable in the omission, and I would like that noted.”

“Don’t.”

“You never asked where I was from, Lyle.”

“That’s—”

“Not once. Eight months. You have asked me what it’s like to be dead, what it’s like to be inside a man, whether I can taste through Cora, whether it hurts when a body rejects me, whether I’d have fancied you when I was alive—”

“That was a joke.”

“It was three times.” She let that sit. “You have asked me a great many questions about what I am. You have never once asked me who I was, and I have let you not ask, and we have both been having a lovely time.”

Lyle’s hands were shaking on the chair back.

Not from anger. That was the humiliating part.

“There’s a thing that comes out,” he said. “Isn’t there.”

Freya didn’t answer.

“I heard it. Behind the wall. There was a chainsaw behind a wall and the woman shoved me back into an alley and the door went into the brick like it was never there.”

“Yes.”

“Once a year.”

“Yes.”

“And they lock it up afterwards.”

“Yes.”

“And you know its name.”

He watched Martin’s throat move around a swallow she had not chosen.

“Yes,” Freya said.

“Say it.”

“No.”

“Say it.”

“Ask me for anything else in the world,” she said, “and you’ll have it before you finish the sentence.”

The lamp on the side table buzzed the way it did when the heating came on.

Lyle looked at Martin’s face, at the seventy-one-year-old retired actor whose evening this was, whose mouth was being used for the worst conversation of Lyle’s life, and who had asked for one condition, once, politely.

“Iris drew a picture,” Lyle said.

Freya went very quiet.

“On Saturday. After the truck. A city made of rooms with pipes instead of roads, and a blue man at the bottom.” He straightened up. “She said the rude lady told her the city loves him and chains him and every year he goes outside.”

“I told her a story.”

“You told my daughter about it before you told me.”

“She asked.”

The words were out of Freya’s mouth before she had time to hear them, and Lyle watched her hear them about a half-second too late, and watched Martin’s hand come up toward Martin’s mouth and stop.

“Right,” Lyle said.

“That isn’t what I—”

“No, it is. It’s exactly what you meant. She asked and you told her, and I didn’t, so I didn’t get told.” He picked up the thermos and put it down again for no reason. “Out.”

“Lyle.”

“Out of Martin. That’s the word. That was his condition, and he asked for it politely, and I’m using it.”

“I’m not finished.”

“That’s the point of the word.”

For one long moment she stayed.

He watched her decide. He watched a woman who had spent forty minutes explaining that she was capable of restraint stand inside a seventy-one-year-old man’s body and weigh, visibly, whether to have the last word anyway.

Then Martin’s shoulders went up two inches.

He blinked, put a hand on the back of the chair, and sat down carefully in it.

“Well,” he said, in his own voice, which was much lower. “That was awful.”

“I’m sorry.”
