“Don’t be sorry at me, I’m fine, I’ve been in worse.” Martin got his scarf. “She stayed about two seconds past the word.”

Lyle stopped. “Did she.”

“About two.” Martin wound the scarf. “It’s not nothing. You should probably know it’s not nothing.” He picked up his thermos, and at the door he turned around, because he had spent forty years learning exactly where in a room a line lands hardest. “The last one who used me like that was my wife,” he said. “She didn’t need a body to do it.”

27

THE PLACE UNDER FULTON

They did not break in.

That was the thing Lyle could never get across afterward, to anyone, in any version of the story. There was no seam-hunting, no thumb pressed into a copper hole, no clever business with the key. He parked on Kerr on a Sunday morning with his daughter in the back seat and a bag of pastries on the passenger side because he had genuinely not known what else to bring, and he walked up to a shuttered laundry and knocked on the roll-down door.

Iris was in the car because Margot had handed her back at ten and there was nowhere else for her to be. Cora was on a double. Pia did not exist yet. He had thought about it in the way he thought about things, which was to hold the thought for the length of the drive and then park.

Section 4.2 said he would not permit any person to be present with his daughter who was unknown to her mother.

He did not think about 4.2 at all until Tuesday, and when he did, he thought: she met a woman called Ruth and ate a bagel. Which was true, and which he did not put in writing, and which was the first thing he kept.

A woman in her sixties opened a personnel gate beside it, looked at him, looked at Iris, and said, “You’ll want the other entrance, love, this one’s got a step.”

Iris went in first.

Nobody stopped her.

It was not a tunnel.

He had built a tunnel in his head over four days of not sleeping, something wet and vaulted with torches. What was actually behind the laundry was a corridor with a linoleum floor, then a room that had been three rooms, then a doorway that had been a window, and then the back of a shop with the shelving still up and somebody’s bicycle chained to it.

The ceiling was a ceiling. There were light fittings.

It smelled of soup and hot metal and washing powder — kitchen weather and laundry weather married to infrastructure, the apartment’s grammar grown to a city.

“It’s just inside,” Lyle said.

“It’s mostly inside,” the woman agreed. “The bottom’s older. Watch the step here, it’s a liar.”

Her name was Ruth and she asked Iris three questions in a row without once looking at Lyle for permission, which Iris found so novel that she answered all three.

They gave him a mug.

It was the first thing that happened after the corridor and he did not understand what it meant until much later. A boy of about nineteen with a burn on his thumb put a chipped mug of tea in his hand at a folding table and said “that’s yours now” and went away again, and Lyle stood holding it, and it was three months before he found out that the mug was a document.

The woman from the stove had a pot going at the mouth of the ramp room and an argument going with a man about salt. Behind her, two children were carrying a six-foot length of copper pipe between them with enormous seriousness, one at each end, taking corners like removal men.

Laundry hung on a line strung between two ceiling pipes, above and slightly to the left of a bank of fourteen televisions.

Lyle stopped walking when he saw that.

The washing and the surveillance were sharing a room. Somebody’s socks were drying nine inches above the screen that watched the Fulton overpass. Domesticity and watching in one weather — the book already knew this marriage from a cheaper lamp.

“That can’t be good for them,” he said.

The teenager in the chair did not look up.

“It’s fine, they’re warm.”

“For the televisions.”

“They are the televisions.”

Iris was gone inside forty minutes.

He found her eventually in a room off the second corridor with a girl her own age called Pia, who had a shoebox of blue thread and a system, and who was explaining the system with the total authority of a child who has invented one.

“You do three and then you swap,” Pia said.

“Why?”

“Because otherwise it goes flat.”

“But why does it—”

“Because it goes flat, Iris.”

The thread came off a cone the size of a fire extinguisher, and the cone lived in the corridor, and Lyle had assumed for most of the morning that somebody down here had once worked in a haberdashery.

Ruth put him right on the way past.

“That’s marking thread. It’s for the line.”

“What line?”

Ruth stopped, and looked at him, and then did a thing he would think about a great deal in November: she did not answer him. She went into the room, crouched down between the two girls, and answered Iris.

“Has anybody told you about the blue?”

Iris shook her head.

“Right. So.” Ruth took a length off the cone and laid it flat on the floor between them, dead straight, like a border on a map. “Everywhere in this city, if you go far enough, you come to a bit of blue. Might be a line painted on the floor. Might be a stitch in a doormat. Might be a bit of this tied round a pipe.”

“Why?”

“Because on this side of it, the city knows you.” Ruth tapped the near side of the thread. “Water knows you, walls know you, and the man knows you, and you are as safe as anybody gets.”

She tapped the far side.

“And on that side, it doesn’t.”

Iris looked at the thread for a while.

“Is that all it is? String?”

“That’s all it is.” Ruth stood up. “Doesn’t need to be more. Everybody down here learned it at your age and nobody’s ever needed telling twice.” She was already going. “Not past the blue, love. That’s where the city stops knowing you.”

Pia had not looked up from the bracelet through any of it.

“She says that to everyone,” Pia said.

Lyle stood in the doorway.

He had come down here braced. He had spent the drive rehearsing how he would keep her close, how he would explain what to touch and what not to, how he would have her out by two.

She had been in the place for three-quarters of an hour and she had a friend and a project.

“Dad,” Iris said, without looking up. “There’s a dog.”

“Whose dog?”

“Everyone’s.”

It went on like that for most of the day, and the horror of it — the actual horror, the one that came for him at four in the morning three days later — was that it was nice.

He had wanted it to be a cult. He had wanted robes.

Instead there was a man with a clipboard doing a maintenance round, marking off valves. There was a noticeboard with a rota on it and somebody’s handwriting getting cross about the bins. There was a woman who had lived down here for thirty-one years and complained about it constantly, in detail, with pleasure, the way people complain about a marriage they intend to stay in.

There was a room with eleven chairs in it that were all different.

Lyle asked about the chairs.

“You bring your own,” Ruth said.

“From where?”

“From wherever you were before.” She shrugged. “You get here, you’ve usually got one thing. Some people have a chair. Some people have a dog. One fella turned up with a boat engine and no boat, and he’s still got it, and we’ve all had to make our peace with it.”

“And you just—”

“We find somewhere to put it. That’s the whole thing. That’s the entire thing we do.” She looked at him with a directness he was beginning to understand was the local dialect. “You’ve got a girl and half a car. We’ll find somewhere to put those too.”

Freya came with them on the second visit, in Martin, on ninety minutes and the same conditions.

She was wrong from the moment they came through the laundry.

Lyle had watched her be a dozen people. He had watched her be a nervous man at a christening and a large woman at a funeral and, once, memorably, a traffic warden. She was never wrong. Being right in a body was the thing she was for.

She walked Martin down that corridor like a woman going into a dentist she owed money to.

“You’ve gone quiet,” he said.

“I’m being polite.”

“You’ve never been polite in your life.”

At the folding table, an old man looked up from a radio he was fixing.

He looked at Martin for slightly too long.

Then he said, “Do I know you?”

“No,” Freya said.

“You’ve got a — ” He gestured vaguely at his own face. “There was a woman used to do that. With the hands.”

“Lots of people do things with their hands.”

“Not like that they don’t.” He went back to the radio. “Long time ago. Before the fountain went funny.”

Freya walked on.

Lyle looked back at the old man, who was not looking at them any more, and then at Martin’s shoulders, which were up around his ears in a way Martin’s shoulders did not go.

“What’s the fountain,” he said.

“Plumbing.”

“Freya.”

“It’s plumbing, Lyle. This place runs on water pressure and eleven hundred people’s goodwill, and one of those two is much more reliable.”

She kept walking.

Three separate people looked at Martin that day and did not place him, and one woman went white and had to sit down, and Freya walked past all of them at a woman’s pace in a man’s legs and did not turn her head once.

On the way out, at the laundry, the woman in grey coveralls was leaning against the wall with her arms folded.

She looked at Martin.

Martin looked back.

Nobody said anything at all, and the whole exchange took less than two seconds, and Lyle understood that he had just watched two people who knew each other extremely well agree not to.

28

THE PREPARATION

The city did not announce it.

There was no meeting, no bell. Lyle came down on the last Monday in October — still carrying Fulton soup-and-surveillance weather in his lungs, cracked cuff of the secondhand jacket catching the rail on the way in — and the place had simply changed gear, the way a house changes gear the week before a wedding. Laundry powder. Hot metal. The fourteen screens already watching streets he had driven with Iris in the back after handback.

Everything was about the ramp.

The ramp was a concrete incline about eleven feet wide running up from the deep chamber to a garage door on the outer wall, and for eleven months of the year it functioned as the least interesting corridor in the city. People stored things on it. There was, that October, a chest freezer on it, two bicycles, forty feet of hose, a stack of chairs, somebody’s washing machine, and a motorbike in pieces.

Clearing it took four days and produced eleven separate arguments.

“It’s always the freezer,” said the man with the clipboard.

“It’s a good freezer.”

“It’s on the ramp.”

“It’s been on the ramp since March and nobody’s said—”

“Everybody has said. Everybody has said constantly.”

Lyle carried the far end of the freezer with a man named Osman who did not speak the entire time and then, at the bottom, said, “Thanks. My back’s gone, the bastard,” in a tone of complete satisfaction, as though a prediction had come good. The jacket made him sweat. The cracked cuff darkened. Armor that could not lift a freezer either.

He learned four names that week and got two of them wrong for a fortnight.

The one he got right immediately was Vi, because she told it to him twice in the same sentence.

“Vi,” she said, putting a bowl down in front of him without being asked. “That’s Vi. You’ve been calling me love for nine days and I’ve let you because you’ve had a time of it, but it’s Vi.”

“Sorry.”

“Don’t be. Eat that while it’s hot.”

They tested the televisions on the Friday.

All fourteen, one at a time, each one taken off the frame, opened, dusted with a paintbrush, put back. The teenager — his name was Gil, Lyle learned that week, and he was seventeen and had been doing the monitoring for three years because his mother had done it before him — sat cross-legged on the floor with a multimeter and a bowl of cereal and worked through them like a man tuning an orchestra.

“That one’s dying,” he said, of a curved yellow one.

“Can you fix it?”

“No. It’s been dying for four years.” He put it back on the frame. “It’ll do one more.”

“And then what?”

Gil looked at him like the question was foreign.

“Then we get another one,” he said. “People throw them away, mate. That’s the one thing this city has never had a shortage of.”

The chains came out on the Saturday.

They were kept in a wooden crate in the deep chamber, wrapped in oiled cloth, and there was more of them than Lyle had prepared for — not one chain but a system, eleven separate runs of different gauges, each one with its own numbered plate stamped into the terminal link.

Mr. Hallow laid them out on the floor of the chamber in order.

He did it alone. Lyle offered twice and was refused twice, politely, and on the third time Mr. Hallow said, “It’s not that I don’t want the help,” and did not finish the sentence.

Lyle watched from the doorway. Hands in pockets that were not deep enough. Copper smell under the oil of the chains — the same mineral family as his key, scaled into a system that steered instead of held.

The old man went down the line on his knees with a wire brush and a rag, and he checked every link of every run with his hands rather than his eyes, and it took him five hours, and about two hours in Lyle realised he was watching a man do a thing he had done thirty-odd times and had never once been able to make quicker.

“They’re his own design,” Mr. Hallow said, at some point, without looking up.

“Sorry?”

“The chains. He drew them.” He turned a link over. “For the floodgates, originally. Then he did the sums again for this.”

“He designed his own—”

“Yes.”

“Why would he—”

“Because he could do the sums,” Mr. Hallow said, “and nobody else could, and it needed doing, and he was still the sort of man who did the thing that needed doing.” He set the run down and picked up the next. “That was a long time ago. I’d ask you not to bring it up with anyone. It upsets the ones who remember him and it makes a story for the ones who don’t.”

“Can I ask something stupid?”

“You’ve been asking something stupid for four days.”

“If the chains hold him,” Lyle said, “why open the door at all?”

Mr. Hallow put the brush down.

“Because they don’t hold him. They steer him.” He turned a link over so Lyle could see the plate stamped into it. “Number, see. Eleven runs, eleven numbers, and they go on in an order, and the order is a route. Same route his gates used to take the overflow when the river came up.”

“Overflow.”

“Pressure has to go somewhere, son. It went through his gates for thirty years and then it went through him, and now once a year it has to come off or it comes off by itself and takes the second corridor with it.” He picked the brush back up. “So we open the door and he goes round, and it drains through him and back into the water, and we chain him to the route because the route is the only part of this anybody designed on purpose.”

Lyle looked at the eleven runs laid out on the floor in order.

“So he isn’t released.”

“Nobody’s ever been released,” said Mr. Hallow. “It’s just what people call it, because a door opens.”

He worked the oil into the next link.

“I’ve heard four people this week say we let him out. We don’t let him out. We let him move, because if he doesn’t move, the pressure finds a way through eleven hundred of us instead of one.”

“And he knows the route.”

“He follows it.”

“That’s not the same thing.”

“No,” Mr. Hallow agreed, “it isn’t,” and did not offer anything else, and Lyle understood that he had reached the edge of what a careful man would say out loud about whether Elias was choosing.

“What happened to him?”

Mr. Hallow worked the brush along four links before he answered.

“He got sick. The water gets into you if you’re in it thirty years — his hands went first, then the legs. And there was a doctor.” He said the word doctor with a small pause on either side of it, the way you handle something you have only ever been handed. “Not a doctor. A woman who could do it. She fixed him.”

“Fixed him.”

“That’s the word they use.” He turned a link over. “Some of them will tell you she saved his life and some of them will tell you she should have been drowned in her own fountain, and the ones who say either loudest are the ones who weren’t born yet.”

“Which do you say?”

“I say he agreed to the first bit.” Mr. Hallow put the run down. “That’s the only part anybody agrees on. He agreed to the first bit.”

Lyle waited.

Nothing else came.

He told Freya about it that evening, in Martin, in the corridor by the folding table, and it was the first time in eight months that she did something with a borrowed body he could not read.

Martin’s hands went flat against his thighs and stayed there.

“Who told you that.”

“Mr. Hallow.”

“What else did he say.”

“That the man agreed to the first bit.”

Freya did not say anything for four full seconds, which in her was an eternity, and then she said, “He would remember it that way,” in a voice with nothing in it at all, and changed the subject to whether the soup had gone salty.

Lyle noticed.

He did not do anything with it.

Lyle asked the other question on the way back along the corridor, to Ruth, because Ruth answered things.

“How many people does he kill?”

Ruth did not slow down.

“Out there? Six, seven. It was eleven the year of the fire.” She stepped over a hose. “Some years it’s two and everybody has a lovely winter about it.”

“And nobody—”

“Nobody what, love. Nobody rings anybody?” She stopped at the stairs. “There’s no phone number for this. There’s a police force that would come down here with lamps and find eleven hundred people who aren’t on any list, and half of them would be back out on the street by Christmas, and the ones who’ve got nowhere would be the first ones out.” She started up. “So we do the ramp. And every year people who don’t know we exist pay for the fact that we do, and if you want to tell me that’s a bad arrangement I’ll agree with you and then I’ll go and get the chair out.”

The chair went out last.

Lyle had assumed the chair was a joke, or a shrine, or one of those objects that accretes meaning in a community until nobody can remember what it was for. It was a wooden kitchen chair with a rush seat and a blue cushion, and it lived the rest of the year in the corner of the room behind the stove.

On the Sunday, two women carried it up and set it at the bottom of the ramp, facing the garage door.

Then they had a twenty-minute argument about the cushion.

“It’s gone.”

“It’s not gone, it’s thin.”

“Feel it. Feel the corner. That’s not thin, that’s gone.”

“I patched that corner in — when did I patch that corner.”

“Two years ago. You patched it with the wrong blue.”

“There isn’t a right blue.”

“There’s a closer blue than that.”

They patched it again, with a slightly closer blue, sitting on the floor at the foot of the ramp with the cushion between them, and Vi held it up at the end and turned it over twice and said, “That’ll do him,” in exactly the voice Lyle’s own mother had used about a school jumper.

He had to leave the room.

He went and stood in the corridor with his hand on a pipe, and Ruth found him there four minutes later and did not ask, and brought him back a mug.

A man in his sixties came down afterwards with a ceramic cup, the kind with a chipped rim that suggested long use, and set it on the floor beside the chair, angled toward the kitchen as though somebody might sit and eat the soup. Lyle had seen cups like it in his grandmother’s house.

“He never sits,” Iris said. She had appeared beside the man without making a sound.

The man startled, then smiled down at her. “No, miss. He never has.”

“Then why do you put it out?”

“Because one year he might want to. And if he wants to, it ought to be ready.”

Iris considered this. “That’s stupid,” she said, not unkindly. “If he wanted to sit, he’d sit on the floor. He’s big enough.”

The man laughed. “You’re not wrong. But my mother put the chair out, and her mother before her. It’s a welcome, see? Even if he don’t use it.”

“Does he sit in it?”

They were on the ramp on the Monday evening. Everything was clear. The concrete had been swept and then, incomprehensibly, mopped.

Gil was checking the last of the cable runs.

“No,” he said.

“Ever?”

“Not that I’ve seen. Mum said not that she’d seen.” He coiled the cable. “There’s a bloke says he did once, in the eighties. There’s a bloke who says a lot of things.”

“Then why put it out?”

Gil straightened up and looked at the chair, and for a second the seventeen-year-old who thought the fusion was going to be incredible was visible in him a year early.

“Because if we stopped,” he said, “that’d be a decision.”

There was a young man on the fountain that week called Tomas.

He was down there most days with a wire brush and a bucket, working at the mineral crust that grew on the inside of the basin and had to come off twice a year or the circulation slowed. Lyle had assumed for a fortnight that he was maintenance. He was, in the sense that nobody paid him.

“You want to keep your hands out of it,” Tomas said, the first time. “Not because it’s dangerous. Because you’ll want to keep doing it.”

He was about twenty-four and he talked about the water the way other people talked about a football club.

He knew, or believed, that a cut washed in it healed in half the time. That the woman on the west side had gone eleven years past a diagnosis and everybody knew why and nobody said it. That there had been, in the sixties, a child who came back — not properly, not for long, but came back — and that the reason nobody would discuss this was embarrassment rather than doubt.

“Do you think that’s true?” Lyle asked him.

“I think there’s about four things it’s doing that nobody’s written down,” Tomas said, brushing. “And I think everyone down here decided a long time ago that it’s rude to ask.”

He put the brush in the bucket.

“My dad went in ninety-nine. Went in over three weeks with something in his liver, and he was on that platform for a month afterwards not going off, and everybody said isn’t the water good.” He looked at Lyle for the first time. “And I remember thinking, right, so it can hold him. So what else can it do. And nobody would tell me, and I was nine.”

On the Saturday, two women went round the whole habitable district with the cone and re-laid the thread.

They did the doorways, the head of the ramp, the mouth of the second corridor, and forty feet of the west side where somebody had lifted it in the summer to get a sofa through and had not put it back properly. It took them four hours and they argued about it constantly.

Lyle offered to help and was refused, kindly, on the grounds that he did not know where it went.

Iris was allowed to help with the soup and nothing else.

Lyle had drawn the line there and been surprised, mildly, when the city drew it in the same place without being asked. She was kept out of the deep chamber. She was kept off the ramp after the Sunday. Pia’s mother took both girls to the far side of the second corridor on the Monday and produced, from somewhere, a portable DVD player and four films.

On the Tuesday morning Iris found him at the folding table and stood next to him for a while without saying anything, which was not like her.

“Dad.”

“Yeah.”

“Are they scared of him or sad about him?”

Lyle put his tea down.

Across the room, Vi was cutting bread. Osman was carrying a crate. Two men were arguing about a valve. Somebody had put the radio on, and the radio was fixed now, and it was playing something from a long time ago, badly.

“I think both,” he said.

Iris thought about it.

“You can’t do both,” she said. “Not properly. One of them gets to be the top one.”

“That’s not—”

“Mum’s both about you,” Iris said, and picked up a piece of bread, and went off down the corridor to find Pia.

The siren went at nine minutes past eleven that night.

29

HOW IS HE

THE FAILED SEARCH

The siren did not sound like a warning.

It sounded like a machine clearing its throat before saying something it had said many times before — kettle, throat-clear, the apartment’s grammar scaled to a city that had been listening longer.

Lyle had expected a scream. Air raid. Something with urgency built into it. Instead the note came up through the floor, held for eleven seconds, and stopped, and the hidden city responded the way a household responds to a kettle.

Vi moved the soup off the burner.

Two men carried the folding chair to the bottom of the ramp and argued about whether the cushion needed replacing before or after.

A girl of about twelve went down the corridor turning off every second bulb.

Nobody ran.

Lyle found that harder to watch than panic.

He stood in the monitoring room with his hands in his jacket pockets because he could not think of anything to do with them. Fourteen televisions were stacked against the wall in a frame someone had welded out of bed rails. Most of them were smaller than a microwave. Two were curved and yellowed. One had a hairline crack across the glass that had been filled with clear epoxy and left alone.

The pictures were bad. Grey, grainy, weather-blown.

They were also, without exception, of streets Lyle had driven on.

“That’s the Fulton overpass,” he said.

The teenager in the chair did not look up. He was eating cold macaroni out of a plastic container, holding the fork like a pen.

“Second mark.”

“There’s a bagel place under there.”

“There was.”

The teenager tapped the top left screen with the fork handle.

“He comes up through the drainage. Always the same three ways in. We named them. Doesn’t mean anything, we just got tired of saying the one by the school.”

Lyle looked at the screen.

Nothing moved on it except rain.

“How do you know where he is if you can’t see him?”

“Water tells us.”

“See, that’s — that’s the kind of sentence that sounds like information.”

“It’s the one we’ve got.”

The teenager scraped the container.

“You want the honest version? We don’t know where he is. We know where he was. About four minutes ago. Then we guess. We’ve been guessing for a long time, so we’re good at it.”

Behind them, Mr. Hallow came in with a coil of chain over one shoulder and stopped when he saw Lyle.

He was maybe sixty. Narrow through the chest, thick through the hands. He had the particular tiredness of a man who has been competent for too many consecutive years.

“You should be downstairs.”

“I’d like to see it.”

“You’ll see it when he comes back.”

“I’d like to see it before.”

Mr. Hallow set the chain down.

It made a sound like coins in a pocket that would not open — false jingle, metallic promise, the transactional feel of links that steered a man.

“No,” he said. “You wouldn’t.”

They fed him.

He kept returning to it afterward. Somebody put a bowl in his hands and somebody else told him the bread was two days old but fine if you didn’t think about it, and a woman he had not been introduced to asked whether Iris had eaten, by name, having learned it that afternoon.

Iris was asleep in a stone room behind the fountain with a girl called Pia who had shown her how to make a bracelet out of blue thread.

Lyle sat on an overturned crate and ate soup while a man was killed somewhere above him.

He was fairly sure that was happening.

Nobody said so.

The conversation around him was about a valve.

He asked the question at the wrong moment, the way he asked most things.

“How is he?”

Vi looked up. She had a dish towel over her shoulder — Lyle’s kitchen language in another woman’s hands — and a burn scar along the inside of her forearm that was older than the towel by several decades.

“How is who, love?”

“Him.”

She waited.

“The man,” Lyle said. “Out there.”

“Elias.”

She said it the way you say a name. No weight on it, no pause either side, a word she had used four thousand times about somebody she had known for thirty years.

Lyle had asked Freya for that word in his own front room eleven days earlier and been told to ask for anything else in the world.

“Right,” he said.

“You’re asking how he is.”

“Yes.”

She frowned. Not offended. Working on it, the way you work on a word in another language.

“He’s out,” she said finally.

“That’s where he is.”

“That’s how he is.”

She turned back to the pot.

Lyle set the bowl down.

He tried it once more, at the ramp, with a man patching the sleeve of an enormous coat with a needle and a spool of grey thread. The coat was draped across three chairs. One sleeve was blue and did not match. The man had a pincushion strapped to his wrist and a cigarette he was not smoking parked behind his ear.

“Does it hurt him?”

The man tied off a stitch.

“Does what?”

“Any of it. The chains. The grafts. Whatever it is.”

“Couldn’t say.”

“Has anyone asked?”

The man looked at him then. Not unkindly.

“Asked him?”

“Yes.”

“Son,” he said, and went back to the sleeve. “He doesn’t come back with words.”

The blue line was a disappointment.

Lyle had built it up in his head on the walk down. He had expected something — a threshold, a mark, at minimum a difference in temperature. What he found at the end of the maintenance corridor was a steel service door standing open to the rain and a faded stripe of blue paint across the concrete just inside it, chipped, half worn away, the kind of line a city paints to tell a forklift driver where not to go.

Beyond it: an alley.

Ordinary rain falling on ordinary asphalt.

A streetlight at the corner that flickered and held.

He stood on the inside of the line for a long time.

The rules had been explained twice, in the tone people use for allergies. He does not come inside. The water knows you. Stay past the line and you are part of the thing that keeps him alive, and he will not touch you.

Nobody had explained what it meant to be on the other side of it.

Nobody needed to.

“You’ll want to not do that.”

The woman in grey coveralls was leaning against the wall behind him with her arms folded. She had been there long enough to have chosen the spot.

“You’re everywhere,” Lyle said.

“I’m employed.”

“You still owe me a door panel.”

“You still owe the woman in 4B a wall.”

Lyle turned back to the line.

“Somebody’s out there.”

“Several somebodies.”

“So we’re going to sit down here and eat soup.”

“Yes.”

“That’s it.”

“That’s it.”

She pushed off the wall.

“I’ve been where you’re standing,” she said. “About nine years ago. There was a couple in the flat above the laundry on Kerr. I knew their dog’s name. I got as far as the corner.” She rolled the blue thread on her wrist with her thumb. “I want to be clear that I’m not telling you that story so you’ll feel understood. I’m telling you so you know I already tried the thing you’re about to try, and I’m the reason we know it doesn’t work.”

“What happened at the corner?”

“Nothing,” she said. “That’s the whole point of the story. Nothing happened at the corner.”

Lyle stepped over the line.

Behind him, she did not follow.

She also did not shout.

She just stood on her side of a stripe of municipal paint, in a doorway, with her arms folded, and let him go, because the alternative was crossing it herself.

The outer city was empty in a way that had nothing to do with people being indoors.

Lyle had seen the city empty before. Four in the morning, the bad hours after a fight with Margot when he used to drive around burning gas he could not afford. That emptiness had a texture to it. Cars parked badly. A light on in one window. The sense of a place resting.

This was not that.

This was a district that had been given up on, and had learned to prefer it.

He walked north because the teenager had said fourth mark and he had understood roughly one word of that. Shopfronts stood behind roll shutters furred with rust. A dry cleaner had a sun-bleached poster in the window advertising a service in a typeface that had gone out of fashion before Iris was born. Weeds came up through a loading dock in a line so straight it suggested a seam beneath the concrete.

Half the buildings had no glass.

None of them had been demolished.

He could not stop noticing it. In any other part of the city this much vacancy would have grown scaffolding by now. Somebody would have painted a rendering of smiling people on a hoarding. There would be a sign with a phone number.

Here there was nothing. Not even graffiti, past a certain street. As though the people who tagged things had also decided this was somebody else’s.

Lyle stopped in the middle of an intersection and turned all the way around.

Rain came down through four working streetlights and about thirty dead ones.

He understood, standing there, why the hidden city had never been found.

Nobody was looking. The whole district had been quietly written off, and the reason it had been written off was walking around in it once a year with a chainsaw.

He thought: they know that.

Then he thought: of course they know that, and something in his chest went cold in a way the rain had nothing to do with.

He found the first one at eleven minutes past two.

It was a delivery scooter on its side outside a shuttered pharmacy, back wheel still turning, the insulated box still strapped to the rack. The lid had come open. Two containers had spilled and one had not. Steam was coming off the food in the cold.

There was a phone face-down in the water beside it, lit up, buzzing.

Lyle stood over it.

The screen said MUM.

He did not pick it up.

He looked around for the rest of it, for the part of the scene that would make it a scene, and there was nothing. No body. No blood he could identify in that light. Just a scooter, and food going cold, and a phone doing what phones do.

The buzzing stopped.

Then it started again.

Lyle walked away from it faster than he meant to and did not look back, and hated himself for the specific relief of not looking back.

The kid had not lived there. That was what got him later, at four in the morning, when he was finally warm. The kid had been passing through. Had taken a job that sent him into a district nobody else would take, because it paid a bit more, because nobody else would take it, and had never in his life been told there was one night a year.

Nobody had told him because nobody who could tell him would admit to knowing.

He heard it twice.

Both times from a long way off, and both times it stopped before he could fix a direction.

The first was the engine — that cough, then the catch, then a rising note that flattened out and travelled. It came off the buildings from three sides at once. He ran toward what he was certain was the correct street and arrived at a filled-in railway arch and a puddle the length of a bus.

The second time was not the engine.

It was water.

An enormous body of it moving through pipes beneath his feet, going somewhere with intention, and then a bang against metal that he felt in the soles of his shoes before he heard it. It came from a storm grating twelve feet to his left. He got down on his knees in the road and put his face near the slots and shouted into it.

“Elias!”

His voice went down and did not come back.

He shouted again.

“I’m not here to hurt you!”

Nothing.

Rain in his collar. Grit in his palms.

He heard himself say the next part before he had decided to.

“Does it hurt?”

The water below changed pitch.

It might have meant anything. It probably meant a valve had opened four streets away and pressure had done what pressure does. Lyle knelt in the road with his hands flat on a storm grating and waited for an answer from a drain, and understood, distantly, that he had become the kind of person who did that.

Nothing came.

He stayed anyway.

By four he had walked a rough circle three miles across and had crossed his own footprints twice.

He had been in an underpass, two courtyards, a car park with a collapsed roof, and the ground floor of an office building where somebody had left a rota on the wall for a week in a month he could not read.

He had found: the scooter. A front door standing open on a house with nothing in it. A dog barking behind glass, unhurt, furious. Four working streetlights. A smell near the canal he chose not to investigate.

He had not found Elias.

He had not found anyone who had found Elias.

He had not, in three miles, found a single person to warn.

That was the thing he had not budgeted for. He had left the line with his heart going, ready to argue, ready to stand in front of somebody’s door and be enormous about it. He had a speech. He had actually assembled a speech on the walk up the ramp, about how a man is not a season, and he had been looking forward to delivering it.

There was nobody to deliver it to.

The district had been emptied years ago by exactly the thing he had come out to stop, and the last few people in it were a delivery kid and a dog.

He sat down on a bollard outside a shut betting shop.

Water came off the awning behind him in a line.

“Okay,” he said, out loud, to a street.

He had come out here to find a third answer. Not the chains, not the killing. Something else. He had been so sure the third answer existed that he had never once asked what it would look like, and now he was sitting on a post at four in the morning realising he had imagined the whole thing as a conversation.

He had pictured himself finding Elias.

He had pictured himself talking to him.

That was the plan. That had been the entire plan. Locate the enormous grafted man with the industrial saw and have a word.

Lyle put his head in his hands and laughed once, and it came out wrong, and he stopped.

He got back to the alley at ten past five.

The rain had thinned. The blue line was where he had left it. The steel door was shut, and it took him a long moment to find the seam, and he had a bad thirty seconds where he genuinely believed they had sealed it.

Then it opened.

The woman in coveralls stood in it with a mug in one hand.

She looked at him.

She looked past him, at the empty alley, and did the arithmetic in about a second and a half.

“Yeah,” she said.

She stood aside.

Lyle came in over the blue line and stopped just inside it, dripping, and had to put a hand on the wall.

“There was a kid on a scooter.”

“I know.”

“You know.”

“We watched him come in off Fulton at half one. We watched him stop. We watched the box come open.” She drank from the mug. “You want me to say something that makes that all right, I don’t have it. We’ve all got a scooter. Mine’s a couple on Kerr.”

“You could have told him.”

“How.”

It was not a question. She had thought about it for nine years and had arrived somewhere.

“We tell one person, we tell the city. We tell the city, they come with machines. They come with machines, they find the corridor, they find the fountain, and then there are eleven hundred of us out on that side of the line with him.” She turned the mug in her hands. “I’m not saying it’s the right sum. I’m saying it’s the sum. Somebody did it a long time ago and we’ve all been living off the answer.”

Lyle looked down the corridor.

Amber bulbs. Somebody’s washing on a line strung between two pipes. A child’s bicycle on its side. The smell of soup that had been kept warm all night for people who would want it when this was over.

The place was beautiful.

That was the problem. He had wanted very badly for it to be sinister so that he could be sure about it.

“Come on,” the woman said. “Ramp’s in an hour. You’ll want to be standing somewhere useful.”

Iris was awake when he came down.

She was sitting up in the stone room with the blue thread bracelet around her wrist, half finished, the loose ends still hanging.

“Did you find him?”

Lyle sat on the edge of the cot.

His clothes were soaked through and he had not thought about it once in five hours and now could not think about anything else.

“No.”

Iris considered that.

“Is he okay?”

“I don’t know.”

She nodded, and looked at the bracelet, and pulled one end of it tighter.

“Nobody knows that,” she said. “I asked four people.”

Somewhere far above them, water began to move in a new direction, and the pipes along the ceiling started to knock in a rhythm that had nothing to do with Freya.

Three even blows.

Then three more.

The city stood up around them and went to open the door.

30

THE RAMP

By the time Lyle came back from the wet district, the fourth mark had already sounded. Water still on his boots. Jacket dark at the shoulders. The city had not rebooted while he was gone; soup still going, chair still waiting, Freya already in the throat of the place.

“You’re late,” Mr. Hallow said.

The chair was still waiting at the bottom of the ramp with the cup beside it. The soup was still going. Nobody had gone to bed.

Freya was there in Martin, standing where the corridor opened into the ramp room, in a coat that was not his. Lyle did not ask when she had come, or who had filled a bowl for her, or how long she had been watching the screens.

Martin’s face was usually mobile, a face that smiled at strangers and argued with prices. Now it was still in a way that looked practiced. She was staring at the ramp’s entrance, a concrete throat sloping down into black water, and her fingers were curled around a ladle she had picked up without noticing.

“Freya,” Lyle said.

She turned. Martin’s eyes focused on him with an effort that showed.

“He’s coming up,” she said.

Lyle looked for the fear.

He found it in a woman standing near the fire exit, holding a sweater closed at her throat with both hands. She was watching the screens with the fixed expression of someone who had seen what came up the ramp before and had not recovered from the specifics. When she noticed Lyle looking, she looked away.

Lyle felt Freya’s hand on his arm. Martin’s fingers were cold through his sleeve — through the cracked leather. She was staring at the screens.

The monster filled them.

He was larger than Lyle had expected, but not in the way of things that grow. He looked like something that had been built from salvage—limbs too long, shoulders hunched beneath a coat repaired so many times it was more thread than wool. The left sleeve was rewoven with blue yarn that didn’t match — the same blue family as Pia’s thread, Iris’s bracelet weather. Someone had done that by hand, carefully, as though the colour were the point.

Where the coat gaped, the seams showed. Not scars in the ordinary sense. Margins. Long clean-edged borders where one kind of tissue had been persuaded to meet another and had never entirely agreed, the older skin gone the grey of a drowned thing and the newer running through it in pale channels that did not match anything else on him.

The left side of his chest moved on a different beat from the right.

And low on that side, tucked under the ruined architecture of him like a good spoon in a drawer of bent ones, was a section of skin so fine and unmarked that Lyle looked away from it before he understood why.

He moved up the ramp on all fours, but there was a deliberation to it that Lyle hadn’t anticipated. Not a charge. A climb. He was breathing hard. His face—what Lyle could see of it beneath the matted hair and the growth that had changed the shape of his jaw—looked tired.

“He’s walking,” the teenager said. “Walking slow.”

“Thank God,” someone whispered. The woman by the fire exit.

But the others nodded with a different kind of relief. Not that he was slow, Lyle realised. That he was walking. That he was coming home in a condition where they could receive him.

The monster reached the top of the ramp.

The chains were waiting. Mr. Hallow and two younger men approached with the oil-dark links looped over their shoulders, and Lyle tensed, ready for violence, for roaring, for the chaos of a thing being subdued.

Instead, the monster stopped.

He looked at the chair. He looked at the cup.

Then he lowered his head, almost—almost—like a bow, and one of the younger men stepped forward and looped the first chain around his neck. The monster did not flinch. The man fastened it with a padlock the size of a fist, and as he did, he said something too quiet for Lyle to hear. But he saw the shape of it on the man’s lips.

Easy, sweetheart.

The second chain went around the monster’s waist. The third, his wrists. Each was applied with the same gentleness, the same efficiency, as though they were dressing him for bed rather than binding him for containment. The monster’s breathing slowed. His eyes—Lyle could see them now, pale and wet and terribly aware—moved across the room and stopped.

On Freya.

Martin’s body went rigid. The ladle in her hand trembled, tapping once, twice against her thigh. Freya’s pattern — two fast, one slow, two fast — but slower. Sadder. Rhythm as grief, not invasion, not attention. Another meaning for the same count.

The monster made a sound. It wasn’t a growl. It was lower than that, almost a hum, resonating in the concrete floor. Freya looked away.

The monster was led past the kitchen. The woman at the stove ladled soup into a bowl and set it on the floor near the chair, just in case. The monster didn’t look at it. He looked at Freya until the chains pulled him around the corner and out of sight.

The room exhaled.

Mr. Hallow coiled the excess chain with practiced loops. The teenager turned off two of the CRTs, leaving the others running for safety. The man in his sixties folded the chair and leaned it against the wall, ready for next year.

Iris took Lyle’s hand. Her palm was warm and dry.

“Dad,” she said.

“What?”

“Why does he come back if he hates it here?”

The room went quiet.

Mr. Hallow stopped coiling. The woman at the stove held her ladle motionless over the pot. The teenager looked at the floor.

Freya—still staring at the empty ramp—spoke in a voice that was barely audible.

“Maybe he doesn’t hate it,” she said.

The woman at the stove flinched. Mr. Hallow’s shoulders curled up around his ears.

Lyle looked at the chair against the wall. The cup on the table. The coat with its mismatched blue sleeve.

He looked at Freya, who was crying inside Martin’s body, using Martin’s tears because she had forgotten to stop them.

“Let’s go,” Lyle said.

But he didn’t move. He was looking at the ramp, at the dark water at its base, at the place where the monster had climbed from. Iris tugged his hand. “Dad.”

“I heard you.”

“You didn’t answer.”

Lyle looked down at his daughter. She was watching him with the same precision she had used on the fake exorcism, seeing the thing he was not saying.

“Because,” Lyle said slowly, “sometimes people come back to places that know how to hold them.”

Freya turned. Martin’s face was wet, but her eyes were Freya’s, and they held something that looked almost like envy.

“That’s not an answer,” Iris said.

“No,” Lyle agreed. “It isn’t.”

He led her out, past the kitchen where the soup was cooling in the pot, past the chair that had never been sat in, past the chains that had been oiled with love. Freya followed, her footsteps too light, as though the body she wore were already trying to float away from what she had seen.

Behind them, the CRTs flickered in the dark, watching an empty ramp, waiting for next year.

31

THAT’S WHAT SHE DOES

They talked about it for three days afterward, and none of them could stop. Digestion weather: soup rooms, stairs, folding table, the same forty seconds turned over like a coin that might still land either way.

That was what the city did in the week after. Lyle understood it eventually as a kind of digestion — eleven hundred people going over the same forty seconds from every angle, in kitchens, on the stairs, at the folding table, because forty seconds was all any of them ever got.

The forty seconds under discussion were these.

Elias had come up the ramp. He had stopped at the top, where the light changed. And before Mr. Hallow got the first run on him — before anyone touched him — he had reached up with his left hand and pressed two fingers against the seam at the base of his own throat, and held them there, and then taken them away.

“He checked himself,” said Vi.

“He always does that.”

“He does not always do that. He did that in — Ruth, when did he last do that.”

“Ninety-eight.”

“He did it in ninety-eight and he did it this year and that’s it.”

Gil, from the doorway: “It’s a pump check.”

“It’s a what?”

“Pressure, pulse, flow.” He shrugged. “It’s what you do on a line you’re not sure about. Osman does it. My mum did it.” He looked at the floor. “He was an engineer.”

The kitchen went quiet in a way that was not sad, exactly. It was the quiet of people who have been handed something they have wanted for a very long time and are afraid of dropping it.

“That’s him,” Vi said. “That’s him in there. That’s a man checking his own line.”

Ruth put her hand over her mouth.

Somebody at the back said, “Twenty-six years,” and somebody else told them to shut up, kindly.

Lyle stood at the edge of it with a mug he had not drunk from — mug as document, still, from the day they handed him one — and felt the room lift, and let it lift him, because he had been down here four days and had watched them oil a chain with love and had wanted very badly for there to be someone inside it.

Iris was under the table with Pia and a shoebox of thread.

She said, without coming out:

“No.”

Nobody heard her.

She said it again, louder, in the flat voice.

“No. That’s what she does.”

The kitchen turned around.

Iris came out from under the table because there was no version of this where she got to stay under it, and stood with a half-made bracelet in one fist, and looked at eleven adults who were all considerably more upset than she was.

“What did you say, love?” said Vi.

“That’s not him. That’s what she does.”

“Who’s she?”

“Freya.”

Lyle put the mug down.

“Iris—”

“She does it on her chest.” Iris put two fingers flat against her own collarbone, high up, at the throat. “Here. She does it when she’s checking if the person’s going to be sick, or if the throat’s going to work, because sometimes it doesn’t work and she has to know before she talks.” She dropped her hand. “She did it in Cora. She did it in Martin twice. She did it in the man with the beard at Donna’s.”

The room did not say anything.

“She does it before she talks,” Iris said. “Every time. She did it in Mom.”

October in the room. The wine, the dress, the shoulder. Iris’s inspection equipment deployed on a monster and finding Freya’s operating system. No charm left in the noticing.

Lyle got her out into the corridor.

He crouched down, leather cuff cracking at the fold, which was becoming a posture he associated with the worst information of his life.

“Iris. Listen to me. That’s a — he was an engineer, sweetheart. Gil says it’s a thing engineers do. It’s a thing he’d have done before—”

“I know what Gil says.”

“So it could be—”

“It could be both,” Iris said. “I know it could be both. That’s what I said.”

“That’s not what you said.”

“It’s what I meant.” She was getting cross now, in the specific way she got cross when adults made her do the sentence again. “I said that’s what she does. I didn’t say it’s only what she does. But they all went — ” she made a gesture that took in the kitchen, the lifting, the twenty-six years — “and nobody said the other one.”

Lyle sat down on the corridor floor.

After a moment Iris sat down too, which she only did when she had decided he was going to be there a while.

“They want it to be him,” she said.

“Yeah.”

“So they only did one of the answers.”

“Yeah.”

Iris pulled at the bracelet.

“Do you want it to be him?”

Lyle thought about lying and found he had run out of the equipment.

“Very much,” he said.

“That’s what I thought.” She turned the thread over. “That’s why I said it under the table first.”

He did not tell the kitchen.

He would think about that afterward, at intervals, for years. It would have taken eleven seconds and he had the words and he stood in the doorway with the mug and watched Vi tell Osman about the pump check with her hand on his arm, and Osman’s face doing what it did, and he went and sat down instead.

He told himself they had earned it.

He told himself the difference between he’s in there and we don’t know was a difference that made no practical alteration to anything anybody was going to do on Tuesday.

And under both of those, unexamined, in the place where he kept the address:

if the gesture was Freya’s, then the tissue was doing it.

And if the tissue was doing it, then there was no reason at all to believe the tissue could not do the rest.

He worked it into a question over the following two days, which was not a thing he was good at, and which took him two days.

It came out, finally, in the form he could not put down:

Is there anybody left inside Elias — and if there isn’t, what does that make the thing Freya wants?

Because she had told him what she wanted. She had been telling him for eight months, in car parks and kitchens and other people’s mouths. She wanted to stay. She wanted a body that did not have a timer on it and did not have to be asked and did not have somebody else lying underneath it, and every single arrangement she had ever proposed for getting one had a person in it.

Cora. Martin. Desmond. Him.

And Elias, who had been the first, and who was the only completed experiment in the world.

If Elias was still in there, then it could be done. A body could hold two, and Freya could stop borrowing, and there was a version of the next forty years in which nobody had to be emptied out to make room for her.

If Elias was gone, then the only method that had ever worked was murder with an extremely long fuse, and the woman Lyle loved was going to keep asking people for permission to perform it.

He needed somebody who had been there.

The writer had been there. The writer had written it down.

He did not tell Freya any of this. He told her he wanted to see the district, and she said the district was wet and unpleasant and full of nothing, and he said he knew, and she came anyway.

He noticed, on the way down, that she had not asked him why.

PART VI

THE WRITER

32

THE WRITER

The water in the wet district didn’t move like water anywhere else in the city.

It hung.

It waited to be disturbed, then took its time deciding whether to bother. After the ramp — after Iris said that’s what she does and the room’s lift failed — this water was not tea-dark convenience. It was infrastructure that had learned to hold a grudge.

Lyle carried the torch low, the way she’d told him to, so it wouldn’t throw their shadows high across the walls where anything watching might measure them. Jacket open. Cuff wet. No noir left in it.

“Writers love mildew,” Freya said through the mouth of the host she was wearing tonight, which was Desmond, who had done nine of these now and had agreed to this one for four hundred and a written note that it was outside the usual terms. “It’s very atmospheric. Very my sadness has a texture now.”

Desmond’s left thumb cracked.

She caught the hand with the other one and held it still, which had never once worked and did not work now; it went again nine seconds later, in the dark, under the dome, while she was mid-sentence.

“I hate this body,” Freya said, with something in it that was almost fondness.

He looked over.

“What?”

“Nothing.”

“You keep doing that.”

“Having hands?”

“Making jokes like you’re trying to get somewhere before the room does.”

She didn’t answer.

Desmond’s mouth tightened briefly in a way Desmond’s mouth had never learned to do on its own.

Then his throat worked around a swallow she hadn’t chosen.

“You okay?” he asked.

“I’m delightful.”

“I asked Desmond.”

A pause.

“He’s being paid.”

“That wasn’t the question.”

“It was the answer.”

They kept walking.

The manuscript was everywhere before it was anywhere.

Pages had been nailed flat across three hollow-core doors laid end to end as a bridge over standing water — his door’s cousins, copper edges dull under mold. Damp had gotten beneath the varnish and softened the ink into blue-black veins. Some sheets had swollen. Others had split where the nails held them too tightly.

He crouched over one page.

A paragraph had been crossed out so violently the pen tore through the paper and scarred the door beneath it.

He was, it said.

Then nothing.

Then, in smaller writing forced into the margin as though the thought had arrived late and demanded room:

someone.

He thought of sixty to fifty, of Nobody is furniture underlined once, of a letter that said incident. Paper always trying to hold a person and failing.

“She really didn’t like finishing sentences,” he said.

“No,” she answered, too quietly for the joke they had been having. “She didn’t.”

A thin sound slipped from Desmond’s throat.

Not a word. More like the beginning of one.

She cleared it away.

The third door dipped beneath his foot.

Her hand shot out and caught his sleeve before the surface cracked further.

“I told you not to step there.”

“You said the third one didn’t hold weight.”

“It doesn’t.”

“You said you were guessing.”

“I guessed correctly.”

“You’ve guessed correctly for two hours.”

She released him.

Desmond’s fingers remained hooked in his sleeve a fraction longer than she seemed to intend. Then they opened.

They found the fountain last, because the fountain didn’t want to be found first.

It occupied the centre of a flooded room with a domed ceiling that had once been somebody’s idea of beautiful. Green copper had gone black. Water sheeted down carved stone in a looping pattern that looked, from the wrong angle, almost like circulation.

Almost like something with a pulse that had forgotten how to stop.

The body was folded into the base of it.

Not laid out.

Not buried.

Integrated.

A root system had grown through the ribs and disappeared into the masonry behind them. The remains wore a dress gone the colour of everything else in the district. One hand trailed in the water, fingers curled slightly inward, and the current bent around them as though it remembered the shape.

He stopped breathing for a moment.

“Who is that?”

She said nothing.

He moved closer, careful of the water.

“Is that the writer?”

Still nothing.

He turned.

Desmond’s face had gone somewhere he didn’t recognize. Not fear exactly. Something closer to the expression people wore outside houses they had once lived in, when another family’s curtains were visible through the windows.

“We should go,” she said.

The words came too fast and too flat.

“We just found her.”

“We found a body.”

“You led me here.”

“I led you to a fountain.”

“You told me which doors wouldn’t hold my weight.”

“I have excellent instincts.”

“You knew where the stairs ended before we found them.”

“The architecture repeats.”

“You told me not to touch the blue tile because it cuts deeper than glass.”

“It does.”

“You knew there was a blue tile.”

Desmond’s shoulders rose around his ears.

A defensive movement, but too small and inward for the man whose body she was using.

She looked toward the exit.

He looked at the corpse.

“I need you to go inside her.”

“No.”

No thought before it.

No joke.

No hesitation.

He turned slowly.

“Why not?”

“That body’s been submerged.”

She spoke briskly now, borrowing the authority of someone reading from a source.

“Water breaks down connective tissue. It won’t hold a presence. The structure is compromised.”

“You can tell that from here?”

“The chest will be open.”

His eyes moved to the body.

Most of the torso remained obscured by vines, dress, and shadow.

She kept talking.

“The left side first. The sternum would separate under pressure, then the ribs. There won’t be enough resistance to keep me gathered. I’d disperse. You’d be standing here with Desmond and two bodies and no useful answers.”

Her breath had shortened.

Or Desmond’s had.

“The left side,” he repeated.

She stopped.

“You said the left side.”

“I said probably.”

“No. You didn’t.”

Desmond’s right hand lifted.

Slowly.

Carefully.

It reached toward the corpse.

Not for the face.

Not in fear.

The hand straightened the ruined collar of the dress. Tucked a strip of hair away from the throat. Then two fingers moved to the wrist and pressed lightly against the pale indentation where a ring had once rested.

She caught herself.

The hand jerked back.

Desmond made a soft sound behind her teeth.

“That wasn’t him,” Lyle said.

She rubbed the borrowed fingers against Desmond’s trousers as though wiping something from them.

“Bodies imitate.”

“No.”

“They do. Muscle memory. Suggestion.”

“You’ve never touched her before.”

She looked at him.

Desmond’s eyes flickered.

For one second, the fear inside them belonged entirely to Desmond.

Then she covered him again.

“The body won’t take me,” she said.

The clinical language was beginning to fray.

“The chest is open. The graft sites won’t hold. The water got under the dress. There’s damage along the shoulder and the lower abdomen and the right hand is missing two nails and there’s no support beneath the…”

She stopped.

The fountain continued running.

He stared at her.

“You haven’t uncovered any of that.”

She said nothing.

“You haven’t looked.”

Nothing.

“What graft sites?”

Desmond’s jaw pulled sharply to one side.

She reached up to hold it in place.

His other hand struck the fountain rim.

“Stop,” she whispered.

The word seemed aimed inward.

Desmond’s knees bent.

He caught himself, then failed to.

She forced him upright.

His face contorted around an expression she couldn’t smooth away.

“I can feel him,” she said, to nobody in the room.

Desmond’s voice pushed through beneath hers.

“Something’s wrong.”

His own voice was thinner than expected. Frightened. Breathless.

“She’s slipping.”

He rushed forward and caught Desmond beneath the arms.

The host’s skin felt feverish through his shirt.

“Come back to me,” he said to her. “Use me.”

Desmond’s eyes found his.

Two people appeared to be looking through them.

“Take me instead.”

Her attention fixed on him.

For one dangerous second, he felt the decision arrive.

Desmond’s hand lifted toward his face.

The fingers opened.

He did not move away.

Then her gaze shifted past him.

To the fountain.

To the body folded into its foundations.

Her hand stopped inches from his cheek.

Desmond gasped.

She looked back at him once.

He could see the temptation there, and the shame of it, and something even worse: relief that he had offered.

Then she turned Desmond’s head toward the corpse.

“No,” she said.

This time the word was for herself.

Desmond collapsed.

Lyle lowered him onto the wet stone. The host curled immediately onto his side, coughing, both hands clutching his own chest as though checking whether it still belonged to him.

For the first time all night, Desmond was alone inside his skin.

The corpse’s hand closed beneath the water.

No lightning.

No rising wind.

No voice from the pipes.

Just a hand closing the way a hand closes when someone decides to hold onto something.

The dead fingers tightened around nothing.

The wrist turned.

The head lifted several inches and fell sideways before the neck found a way to support it.

Water came out of her when she moved. Not from the mouth. From everywhere — the sleeve, the hem, the collar, places that were not openings — running out of the dress in thin unhurried streams and going back down into the basin it had come from.

And the smell arrived, which it had not done while she was lying still.

It was not rot. Rot would have meant something in her had been allowed to finish. This was mineral and green and very cold: the inside of a pipe, standing water in a stone room, the smell of a thing that had been kept.

Her eyes opened.

They did not find him.

They found the ceiling first.

The blackened copper dome.

The water crawling down the stone.

The angle of the room.

Her gaze moved slowly, taking inventory.

The ruined hand left the water and touched the ribs, then the hollow beneath the breastbone. Fingers paused at one of the missing sections as though discovering a door where a wall used to be.

Her mouth opened.

No sound came.

She adjusted her jaw with both hands.

The movement was practiced.

Lyle had watched her do it in Harold, and in Jared, and in Cora at a folding table in a restaurant basement, and once in the front seat of a car with the window down.

Her tongue worked behind teeth that had shifted in death.

She took a breath her lungs could not properly hold.

The first noise was wet and mechanical.

The second almost became a word.

Then her eyes found the hand.

The bare ring finger.

“They moved my rings.”

The sentence emerged in fragments, dragged through a throat that resisted every syllable.

But the cadence beneath the damage was hers.

The same irritation.

The same small fury reserved for violations other people would call insignificant.

He knew her before he allowed himself to know.

His body moved toward her first.

One foot splashed into the fountain water. His hand lifted as though to touch her face.

Then he stopped.

The movement broke in the middle.

Recognition pulled him forward.

Everything else drove him back.

He looked toward Desmond, shaking and soaked beside the fountain.

Back to the corpse.

He remembered every joke she had made on the walk down.

Every moment she had pretended to speculate.

Every correction.

Every turn chosen before the corridor appeared.

His raised hand curled shut.

“You knew.”

The corpse watched him.

“You brought me here.”

Her head tilted.

A familiar gesture performed by anatomy that could no longer make it graceful.

“You let me look for you.”

Her eyes lowered, and stayed down.

His torch lay beneath the water near the fountain’s edge, still burning weakly. He reached down and pulled it free.

The beam shook across her dress.

Across vines.

Across the torso.

The damage was not all decay.

There were old margins in the body.

Clean decisions softened by time.

Sections removed in deliberate shapes.

A wide absence along the left side. Smaller omissions near the shoulder, abdomen, and thigh. Old cuts that had healed before death, unlike everything the water had done afterward.

He followed one with the light.

The corpse flinched.

Not from pain.

From being seen.

Behind her, half hidden beneath hanging vines, a hollow-core door had been fixed horizontally against the wall. Something had been scratched into its surface.

The torch passed over it.

A human outline.

Front and back.

Small numbered sections marked along the body.

Several corresponded to the absences in front of him.

And in the margin, drawn twice.

Once at the top, small and exact, beside a column of numbered runs — a two-pronged instrument with a threaded stem, in the flat hand of a man drawing a part he had machined himself. Under it, in the same hand: G7 IDX.

Once at the bottom, larger, laid lengthwise across a diagram of a throat. Different pen. Different hand entirely, faster, with the pressure of somebody working out an approach rather than recording a design.

Under that one it said: entry, second attempt.

Lyle put his own hand into his coat pocket, where the thing had been sitting in a dish towel for eight months, and did not take it out.

He saw it.

He did not understand it yet.

His gaze returned to her.

The woman he loved was occupying the corpse of the woman he had come to investigate.

The distinction collapsed so completely he felt stupid for ever believing there had been two of them.

“He made this.” Lyle’s voice had gone somewhere strange. “It’s got his numbering on it.”

“Seventh gate,” she said. “It indexed the seventh gate.”

“And you used it for—”

He stopped, because the second drawing was eighteen inches from his face and answered the question.

“Say it anyway,” Freya said.

“It’s the reason he could talk afterwards.”

“Yes.”

“And he’d already said no.”

Water came off the dome somewhere behind them and hit the standing water and kept going.

“Yes,” she said.

“You’re the writer.”

She looked down at her ruined hands.

At the wrist where the rings had been.

At the missing parts she had not yet explained.

“I was.”

He recoiled at the tense more than the body.

His heel struck the edge of one of the hollow-core doors. It boomed beneath him, an empty sound pretending to be solid.

She reached toward him.

The arm moved badly.

He nearly reached back.

Even now, after the lies had acquired a face, his body still recognized the beginning of her gesture.

He hated the relief that came with finally seeing her.

He hated that the relief survived everything else.

Her hand fell before it reached him.

Water escaped the corner of her mouth and traveled down her cheek in the path a tear might have taken.

“I was hoping,” she said, “you’d meet me first.”

33

WHAT SHE CAN CARRY

Lyle turned toward the corridor.

After Iris’s equipment had named Freya in Elias’s gesture — after the hollow-core manuscript bridge — the fountain’s loop was no longer wonder. Mechanical circulation. Care as plumbing. Bodies as route. The water kept its loop. Behind him, he heard her shift against the stone.

“Don’t,” she said.

The word came out thick, as if her tongue were still learning the shape of a mouth that had been dead. He didn’t stop.

He made it three steps before the splash.

Not a fall exactly. A failure of architecture. The corpse’s knee buckled in a direction knees don’t bend, and the body went down hard against the shallow water at the fountain’s edge. The sound was wrong—too hollow, too much like something dropped rather than someone falling.

He stopped.

He told himself to keep walking.

Then he was beside her, his hands under her arms, lifting. The dress was wet and cold and heavier than fabric should be. He felt the give of the left side where the ribs were compromised, the way his fingers sank into an absence that had been carved out long before the water got in.

She tried to help and only made it worse. Her hands clawed at his shoulders with the coordination of a puppet operated by someone reading instructions from a great distance.

“I don’t need—” she started.

“Stop talking.”

He got her upright. The body swayed. Jacket wet at the chest where he had braced her. Cracked cuff black with fountain water. No noir. Just load-bearing.

“You said it couldn’t hold you.”

“It can’t.”

“You’re in it.”

“I’m not in it.” She reset the jaw with both hands. “A living body holds me. It has tension. It pushes back. This one has nothing to hold with — I’d go through it like water through a sieve.”

“Then what is this.”

“The fountain.” She looked back down the corridor, at the black doorway they had come out of. “The water’s been running through her for twenty-six years. It knows the shape. It’s holding her up, and it’s holding me in her, and it is doing both at a distance it was never built for.”

“How far does it reach?”

“I don’t know. Nobody has ever done this.” Something in the ruined face was almost amused. “I am, at present, plumbing. Do you know how humiliating that is.”

He knew. The city ran on that humiliation — kindness with a shadow, holding bodies up so the system could keep using them.

Whatever the fountain was lending her, it was being withdrawn on a schedule neither of them controlled. He kept one hand at her back, not touching her so much as propping her, the way you might steady a door before deciding whether to walk through it or close it.

Her head lolled forward, then jerked up with a visible effort of will. “You were leaving,” she said.

“Yes.”

“I can’t walk out of here alone.”

“I know.”

He said it flatly, without cruelty, which made it crueler. He began moving toward the archway, keeping his pace slow enough that she could follow if she chose to, fast enough that she would have to work for it.

She followed.

The first dozen steps were a negotiation between will and decay. Her left foot dragged. Her right shoulder dipped to compensate, then failed. She caught herself against a wall and left a wet print that looked less like a hand and more like a stain.

“Were you married to him?”

She did not answer for four steps.

“No.”

“There were two heights marked on a doorframe back there.”

“That was his sister. She was nine and I was eleven and neither of us has been that height in a very long time.” The bad foot dragged. “He wasn’t my husband, Lyle. He was the first person who believed me when I said I could save him.”

Lyle waited.

“And the last person who believed I would stop.”

They went about twenty feet without either of them saying anything.

“The woman you knew,” she said, breathing in shallow bursts her lungs couldn’t hold properly, “didn’t lie to you.”

He didn’t look back.

“She knew where the body was.”

Silence behind him. Then the wet slap of her foot against stone, continuing.

“That isn’t the same thing.”

“It is exactly the same thing.”

He reached the stairs. The architecture repeated, she had said earlier, and now he saw how: the same blue tile, the same hollow-core doors laid across standing water, the same mildew blooming in patterns that looked almost deliberate. He waited at the first step until she caught up, then offered his arm without looking at her.

She took it.

Her grip was wrong. Too tight in some places, too loose in others, as though she were operating the hand by remote and the signal kept cutting out.

“She made the jokes,” she said. “She corrected your footing. She was the one who—”

“Stop.”

He halted on the second door-bridge. The water beneath them was black and still.

“You’re doing it again. Dividing yourself into before and after. The woman I knew and the writer who did… whatever this is.” He gestured at the corridor, at the doors, at her body without looking at it. “But it’s the same voice. The same irritation when I step somewhere you told me not to. The same—” He caught himself. His hand was shaking. “You stood beside me and listened to me miss you.”

She stopped.

The corpse’s chest rose and fell in a rhythm that served no physiological purpose. Water escaped the corner of her mouth and traveled down her cheek.

“I was still me,” she said quietly.

“That’s worse.”

“I was hoping—”

“I don’t care what you were hoping.”

But he did. That was the problem. He cared so much that his caring felt like another room she had led him into, one with no exit.

They crossed the second door. Her weight against his arm increased. The graft scars—he could feel them now, even through the dress, ridges of old tissue beneath his palm where something had been removed and something else had been put in its place. He didn’t ask. He refused to ask.

At the third door, she stumbled again.

This time he was ready. He caught her around the waist and felt the absence in her left side press against his forearm, the hollow where a rib should have been, the space that felt designed rather than damaged. A door where a wall used to be. He remembered the diagram scratched into the wood behind the fountain.

She felt him feel it.

Her hand moved to cover the spot, but he was already withdrawing, already resetting his grip higher, on her shoulder, where the bone was intact.

“You don’t have to help me,” she said.

“Yes, I do.”

“Because you forgive me?”

“Because if I leave you here, you’ll turn it into another story. Another manuscript. You’ll write yourself as the abandoned woman in the wet district, and you’ll believe it by morning.”

Her ruined hand found his sleeve. The fingers that were missing two nails hooked into the fabric with a desperation that felt familiar.

“I wouldn’t write that,” she said.

“You write everything.”

They reached the surface. The air was colder above, or maybe he was just noticing the difference. The street was empty, the wet district’s peculiar silence intact. He looked for a cab, a light, anything normal, and found nothing.

She stood beside him, swaying slightly, her hand still in his sleeve.

“I was trying to survive,” she said.

“So was I.”

“Not the same way.”

“No,” he agreed. “Not the same way.”

He began walking again, toward the edge of the district where the streetlights worked and the water moved like water should. She kept pace, barely, her breath coming in wet mechanical sounds that were not quite human.

He didn’t let go of her.

He didn’t forgive her.

He just kept moving, one foot in front of the other, pulling her along with him.

34

MINE

They did not make it out.

Fifty yards short of the district’s edge, where the streetlights started working and the water began behaving like water — ordinary path promised and withheld — the left knee went again and did not come back.

Lyle felt it happen through his arm. Not a stumble. A structural decision.

“Okay,” he said. “Okay.”

“Don’t.”

“Don’t what.”

“Say okay twice. You do that when you’re about to be practical at me.”

He got her against a wall.

The rain had stopped an hour ago and the wet district had not noticed. Water still came off every ledge in the same unhurried way, as though the weather here was a habit rather than an event. Household routine of catastrophe. Kettle logic.

Freya stood propped on the brick with her head at an angle her neck had chosen rather than she had, and looked down at the leg.

“Well,” she said. “That’s humiliating.”

“It’s a knee.”

“It’s my knee, and it’s gone in a direction I specifically remember it not going.”

She tried to laugh.

The sound came out wet and stopped halfway, and she had to reach up and put her jaw back where it belonged with both hands, and the click of it was very loud in the empty street.

Lyle looked away while she did it.

He hated that he looked away. He had made a whole personality out of not looking away.

“There’s a room,” she said.

“We’re forty yards from a bus route.”

“There’s a room, Lyle.”

“You need a hospital.”

She turned her head to look at him, and the ruined mechanism of it took long enough that the look arrived like a punchline.

“Do I.”

The room was up two flights of iron stairs above the flood line, behind a door that had been a fire door once and had been taken off its hinges and put back on the other way round so it opened outward.

He had to carry her.

She weighed almost nothing and it was the worst thing he had ever lifted. Things shifted inside her that should have been anchored. His forearm found the absence in her left side and stayed there because there was nowhere else to put it, and she felt him find it, and neither of them said anything about it for the entire flight.

At the top she said, “Left,” and then, “no, my left,” and then, quietly, “sorry.”

He put his shoulder to the door.

Inside was a small dry room with a desk.

That was all. A desk under a boarded window, a chair with a cracked seat, a mattress on a frame with the ticking gone grey, a paraffin lamp with an inch of fuel, and every vertical surface covered in doors.

Not doorways. Doors. Taken off other rooms and stood against the walls like paintings, and written on — the manuscript bridge’s private library. Numbers, arrows, outlines. A woman’s body drawn front and back, sectioned, annotated in a hand that got smaller as it went down the page and angrier as it went along.

Lyle lit the lamp because his hands wanted something to do. Mineral stain under the nails. The jacket steaming faintly in the paraffin heat.

Then he stood in the middle of her room holding a match that had gone out, looking at a wall of diagrams of the body currently sitting on the mattress behind him.

“You wrote here.”

“I wrote everywhere. I lived here.” A pause. “Badly. There’s a jar under the desk you should not open.”

He didn’t turn around.

“How long.”

“Four years. Give or take. I was very productive and extremely unwell.” Fabric moved. “Lyle.”

“What.”

“Turn around.”

“In a minute.”

“That’s the second-worst thing you’ve ever said to me.”

He turned around.
