She had got herself sitting up against the wall with her legs out in front of her, the bad one arranged by hand into an approximation of the good one, and she was looking at her own arms in the lamplight with an expression he had never seen on any face she had ever borrowed.

Wonder was the wrong word. It was closer to a woman finding her own coat in a lost property office after a year.

“Oh,” she said.

She turned one hand over.

“Oh.”

“Freya—”

“Shut up. Shut up, I’m doing something.”

She touched her own face.

Both hands, flat, the way you check whether you have a fever, and then slower, finding the shape of the cheekbone under the pad of the thumb, and then the jaw, and then the small hard ridge at the temple that she had apparently been looking for, because when she found it she made a sound.

“I have a scar,” she said. “I have this scar. I fell off a wall when I was nine and I have this — it’s still — ” She pressed it. “It’s still here. It’s still here.”

“It’s still there.”

“You don’t understand what I’m telling you.”

“I know.”

“Two hundred and eleven bodies, Lyle.” Her fingers went along the eyebrow, into the hair, and stopped where the hair had gone wrong. “Two hundred and eleven and not one of them had this.”

Her hands kept going.

He watched her take inventory of herself and understood, slowly, that she was not examining a corpse. She was going through the pockets of something she had been told was lost.

The throat. The notch at the base of it.

The collarbone, and a small satisfied noise at the collarbone.

The long incision down the sternum, which she followed with two fingers like a woman reading a receipt she intended to dispute.

“I did a terrible job.”

“You—”

“No, look at it. Look at the closure.” She pulled the edge of the dress back to show him and he wished very much that she hadn’t. “That is the work of a woman doing her own chest at four in the morning with one lung already gone.”

“Freya.”

“I’m allowed to be a critic. It’s my bastard sternum.”

She got the dress open at the front.

Lyle turned his head before he had decided to.

“Oh, now you’re shy.”

“I’m being—”

“You have caught me in Cora’s body four times and carried me twice, and you’re going to be a gentleman about my tits.”

“That’s not—”

“Look at them.”

“Freya.”

“Look at them, because I want to say something rude about them and I need a witness.”

He looked.

She looked down at herself at the same time, and whatever she had been about to say did not arrive.

The water had been in her for a long time. The left side was mostly the wide clean-edged absence he had followed with the torch an hour ago, and the skin over what remained had gone the colour of the inside of a shell. There was a delicacy to it that was worse than damage. She had been a woman who was fond of her own body — he could hear it in every joke she had ever made — and the thing she was sitting in had been beautiful and had been taken apart deliberately, in numbered sections, by her.

She put one hand flat over the absence.

“I was going to say they used to be better,” she said.

“Yeah.”

“That was the joke. That was the whole joke, and now I’ve ruined it by looking.”

She left the hand there.

“That’s me,” she said.

Not to him.

“Come here.”

“Freya.”

“Come here.”

He came and sat on the edge of the mattress, and the frame took his weight with a complaint, and for a moment they were two people sitting on a bed in a small room with a lamp, and it was almost survivable.

She put her hand on his knee.

The fingers were cold and slightly wrong and two of them had no nails. She tapped them once against his knee — two fast, one slow, two fast — which she had done to him through six different sets of hands and had never once had to explain.

“You told me,” she said.

“Don’t.”

“You told me it wasn’t the face. You told me in the car outside Donna’s with a bag of frozen peas on your eye. You said, it’s not the face, it’s the thing behind the face.”

“I know what I said.”

“I have thought about that sentence more than you have thought about anything in your life.”

“That’s probably true.”

“Don’t be charming, I’m negotiating.”

She moved her hand up his thigh.

He did not move.

That was the first failure, and both of them clocked it, and neither of them said so.

“I have been in twelve bodies you found attractive,” Freya said. Her voice had dropped into the register she used for the good jokes and the bad news. “I have been in a man you found so attractive it made you rude for a week. I have been in the woman you married. And in every single one of them, you touched me like you meant it.”

“That’s different.”

“Say why.”

“You know why.”

“I want to hear you build the sentence.”

He didn’t.

She waited.

She was very good at waiting. She had been waiting for years in a fountain.

“I want you to touch me,” she said. “In mine. Once.”

“Freya—”

“Once, and then I’ll never ask again, and I mean that, which should frighten you.”

He tried.

He wants it recorded, in whatever place these things are recorded, that he tried.

He put his hand on her face first, because that was the part of her he had wanted to hold for eight months and had never once been able to. The skin gave under his palm in a way skin does not. It was cool, and slightly slack, and there was a give at the cheek that told him something under it was no longer attached.

He kept his hand there.

“There you are,” she said.

Her eyes closed.

That nearly did it. That nearly carried him the whole way, because the way she said it was so exactly, so unbearably her — the drop on the second word, the small victory in it — that for four or five seconds Lyle was not in a room with a corpse. He was in a kitchen at two in the afternoon being told he had made the coffee wrong.

He leaned in and kissed her.

Her mouth was cold and did not close properly on one side and tasted of the fountain, of copper and stone and old still water, and something moved under his lip that should not have moved.

He kept going.

He got his hand into her hair and her hair came away.

Not all of it. A handful, from behind the ear, with a small sound like a seam.

Lyle made a noise into her mouth.

She heard it.

Of course she heard it. She was two inches away and she had spent her entire existence, living and dead, reading the exact moment a person decided something about her.

“Keep going,” she said.

“I’m going to.”

“You’re allowed to—”

“I’m going to.”

He put his hand on her chest.

And his body — his stupid, loyal, honest body, which had followed him through eight months of this without once filing a complaint — went cold from the sternum outward, and his stomach came up.

He got about a yard.

He was sick into the corner of her room, on his knees, with one hand flat on the boards, and it went on longer than he wanted, and the whole time the only sound behind him was the very slight rasp of a woman who could not breathe properly trying to be quiet about it.

When he was done he stayed there.

The floor was cold. The lamp had started to gutter.

“Well,” Freya said, “that’s the nicest thing anyone’s done for me in a decade.”

“Don’t.”

“No, genuinely. Most men wait until they get home.”

“Freya, don’t.”

“What would you like me to do instead?”

He turned around and sat with his back against the wall.

She had closed the dress. That was the thing that got him, later, at four in the morning and at intervals for the rest of his life — that while he was being sick in the corner she had used the time to do up the front of her dress.

“I’m sorry,” he said.

“I know.”

“That wasn’t about—”

“I know what it was about.” She was looking at the ceiling. “It was about your body telling the truth faster than you could get to it.”

“That’s not the truth.”

“It’s a truth. It’s the one with a stomach.”

He wiped his mouth with the back of his wrist.

“It’s still me,” she said.

“I know it is.”

“Do you.”

“Yes.”

“Then what was that.”

“I thought—” He stopped and made himself say it properly, because she was owed at least the effort. “I thought it would be different if you were in there. I thought if it was actually you, then the rest of it wouldn’t — I thought that would be the part that mattered.”

“I am in here.”

“I know.”

“So the theory was wrong.”

“The theory was wrong.”

She laughed. It was a bad sound and she let it be a bad sound.

“Eight months of it’s not the face,” she said. “Eight months. And it turns out what you meant was: it’s not the face as long as the face is warm.”

“That’s not fair.”

“Isn’t it? You touched every body I stole.” She turned her head toward him. “Every one. Some of them I hadn’t even asked properly. You touched Cora when Cora had said one hour and we were at ninety minutes and you knew, and you touched her anyway, and you can’t touch mine.”

“They were alive.”

She did not answer for long enough that he could hear the lamp.

“So that’s what you loved,” she said.

He opened his mouth.

There was nothing in it.

Because she was not entirely right — he knew she was not entirely right, he could feel the shape of the counter-argument sitting somewhere behind his teeth, something about how a living body isn’t a preference, it’s a precondition, the way air is —

— and she was not entirely wrong either, because he had in fact spent eight months being extremely relaxed about whose skin he had his hands on, and had discovered his principles at precisely the moment the skin stopped being someone else’s.

“I don’t know how to answer that,” he said.

“That’s the most honest thing you’ve managed all night.”

“Tell me I’m beautiful.”

“Freya.”

“You don’t have to mean it. I want to hear you do it. I want to hear what it sounds like when you try.”

“That’s a horrible thing to ask me.”

“Yes,” she agreed. “It’s been a horrible night. I’m having a horrible time. Do it.”

He looked at her.

He looked at her for long enough that it stopped being an attempt and started being a study — the collapsed left side, the hair gone from behind one ear, the jaw sitting a half-degree off true, the hand with two nails missing lying palm-up on the mattress the way it had lain palm-up in the fountain, the water still coming out of the corner of her mouth in a slow line down her cheek where a tear would have gone if she had any left to make.

“You’re beautiful,” he said.

It came out flat as a receipt.

They both heard it.

“Ah,” Freya said.

“Let me—”

“No, no. Don’t do the second take. The second take would be so much worse.”

She looked away.

And then she tried to cry.

He watched her try. He watched her face do the whole thing, the entire familiar sequence he had seen on six other women’s faces when she was inside them: the tightening at the bridge of the nose, the eyes going hot and quick, the small furious jerk of the chin she did when she had decided not to let it happen and was losing.

Everything worked except the part that produced tears.

The muscle around her left eye pulled, and kept pulling, and something in the tissue there gave with a sound so small he only knew it had happened because she stopped.

She put two fingers up and touched the place.

Looked at them.

“Right,” she said.

“I have to go.”

“Freya—”

“Not — ” She made a gesture. “Not go. Out. Of this. It’s stopped holding. It’s been going for about ten minutes and I’ve been ignoring it because I’m having such a lovely evening.”

“How long.”

“Minutes.”

He got up onto the mattress beside her.

“Don’t sit next to it,” she said. “Don’t do that, it’s grotesque.”

“I’m sitting next to you.”

“You’re sitting next to it, Lyle, we’ve established at some length tonight that those are different.”

“I don’t accept that.”

“You were sick in the corner.”

“I know what I did.” He put his hand over hers. Cold. Wrong. Hers. “I’m still here.”

She was quiet.

Then: “You are still here. That’s the — ” Her voice did something. “That’s the annoying part. You could have left at the fountain. Everyone would have understood. I’d have written you very sympathetically.”

“I know you would.”

“I’d have made you taller.”

“I’d have preferred that.”

Her fingers turned under his and got hold of him, badly, too tight in one place and not at all in another.

“It was never going to work, was it,” she said. “This. Being in it.”

“No.”

“You could lie.”

“You’d know.”

“I’d allow it.”

“You wouldn’t.”

“No,” she admitted. “I wouldn’t.”

She looked around the room. The desk. The doors. The lamp with about six minutes in it.

“It’s not a home,” she said. “That’s what I wanted to see. Whether it was still a home.” She let out something that was not a breath because she had no way to make one. “It’s a room I used to be in. That’s all it is. It’s got my handwriting on the walls and a jar under the desk and it isn’t mine any more, it’s just — evidence.”

“Freya.”

“Don’t you dare be kind to me right now.”

“I wasn’t going to be.”

“Good.”

“I was going to say you have to get out of it before it stops working, because if you’re still in there when it goes I don’t know what happens to you and neither do you.”

She laughed properly then, and it did not matter that the sound was ruined.

“There he is,” she said. “There’s the man who followed a truck.”

He kissed her again before she left it.

He never told anyone about that, not Cora, not Iris, not the woman in the coveralls who asked him one careful question three days later and let it go when he did not answer.

He kissed her again, and it was exactly as bad as the first time — colder, if anything, because the room had cooled and so had she — and he did not close his eyes and he did not pretend and his body did not change its mind about a single thing.

And he did it anyway, on purpose, for a while.

Because the first one had been an experiment, and this one was not.

“You didn’t want that,” she said, against his mouth.

“No.”

“Then why.”

“Because you asked me for something tonight and I gave you a receipt.”

Her hand came up and rested on the back of his neck, and did not grip, and had almost no strength in it at all.

“I’m going to go,” she said.

“Yeah.”

“I want you to not watch.”

“I’m going to watch.”

“Lyle—”

“You’ve spent eight months being invisible to me. I’m not doing that to you in the one body you’ve got.”

She was quiet for a second.

“That’s the nicest thing you’ve ever said to me,” she said, “and I’d like it on record that I hate it.”

Then she said, “Don’t touch me while I do this, it’ll go through your hand and it’s unpleasant.”

He took his hand off her.

He sat on the edge of the mattress in a dry room above the wet district with his hands on his knees, and watched.

It took about four seconds.

The body did not slump dramatically. It settled. The tension that had been holding the shoulders in a shape went out of them, and the jaw came off true by another degree, and the palm-up hand relaxed the last of the way open, and the whole arrangement became furniture in a way that was instant and total and impossible to mistake for anything else.

The water at the corner of the mouth kept going.

That was all.

Lyle sat with her for a while.

Then he stood up and did the only useful thing available to him, which was to straighten her dress, and put the hair back from her face on the side where there was still hair, and close her eyes with his thumb, and blow out the lamp before it could go out on its own.

The room was black.

Somewhere below, the fountain kept running.

He said, to a dark room, in a completely ordinary voice:

“It’s still you.”

Nothing answered, because nothing could.

He went down the iron stairs and out through the wet district and did not stop until he reached a street where the water ran downhill.

35

THREE NIGHTS

The wall came down on the Tuesday.

Not all of it. A section of the kitchen wall about the size of a door — hollow-core sized, of course — which had been damp since August and swollen since October and which Lyle had been managing with a dehumidifier borrowed from Donna and a policy of not looking at it. After Freya-as-plumbing, after the room of annotated doors, the apartment finally spoke the city’s language out loud.

He was at the sink when it went. There was a sound like a wet cough behind the plaster and then the paper bulged and split from the skirting to the height of his chest, and what came out was not clean.

It smelled of copper and stone.

He stood in his kitchen with a plate in his hand for the second time that year and understood, with total clarity and about six months late, where all the water he had ever prepared had been going. Ordinary path and secret path had always been the same pipes.

The building manager came at four with a torch and a man from the council.

They were in the flat for eleven minutes. Lyle followed them around saying the words dehumidifier and temporary and I’ve been on to the landlord about it, and the man from the council wrote things down and did not look at him, and at the door he said the word uninhabitable in the tone of a man who used it four times a week.

“How long?”

“That’s not really—”

“Roughly.”

“Six weeks if he moves on it.” The man looked back down the hall at the split in the wall. “He won’t move on it.”

There was a notice on the door by five.

Cora offered the sofa.

She offered it before he asked, in the car park behind Bellini’s, still in the apron, on a fifteen-minute break she had taken in four separate pieces that day.

“It’s one room, Lyle, I’m not pretending it’s a solution. But it’s a room, and Iris knows it, and I’m out most of the time anyway.”

“You work two jobs.”

“I’m aware.”

“Where would she sleep?”

“With me. I’d take the sofa.”

And that was true, and it was a genuinely bad arrangement for a woman who had been awake since five, and Lyle said so, and everything he said was accurate.

He did not say the other thing, which was that Cora’s address was on a form in Margot’s solicitor’s file, and that a bowl of prepared water on Cora’s kitchen counter would be a thing Cora had to agree to, every single time, in writing, with terms. Kindness with a shadow. Paper still trying to govern desire and access.

“Let me think about it,” he said.

“That’s a no.”

“It’s a think about it.”

Cora looked at him for a second and then went back inside to finish her shift.

Ruth gave them the two rooms off the second corridor.

She did it the way the city did everything, which was without ceremony and slightly faster than was comfortable. Lyle came down at eight on the Tuesday night with two bags and a child and a story he had prepared about the wall, and got about a third of the way through it before Ruth said, “Right, well, there’s the two by the stairs, they’re dry.”

“I can pay something.”

“You can help Osman with the pipe on Thursday.”

“That’s not—”

“It is, though,” said Ruth, and went to find sheets before he could argue about it.

The rooms were dry and warm and about eleven feet square.

The heat came off a pipe that ran across the ceiling, and the light was a bulb in a cage, and there was a window that had been a window into somewhere else before somebody sealed the somewhere else and put a mirror in it, which Iris thought was the best thing she had ever seen.

She slept eleven hours.

He could not get around it afterward, when he was getting around a great many things. She had been waking at two most nights since September. She had been waking, coming to his doorway, standing there.

The first night underground she went down at nine and did not move until eight in the morning, and when he checked on her at one and again at four she was on her back with both arms above her head like somebody who had put something down.

The three nights were good.

That is the honest account and there is no version of it that improves him.

Vi fed them without asking whether they wanted feeding. Pia turned up at seven on Wednesday morning with the thread box and a plan. Osman showed Iris how to sweat a joint and let her hold the torch, which Lyle would have described as unsafe in any other building on earth and which she talked about for a week. Somebody found a lamp. Somebody else found a chair, and it was ugly, and Iris put it next to the bed and did not comment.

She had a routine by Thursday.

She slept in the smaller of the two rooms, in a proper bed Osman had carried down in pieces, and she slept there every night they were underground except the one after the alley, when Ruth took her, and she never once asked to sleep anywhere else.

Lyle walked her to school every morning, twenty-five minutes up through the corridor and out through the laundry and four streets east, and picked her up at three, and it was further than before and he did it every day.

He told Cora where they were.

He also told her he’d messaged Margot about Friday and was sorting it.

That was the only outright lie he told anybody that week. It was four words long, he told it on the phone while carrying a box, and by the time he hung up he had genuinely stopped thinking about it.

He set a date: the following Saturday, the fifteenth, by which time the landlord would have moved or would not have, and either way they would be out.

He wrote it on the back of the notice.

Those were real precautions and they were his own idea and nobody made him take them.

What he did not do was send the message.

Section 4.4 was one sentence. The father shall disclose to the mother, in writing and within twenty-four hours of any change, the address at which the child sleeps. He had not argued about it in the room above the building society, because it had been the easiest thing in the document — it cost nothing and required no change to how he lived.

The city did not have an address.

That was true, and he thought it several times over the following days, and it was not the reason.

Margot’s first message came on the Wednesday.

Where are you staying? Iris says a friend’s. Which friend.

He answered that one. He said Cora’s, which was almost true in the sense that it had been offered.

The second came Thursday. The third Friday.

He answered the third at two in the morning with a sentence he rewrote four times.

The fourteenth was a Friday, and Friday was six o’clock at Cora’s.

He had it in his phone. He had it on the notice, on the back, one line above the date he had written for leaving — Friday the handover, Saturday the move out — because he had meant to do both things in the same weekend and hand her back on the Sunday to somewhere with a door on it.

The landlord had not moved.

Lyle was in the kitchen at the mouth of the ramp at half four with a bowl in his hands, helping Vi get through a pot of something for eleven people, and his phone went in his pocket.

He knew what it was.

He let it go.

It went again forty minutes later, and again at ten past six, and he took the bowls out and stacked the chairs and did the thing where you hold a phone in your hand and look at it and then put it back in your pocket.

Margot was at Cora’s flat at six that evening with the engine still running.

Cora rang him at twenty past.

He did not pick up.

Iris came and stood next to him at eight.

“Is it Mom’s day?”

“It was.”

“Are we going?”

“Not today, chicken.”

“Why?”

“Because there’s nowhere to bring you back to yet.”

Which was true.

It was true in every particular, and he had thought of it in advance, and he heard himself say it and thought: that’s good, that’s actually the reason, and did not notice that he had produced a sentence rather than made a call.

Cora came down on the Sunday.

She did not come to the rooms. She stood at the folding table by the kitchen with her coat on and her bag still over her shoulder, and she had been on shift since five, and she said:

“I had your ex-wife on my landing for forty fucking minutes on Friday.”

“Cora—”

“With a bag of her daughter’s clothes.”

Lyle said the thing about the wall, and the landlord, and nowhere to bring her back to. He heard how good it sounded and could not stop producing it.

Cora waited until he was finished.

“Three nights,” she said. “You told me three nights, and you told me you’d squared Friday, and I believed both because you have never once needed to lie to me about anything this small.” She adjusted the bag. “Is she safe down here?”

“Yes.”

“Is she happy?”

“Yes.”

“Then you tell Margot that. Tonight.” She was already turning. “Because those are the two answers that make this all right, and you’re the only person who’s allowed to give them, and every day you don’t, they stop being answers and start being the reason.”

At the corridor she stopped.

“Next Friday,” she said. “I’m at the flat at six either way. If she’s on my landing again with a bag and I’ve got nothing to give her, I’m telling her what I know.”

“That’s—”

“That’s a week’s notice, Lyle. Which is a week more than you gave her.”

The days after that had a shape.

He helped Osman with the pipe. He helped Ruth move a freezer. He learned four more names and got two of them wrong for a week. He took Iris to school and collected her, twenty-five minutes each way, and on the Wednesday it rained and a man he did not know lent him a coat and did not want it back.

He stopped writing dates on the notice.

The notice was in the bag with the bank letters, and the bag was under the bed, and he stopped taking it out because the top of it said the word uninhabitable and there was no longer any use for that word.

Margot’s messages changed around the eighteenth.

They stopped being angry. That was worse. There was one on the nineteenth that just said Please, and one on the twentieth about a dentist appointment on the second, and Lyle read them both standing in a corridor under a light in a cage and did not answer either, and could not have explained the difference between not answering and deciding not to.

She had been to the school. He learned that from Iris, who reported it the way she reported everything, three days late and in the wrong order: that Mom had come at going-home time on Monday and they had already gone, because Lyle collected at three and the office let out at ten past.

So Margot knew Iris was attending. She knew she was in uniform, and fed, and on the register every day that week.

She did not know where her daughter slept, and the school had Lyle’s old address, because he had not updated it, because the form asked for a postcode.

He thought, more than once, that this proved something. That a man hiding a child does not walk her to the gates every morning in front of forty parents.

He was keeping her exactly visible enough to tell himself this was not concealment.

Freya was in Martin four evenings that week.

She never once asked why they were still down there.

The second exchange was Friday the twenty-first.

He knew all day.

At six he was in the room off the second corridor with a plastic bag from the laundrette and Iris’s clothes in it, still warm, and the drawer that Ruth had found for them the week before — a proper one, out of a chest, on its own, standing on two crates against the wall because there was no chest to put it in.

He folded her school jumper.

He folded the two pairs of leggings and the ones with the fox and the good top she wore for things, and he put them in the drawer, and the drawer was slightly too full now to close with one hand.

His phone was on the bed.

It went at ten past six.

Iris was sitting on the floor doing the thread with her back against the bedframe.

“Is it Mom’s day again?”

“Yes.”

“Are we going home?”

And Lyle said, “Tomorrow.”

He said it easily and without any effort at all, in the voice he used for true things, and he did not look at the phone, and he folded the last of the washing and pressed it down with the flat of his hand so the drawer would shut.

It shut.

Iris said, “Okay,” and went back to the thread.

The phone stopped.

They were down there another seven days.

Nobody made a decision on any of them.

PART VII

MOVEMENT ONE: THE BOUNDARY

36

CUSTODY NIGHT

The pounding began at the street-level door.

Three good nights of underground sleep already in Iris’s body. Jacket on the chair. The city folded around them like a household that had decided they were inventory. Then the breach note — not kettle, not annual throat-clear. The short doubled sound. Wrong appliance.

Margot had found the building.

Of course she had.

She had hired someone, followed Lyle, or simply refused to stop searching until she mapped the place where her daughter slept. She was a mother with documentation, a lawyer’s telephone number, and enough fear to burn through any lock.

“Lyle!”

Her voice came through the steel, distorted but unmistakable.

“Open this door. I have a court order.”

Lyle looked at Iris.

She was awake in bed, sitting upright with the stuffed rabbit against her chest.

The alarm had gone ten minutes earlier, and it was the wrong one.

Not the long single moan of the annual release — the household siren that cleared its throat once a year. The city had already had its annual that autumn, and had survived it, and had folded the chair away for next year. This was the short doubled note the residents called the breach, and Lyle had heard it exactly once, during a drill, when Mr. Hallow had explained without any particular emphasis that if it ever sounded for real it would mean Elias was outside on a night nobody had opened the ramp for. Catastrophe that was not on the calendar. Kettle boiling dry.

Iris knew what it meant.

Margot did not.

“Stay here,” Lyle said.

He crossed the apartment and looked through the peephole.

Margot stood in the rain with her hair plastered to her face. Behind her, the streetlights were going out one by one. Water covered the pavement, but it did not move with the rain. It gathered in slow black currents, pulling toward drains that had begun to breathe.

“Margot,” he said through the door. “You can’t be here tonight.”

“I’m taking Iris.”

“Go back to your car. Drive until you reach a streetlight that stays on.”

“Open the door.”

“Not tonight.”

“Iris!”

Margot struck the door with her palm.

“Iris, come to the door!”

Lyle heard footsteps behind him.

Iris stood in the hallway in her socks.

“Mom’s outside.”

“She has to leave.”

“She’s not leaving.”

Iris looked at the door with the precision she used when adults insisted their faces meant something their bodies contradicted — the only true instrument in a building full of screens.

“She’s scared. Real scared. Not mad-scared. The other kind.”

Margot struck the door again.

Lyle reached for the bolt.

He would pull her inside, force her away from the street, and survive the custody consequences tomorrow.

He opened the door.

Margot stumbled across the threshold.

Her coat was soaked. Rain and street water poured from the hem.

She grabbed Iris by the wrist.

“We’re leaving.”

“You can’t,” Lyle said. “The annual night has started.”

“I don’t care what you call it.”

She pulled Iris toward the open doorway.

Iris resisted. Her socks slipped against the wet tile.

Margot stopped.

Not the way a person stops. The way a room stops when a machine in it switches off.

Her body had begun before Freya did. The grip on Iris’s wrist opened while Margot’s face was still saying the sentence, her shoulders dropping into a set she had not chosen, her weight shifting onto the left foot the way it always did, because it had done this once already and had not forgotten how.

Then her head tilted with the tiny adjustment of someone fitting herself into a frame that had been left out ready.

Lyle knew before she spoke.

Freya looked through Margot’s eyes.

“Close the door,” she said.

Margot’s voice. Freya’s cadence.

“He’s past the fourth mark.”

“Get out of her.”

“Close it, Lyle.”

The lights flickered.

Far below them, something metallic struck concrete.

Lyle closed the door.

He locked it.

He knew what Freya had done to Margot before. He knew Margot had explicitly forbidden it from ever happening again.

He allowed it because Freya sounded certain.

Freya knelt in Margot’s body and took Iris’s hands.

“Come with me, little bird.”

Iris stared into her mother’s eyes.

“You’re Freya.”

“Yes.”

“Mom said not to do this.”

“I know.”

“Then get out.”

“Not until you’re safe.”

Freya led Iris into the interior corridor.

Lyle followed.

They passed the kitchen where the soup had been taken from the stove and abandoned on the counter. The CRT teenager stood before the monitors, no longer eating, one hand pressed against his headphones.

“Fifth mark,” he said.

Mr. Hallow was fastening chains around his shoulders.

“Outer drainage route,” the teenager continued. “He’s following the south pipes.”

Freya did not stop.

She knew the safe interior better than Lyle. She chose turns before he saw them.

They reached a stone room behind the fountain where the pipes were thick and the water could be heard moving through every wall.

Freya guided Iris inside.

“Stay here,” she said. “He does not enter the protected district.”

“Get out of her,” Iris said again.

Freya looked at the child through Margot’s face.

“Iris.”

“You said I’m safe.”

“You are.”

“Then get out.”

Freya looked at Lyle.

He wanted to tell her to remain. Margot would fight. Margot would try to leave. Freya could keep her still until the chains were secured.

It would be easier.

He heard Margot’s voice from the night Freya had taken her body before.

You let her use me.

“Get out,” Lyle said.

Freya’s expression hardened.

Then Margot’s body convulsed.

Her breath stopped.

Her knees folded.

Lyle caught her before her head struck the stone.

For a moment, the body was slack and vacant.

Freya was gone.

Lyle looked around the room, knowing she might still be there and knowing it made no difference.

Margot gasped.

Her eyes opened.

She looked at Lyle’s hands on her shoulders and recoiled.

“You did it again.”

“Margot, listen to me.”

“You let her inside me again.”

“You were outside.”

“I was taking my daughter.”

“You would have died.”

“And now I’m trapped in a basement with the woman who assaulted me and the man who keeps explaining why she had to.”

“Mom,” Iris said.

Margot turned.

Iris stood against the wall with the rabbit clutched beneath her chin.

“We have to stay inside.”

Margot looked from Iris to Lyle.

“No.”

“The street isn’t safe.”

“This place isn’t safe.”

A deep sound passed through the walls.

Not footsteps.

Water moving through pipes under impossible pressure.

The south drainage valves shook.

A metal gate slammed somewhere beyond the protected rooms.

The monster did not enter.

He moved through the outer infrastructure, circling the hidden city along the storm drains and exposed service routes.

Mr. Hallow appeared in the doorway.

“Stay away from the maintenance exit,” he said. “He’s moving toward that side.”

Margot saw the resident standing behind Lyle.

She saw the stone room, the wet walls, the pipes carrying Freya’s water.

She saw an entire community prepared to explain why she needed to surrender control of her body for her own protection.

She took Iris’s hand.

“We’re leaving.”

“No,” Lyle said.

Margot moved toward the corridor.

Lyle blocked her.

She stopped inches from him.

“Are you going to imprison me too?”

“No.”

“Then move.”

“The outer route opens onto his path.”

“I don’t believe you.”

“I know.”

“That’s your fault.”

He did not move.

Margot looked down at Iris.

“Come with me.”

Iris hesitated.

Margot saw it.

The hesitation wounded her more visibly than anything Lyle had said.

She stepped around him.

Lyle followed as she pulled Iris through the corridor toward the maintenance route.

Residents emerged from doorways.

They warned her.

They pleaded.

Nobody touched her.

Nobody crossed her path.

The corridor narrowed until the architecture ended at a steel service door standing open to the rain.

A faded blue line crossed the concrete just inside the threshold.

It did not glow.

It had no symbols.

It looked like an old maintenance marking someone had forgotten to repaint.

Beyond it was an ordinary alley.

Ordinary rain.

An ordinary streetlight flickering at the corner.

Margot crossed the line.

She pulled Iris with her.

Iris said, “Mom—” and got one foot back toward it, and Margot did not feel her do it.

Lyle stopped inside the threshold.

Residents gathered behind him.

Mr. Hallow.

The woman from the kitchen.

The teenager from the CRT room.

Some reached toward Margot.

None stepped beyond the blue line.

Margot looked at them.

She understood that the kitchen, the soup, the chair, and the tenderness all ended here.

The hidden city’s hospitality was real.

So was its border.

“You’re not keeping her,” Margot told Lyle. “I would rather take my chances out here than leave her inside with you.”

“Two steps,” Lyle said. “Come back two steps.”

Margot turned away.

Then the storm drain behind her exhaled.

Something unfolded from the rain.

Elias stepped into the alley.

He wore the repaired coat with the mismatched blue sleeve. Water moved beneath his skin in slow, visible currents. The chainsaw hung from one hand, silent for the moment.

He looked at Margot.

He looked at Iris.

Iris stood outside the boundary.

Elias took one step toward them.

Margot turned.

She saw the monster.

She saw the surgical seams.

She saw the deliberate absences and additions.

She understood that the woman who had occupied her body had once done something similar to him and never stopped calling it love.

Margot pushed Iris.

It was not a gentle push.

It was a mother’s throw.

Iris stumbled backward across the blue line and struck Lyle’s chest. He caught her.

The moment Iris entered the hidden city, Elias stopped looking at her.

He looked only at Margot.

Margot did not run.

There was nowhere she could reach before he reached her.

She stepped between Elias and the blue line.

Her court papers crumpled in one hand.

“Don’t look,” Lyle told Iris.

He covered her eyes.

He could not cover her ears.

Elias moved.

His hand closed around Margot’s throat and lifted her from the pavement.

She struck at his wrist.

Her fingers found scar tissue, grafted muscle, and a pulse that did not keep a human rhythm.

Her eyes found Iris behind Lyle’s hand.

Margot stopped struggling long enough to raise one hand.

Not toward Elias.

Toward her daughter.

A final instruction to remain where she was.

Then Elias tightened his grip.

The sound was small.

The papers slipped from Margot’s hand and scattered in the rain.

Elias held her for another moment.

No one could tell whether he was examining her, remembering something, or waiting for a response that would never come.

Then he released her.

Margot struck the pavement.

Her eyes remained open toward the blue line.

Elias looked at Lyle and Iris inside the hidden city.

He made the low humming sound they had heard on the ramp.

Then he turned and continued through the outer streets.

He did not cross the line.

Nobody moved until the rain became louder than his footsteps.

Lyle handed Iris to Mr. Hallow.

Then he stepped across the boundary.

He knelt beside Margot.

He placed two fingers against her throat despite already knowing.

He closed her eyes.

He did all of it in order, the way he did the card and the whisk and the salt, and there was nothing in her to be sent anywhere.

Iris stood behind the blue line in Mr. Hallow’s arms.

She looked at her mother’s body with the precision she once used to recognize Freya.

She searched for a gesture.

A cadence.

A pattern.

There was only stillness.

“She’s not in there,” Iris said.

“I know, baby.”

“Freya’s not in there either.”

Lyle looked at the empty rain around Margot.

Freya had not entered her.

She had not attempted to replace the silence.

Margot was dead.

For once, the absence belonged only to the person who was gone.

37

NEXT OF KIN

They brought her in.

Four of them, with a door.

Lyle understood, later, that this was not improvised — that somewhere in the city there was a protocol for this and these four had done it before, and that the door was a door because a door is flat and rigid and eleven hundred people who have never once called an ambulance own a great many doors. Hollow-core cousins again. The book’s materials doing emergency work. Care welded to mechanism until the mechanism carries the dead.

They carried her past the blue line at eleven minutes past midnight, and Mr. Hallow said, “Not the fountain,” to nobody in particular, and they took her to the dry platform room where the city put its dead.

Lyle walked behind them.

Somebody had given Iris to Ruth.

The reasons were given to him in the corridor, in order, by two people at once.

If she stayed out there until morning she would be found by the outer city, and then there would be police at a maintenance entrance that had never been on a map.

The storm water was in her clothes and the storm water went back into the system.

Elias was still out there and had not finished.

Every one of those was true.

Lyle said, “Just tonight.”

He is fairly sure he said it before anybody suggested it.

Her phone rang at one-forty.

It was in her coat, which was on the platform with her, and it went off in a room with three people in it, and none of them moved.

It rang again at one forty-four.

Lyle took it out of the pocket with two fingers.

The screen said MUM, and there was a photograph attached to the contact, a woman in her seventies at a table with a paper hat on.

He stood in a stone room at one forty-four in the morning holding his ex-wife’s phone while her mother called it for the second time, and he did not answer, and afterwards he could not have told anybody whether that was because he did not know what to say or because answering meant saying where he was.

The phone stopped.

Then a text arrived, which he read on the lock screen without meaning to.

Did you get her? Let me know either way, I’ll be up.

Iris slept in Ruth’s room.

Not because anybody decided it. Ruth took her, and put her in the bed, and sat in the chair, and when Lyle came at three she said, “She’s off. Go and sit somewhere,” and he went and sat somewhere.

He sat in the kitchen at the mouth of the ramp for four hours and did not sleep and was not thinking about anything, which he mistook at the time for shock and which was, at least partly, waiting.

Around five Vi came in and started the pot without putting the light on. Soup weather. Kindness with the ramp’s shadow in it — feeding the ones who would come back and the one who would have to do things after light.

She said, “You’ll want to eat before it’s light.”

“I’m all right.”

“It’s not for you, love, it’s for them coming back off the ramp.” She put the lid on. “But you’ll want to eat before it’s light, because after it’s light you’ll have to do things.”

The things.

He wrote them on the back of a delivery note at half past six, because writing them down was the only thing he could see how to do. Hands that needed a list so they would not open.

Tell her mum

Tell the school

Iris clothes

Court thing — ask?

Body

He looked at the last one for a long time and then crossed it out, because it was on a list and lists get lost, and then he looked at the crossing-out.

The custody order died with her.

Nobody told him that. He worked it out at about seven in the morning sitting on a crate, going through it clause by clause the way he had gone through Cora’s contract in a basement in May — initial boxes, cool paper, desire and access and a child, all of it suddenly without a living party on one side.

3.1 — The mother shall have the child from Friday 18:00 to Sunday 10:00 on alternate weekends, until review.

There was no mother.

3.2 — Exchanges shall take place at the address of Ms C. Bell.

There was nothing to exchange.

4.4 — The father shall disclose to the mother, in writing and within twenty-four hours of any change, the address at which the child sleeps.

He read 4.4 four or five times.

The obligation he had failed every night for seventeen nights had stopped existing at approximately eleven o’clock the previous evening, and he was now, in the eyes of every institution that had been circling him since October, simply Iris’s father, and the only person with any standing at all.

He had been trying to get to this position since the third of October.

Not this. He had never once wanted this, and he would have given a great deal to be able to prove that to somebody. What he had wanted was the administration of it — no Friday six o’clock, no bag on a landing, no solicitor’s letterhead, no address to disclose, no week in which somebody else had the right to interrupt him. He had wanted the scrutiny to stop.

The scrutiny had stopped.

He sat on a crate in a corridor and understood that he had arrived at it, and that he would have to live inside the fact that he had wanted it, and that wanting it and causing it were not the same thing and would never again feel entirely different.

He phoned Margot’s mother at eight.

Her name was Enid and he had not spoken to her in three years and she had sent Iris a card in April with ten pounds in it.

He did it standing in the alley behind the laundry because the corridor had no signal, in the rain, and he got as far as saying her name.

“Lyle?”

And then he found that he could not make the next sentence exist.

“Lyle, where’s Margot.”

There is no version of this he tells well afterwards. He said something. Enid said “What?” twice, the second time much louder, and then she stopped asking questions and started saying no at intervals, and Lyle stood in an alley holding a phone against his ear and said I’m so sorry eleven or twelve times into a sound he had never heard a person make.

At the end of it, when she could, Enid said:

“Where is she now?”

And Lyle said, “They’ve got her.”

Which was a sentence with a shape like an answer.

Enid said, “Who has?”

“The — she’s with people. It’s being handled. I’ll ring you when I know more.”

“Lyle, who has got my daughter?”

“I’ll ring you back.”

He did not ring her back that day.

He rang the school instead, at nine, and told the office that Iris’s mother had died and that Iris would not be in, and the woman on the phone was so immediately and completely kind that he had to sit down on the wet step.

She asked whether the school should tell the class.

He said he did not know.

She said, “Take your time. Would you like me to email you rather than ring? Some people prefer the email.”

He said the email would be better, and gave her his address, and she read it back and said, “And we’ve got the same address for you as before, is that right?”

Lyle said, “Yes.”

That was the first time he lied about it to somebody who was being kind. It went in cleanly. Nobody had any reason to check.

Iris did not eat on the Saturday.

She sat at the folding table with a bowl and moved it and did not speak, and Pia sat with her for two hours doing thread and did not speak either, which was the single most useful thing anybody did that week.

At four o’clock Lyle tried, and got:

“Where is she?”

“She’s downstairs, chicken. In the cold room.”

“Is she going to the hospital?”

“Not the hospital. There’s a — when someone dies there’s a place they go, and people who look after them.”

“When?”

And Lyle said, “Soon.”

Which was the first time.

The residents were extremely good about it and that was the problem.

Nobody asked him when. Nobody said you need to ring somebody today. They brought food, and they moved a heater into the platform room without being asked, and Ruth took Iris up to the school gates on Monday and back again, and Osman fixed the door on the second room so it shut properly, and Vi washed Margot’s coat and hung it in the corridor to dry as though it would be wanted.

Every single one of those was a kindness.

Every single one of them made the next morning easier to arrive at without doing anything.

By the Sunday there was a version of events in which Margot had been dead for two days and the reason nobody had been told was that the city could not have strangers in it, and Lyle believed that version in the way he believed most things by then — completely, and only for as long as he was inside it.

Freya said nothing about the body at all until the Tuesday.

He noticed that too.

38

WHAT I KNOW

Cora came down on the second of December, which was four days after, and which was also the day of the dentist appointment nobody had cancelled.

The kitchen still smelled like soup that was doing real work. She had a bag of Iris’s clothes, washed, and a document wallet, and she stood at the folding table and did not sit down.

“I need to tell you something and I need you not to be kind about it.”

“Cora—”

“On the twenty-first,” she said, “I rang Margot and I told her where you were.”

The kitchen went on around them. Somebody was arguing about a valve.

“I gave her the laundry on Kerr, the roll-down door, and the name of the woman who’d let you in. I gave her the corridor and I told her the rooms were off the second one on the left. I told her Iris was safe and sleeping and going to school and that you were not well.” Cora put the bag on the table. “She wrote it all down. She read it back to me.”

Lyle heard himself say, “You didn’t have a choice.”

And Cora’s face did something he had never seen it do.

“Don’t,” she said. “Don’t you dare.”

“I only meant—”

“I know exactly what you meant. You meant it was inevitable. You meant the situation made the decision.” She had both hands flat on the table now. “That’s yours. That’s the thing you do, and I have watched you do it for six months, and I am not having it handed to me four days after she died.”

“Cora.”

“I had a choice. I picked. I rang her at ten past eight on Friday the twenty-first and I told her, and I would do it again, and she is dead in a corridor at the coroner’s because of the sentence I chose.”

“That’s not—”

“It’s partly true,” Cora said. “That’s what a real one sounds like. It’s partly true and I’m going to be carrying the partly for the rest of my life, and I would rather have that than have what you’ve got.”

They sat with it a while.

Somebody put tea in front of both of them without being asked, which was the city all over.

“She’d have found it anyway,” Lyle said eventually.

“Probably.”

“Her solicitor had already—”

“Lyle.” Cora looked up. “I’m not asking you to argue me out of it. I’m telling you what I did so that you know one adult in this is prepared to say a plain sentence about their own part in it.” She turned the cup around. “That’s the whole reason I came down.”

“That’s not the whole reason.”

“No,” she said. “It isn’t.”

She opened the document wallet.

“I want to talk to her.”

Lyle did not answer immediately. Then: “She’s — I don’t know where she is. She’s not anywhere. She can’t hear this.”

“I’m aware of how it works. I wrote the paperwork.” Cora took out four pages, stapled, and set them on the table facing him. “Which is why I need you to fill a bowl.”

He looked at the pages.

He looked at her.

“Cora, you said—”

“I said I wouldn’t host her again.” Cora pushed the pages an inch closer. “I’m aware of what I said. I’ve had six weeks of being right about it, and I want you to know that being right about it did not do one single thing for anybody, including me.”

“You don’t owe her—”

“I don’t owe her anything.” Cora’s voice went flat and stayed there. “Iris hasn’t spoken since the alley.”

The valve argument ended somewhere behind them.

“She hasn’t spoken to me, she hasn’t spoken to you, and she hasn’t spoken to Ruth or Pia or the woman with the soup. And there is one person left in this city who Iris has ever, in her life, been able to identify by behaviour through a stranger’s face.” Cora sat down at last. “And that person currently cannot be in a room.”

The new pages were not the old ones.

He could see that from the header. SECOND AGREEMENT — C. BELL / F. — SUPERSEDING 20 MAY. She had typed superseding, which meant she had thought about the word.

“Read it out,” Cora said.

“All of it?”

“The changed clauses. I want to hear you say them, and I want her to hear you say them, and I want to be able to point at the moment on this table.”

He read.

2.1 — Sessions occur only in the presence of a third party who is not Lyle Mercer.

He stopped.

“Keep going.”

2.4 — The host may end any session for any reason, and is not required to be able to state the reason afterwards, and no person present shall ask her to.

3.1 — No session shall be used to perform, imitate, reproduce, or approximate any deceased person.

4.2 — No session is agreed in advance. Each is agreed on the day, aloud, in front of the third party, and may be refused on the day with no consequence to any other arrangement, including school pickups, dinners, and my relationship with Iris, which are not part of this and never were.

5.1 — I am not doing this for Lyle.

5.2 — I am not forgiving anybody.

Lyle put the page down.

“5.2 is in the contract.”

“It’s the most important clause in it.”

He filled the bowl.

Ruth stood in the doorway with her arms folded, having been asked, and having said fine in a tone that meant she thought all of them were unwell.

Cora put her left hand flat on the table where everyone could see it, smallest finger extended.

“Before you come in,” she said, to a room, “I want to say a thing you won’t be able to answer, so I’m saying it now while you can’t argue.”

The water moved.

“You held me down,” Cora said. “For forty minutes. And then you left when I asked, and you have left every time since, and both of those are true and I have stopped trying to make them cancel.”

The surface of the bowl went still in the way it did.

“I’m not doing this because you’ve changed. I don’t know if you’ve changed. I’m doing it because there’s a seven-year-old upstairs who has stopped talking, and you are the only person alive who she can find inside somebody else’s face, and if I sit here being correct about you she goes on not talking.”

She looked at Lyle.

“Now,” she said.

Freya came in slowly, which she had never done before.

Cora’s shoulders resettled. The head tilted. The hands stayed exactly where Cora had put them, flat, in view, which was not a thing Freya did.

“I’ve read it,” Freya said.

“You’ve been in here nine seconds.”

“I’ve read it.” Martin’s cadence, in Cora’s throat. “Five point two.”

“Yes.”

“You didn’t have to put that in writing.”

“I did, though,” said Cora.

Freya was quiet.

“Say the thing,” Cora said. “Whatever it is. You’ve got about four seconds of it in you and then you’ll make it funny.”

And Freya said, “I have never once been sorry in a way that cost me anything. I have been sorry. I’ve been sorry beautifully. I have made people feel better about what I did to them, on the day, in their own voices.” Cora’s hands stayed flat. “That’s not a confession, it’s an inventory. I don’t know how to do the other one. I’m going to try it on you because you’re the only person who’d notice the difference.”

“That’s not an apology.”

“No.”

“Good,” said Cora. “I’d have known.”

The session ran eleven minutes.

Ruth stayed for all of it. Lyle was asked to stand where he could be seen and not to speak, and did both, badly.

At the end of it Cora curled her smallest finger three times, and Freya left, and Cora sat forward with her elbows on her knees and breathed for a while.

“Well,” she said. “That’s still horrible.”

“You don’t have to—”

“Lyle.” She got her coat. “The next one’s Thursday. Ruth’s there, or Osman, or anybody, but not you on your own. And if Iris talks, we keep going, and if she doesn’t, we stop, and that’s the whole arrangement.”

At the corridor she stopped, the way she always did.

“One more thing, and then I’m going to go and cancel a dentist appointment for a dead woman.” Her voice did not change at all. “When this is over, whatever over is — I’m not staying friends with you. I’ll be in Iris’s life until she’s forty. I’ll be at things. You and I are finished, and I want that said now, while it can still be a decision instead of a thing that happens.”

Lyle said, “Okay.”

“Say it back.”

“You and I are finished.”

“Good,” Cora said. “That’s how it’s done.”

PART VIII

MOVEMENT TWO: THE ABSENCE SHE LEAVES

39

STOP DOING HER

Five days later.

Or six.

Time moved differently in the hidden city. It moved differently in grief. Soup still smelled good. Beds were still made. That was the danger.

Iris sat at the kitchen table in the hidden city.

She had eaten when food was placed before her. She had slept when exhaustion took her. She had not spoken since the alley. Margot’s absence was in the empty chair angle, the cup that no longer waited for a second adult with a downward cadence.

Freya sat beside her inside Cora’s body.

Cora had explicitly agreed to one hour.

She remained conscious beneath the possession.

They had established a withdrawal phrase and a physical signal. If Cora curled the smallest finger of her left hand three times, Freya would leave immediately.

Freya had agreed.

She had not yet broken that agreement.

Freya held a coffee cup in both of Cora’s hands.

The posture belonged to Margot.

She tucked Cora’s hair behind one ear.

Margot’s gesture.

She allowed her shoulders to slump in the way Margot’s had when she was trying not to frighten Iris with her own exhaustion.

None of it fitted. Margot had been taller and had done these things with more arm. In Cora’s body the gestures came out short — the hand tucking the hair reached the ear too early, the slump folded too far forward — so that what sat in the chair beside Iris was not her mother, and was not Cora either, but a smaller and more careful copy of a woman, performed at the wrong scale. The more accurate the gesture, the wronger the room. Cheap bulb. Warm soup smell.

“Your mother was very brave,” Freya said.

Margot’s inflection.

Margot’s downward cadence.

Iris did not look at her.

“She protected you. That’s what mothers do. I want you to know that I would have…”

“Stop doing her,” Iris said.

Freya froze.

The hand reaching toward Iris’s hair stopped in midair.

“What?”

“Stop doing her.”

Iris’s voice was flat.

Not angry.

Empty.

“Your voice is wrong. You go up at the end. Mom went down.”

Freya withdrew Cora’s hand.

She straightened, abandoning Margot’s posture.

“Iris, I’m trying to help.”

“You’re trying to make it okay by being her.”

“I’m not trying to replace her.”

“You are when you move like that.”

Iris turned and looked into Cora’s face, searching for Freya behind the eyes.

“When you do her, I can’t remember what she actually did.”

Freya said nothing.

“You’re eating her. Like you eat everyone.”

Cora’s smallest finger twitched once.

Not the full withdrawal signal.

A warning.

Freya felt it and kept both hands still.

“Jared didn’t tap his fingers before you,” Iris continued. “Desmond didn’t make jokes like you. Mom didn’t go up at the end.”

Her eyes remained dry.

“You’re not comforting me. You’re covering her up.”

Freya’s composure faltered.

“And I need her to stay where she is,” Iris said. “I need her to be dead and you to be you. Because if you keep wearing her, I won’t have anywhere to put the missing.”

Cora’s face trembled.

“Iris,” Freya said.

This time she used her own cadence, as close to her own voice as Cora’s throat permitted.

“I didn’t mean for her to die.”

“I know.”

Iris looked toward the corridor.

“You meant for her to be yours.”

Freya lowered her eyes.

“That’s different,” Iris said. “But she’s not yours. She’s dead. And you’re still trying to put her on.”

Iris stood.

At the door Osman had fixed for her, she stopped.

“Don’t be Mom tomorrow.”

Freya did not answer.

“Don’t be Mom ever.”

Iris looked back.

“If you want to say sorry, be the person who made the mistake. Not the person who got hurt by it.”

She closed the door.

Freya remained at the table.

For a moment, she continued holding Margot’s posture.

Then Cora curled the smallest finger of her left hand three times.

Freya left immediately.

Cora’s body went slack, then inhaled sharply.

Cora sat alone in the chair, blinking tears from her eyes.

She wiped them with the heel of her hand.

“I don’t know whose those are,” she said.

Lyle stood at the mouth of the corridor.

Neither of them attempted to answer.

The kitchen held the absence of two women. Tea still warm. Hospitality still working. Shadow long enough to sit at the table.

One dead.

One present only where nobody could perceive her.

PART IX

MOVEMENT THREE: WHAT DEATH IS FOR

40

BLUE MORNING

THE ABORTED RESURRECTION

Three nights after Iris told Freya to stop doing Margot, Lyle helped move Margot’s body into the wet district.

He told himself they were preparing to return her to the ordinary authorities. Kindness that made avoidance possible.

Freya said the water could preserve her until morning.

Neither of them spoke about what else the water could do.

Margot lay on a hollow-core door positioned across the dry edge of the flooded room. Jacket off.

She was not placed in the fountain.

Not yet.

Tomas had agreed to let Freya possess him for one hour.

He had agreed within about four seconds of being asked, which Lyle noticed at the time and did nothing with. He had been nine years old on a platform beside a father who would not go off, and he had spent fifteen years believing there were four things the water did that nobody had written down, and here was somebody offering to let him be in the room while one of them happened.

He believed he was helping her prepare Margot’s body for transport and document the injuries for Lyle.

That was the full extent of his consent.

Freya entered him through a bowl of prepared water.

Tomas remained conscious.

They had established a withdrawal phrase.

Blue morning.

If Tomas spoke or attempted to mouth those words, Freya was required to leave immediately.

Freya stood over Margot inside Tomas’s body.

Margot’s throat had gone the colour of a storm three days out. The marks on it were not finger-shaped, because the hand had been too large for fingers to register separately. They were one continuous band, and the deeper print at the front, where the thumb had rested, was the size of a child’s fist.

The fountain circulated behind them.

Her own corpse remained within it, hidden beneath roots, stone, and moving water.

She had everything she needed.

Margot’s body was recent enough.

The water could preserve the tissue.

The manuscript on the doors contained the original surgical vocabulary.

Lyle’s ritual authority could anchor the binding.

The city contained enough of Freya’s remaining biology to begin the graft.

Freya had already written the first numbered sections onto the door beneath Margot.

A lung from the fountain’s circulation.

A voice assembled from preserved nerve memory.

Marrow drawn from the mineral deposits around Freya’s corpse.

Enough to restart a heart.

Enough to make Margot breathe.

Iris needed her mother.

Lyle needed forgiveness.

Freya needed to undo the last thing she had done.

She lifted a shard of blue tile — blue morning, blue thread, blue dress once.

Tomas’s hand held it above Margot’s sternum.

The tile was not a surgical instrument.

It had simply always cut more deeply than glass.

Tomas’s smallest finger began to tremble.

Freya tightened the hand around the tile.

The finger trembled again.

Not the agreed signal.

Not yet.

She placed the point against Margot’s skin.

“She would choose this if she could answer,” Freya said through Tomas’s mouth.

The sentence echoed beneath the copper dome.

Tomas’s hand stopped.

Freya had not stopped it.

Something beneath her had.

A host surfacing through sudden, singular refusal.

Tomas’s lips moved.

“Blue…”

Freya froze.

She knew the sentence she had just spoken.

She remembered Elias strapped to the table before the fifth procedure.

His head moving from side to side.

His mouth struggling around the voice she had placed inside it.

Freya leaning close.

Her hand against his forehead.

You would choose this if you could answer.

I know you would.

I know you better than you know yourself.

Tomas tried again.

“Blue morning.”

Freya left him immediately.

His body collapsed to its knees.

The shard fell into the shallow water.

Tomas coughed and pressed both hands against his mouth.

Lyle moved toward him.

“What did she do?”

Tomas looked at Margot.

Then at the numbered chart beneath her.

His face changed.

“That wasn’t what I agreed to.”

“No,” Lyle said. “It wasn’t.”

Tomas stood unsteadily.

At the archway he stopped and put a hand flat on the stone, and stayed like that for a while, and when he took it away he looked at his palm the way a man looks at a hand that has done something without him.

“I don’t want to do it any more,” he said. “Any of it. The brushing.”

He left without looking at Margot again.

He did not go back to the fountain that winter, and when the crust built up and the circulation slowed, it was Osman who did it, badly, with the wrong brush.

Lyle remained beside the door.

Freya was somewhere in the room, and he could not ask her what she intended and expect an answer.

For several seconds, nothing moved except the fountain.

Then Tomas’s abandoned handprint appeared in the damp ink on the door.

A smear crossed the first number.

Another smear followed.

The marks were being disturbed by water dripping from the ceiling and running through the grooves.

Lyle could not tell whether Freya was causing it through the fountain’s circulation or whether gravity had simply arrived at the correct moment.

He watched the chart dissolve.

Minutes passed.

The fountain’s current changed.

A loose length of cloth caught around Margot’s shoulder and pulled itself free.

Then the hollow-core door shifted slowly toward the dry stone.

Lyle understood the request.

He approached Margot’s body.

He lifted her alone.

He carried her away from the fountain and placed her on the dry platform where the city prepared its dead for burial.

He covered her.

The blue tile remained beneath the water.

Lyle sat beside Margot until morning.

He did not know whether Freya stayed.

He hoped she did.

He hated himself for hoping.

He was still there at seven when Iris came down.

Nobody had brought her. Nobody had stopped her either, because by then nobody was stopping her at anything.

She stood in the archway in her coat over her pyjamas and looked past him at her mother on the platform, and she did not come any closer, and she did not cry, and she had the blue thread bracelet on the wrist she had been picking at for a week.

She had asked him every day since the twenty-ninth.

She had asked at breakfast, and at the school gates, and once through the bathroom door, and every single time he had said the same word to her, and every single time she had taken it, because she was seven and he was her father and that is the arrangement.

“When are they taking her?”

And Lyle, who had been awake for twenty-six hours beside the body of his daughter’s mother, and who had spent most of the night watching a chart come apart in the wet, said:

“Soon, chicken.”

Iris did not move.

“Stop fucking saying soon.”

The fountain ran.

Somewhere behind them Osman, who had come down with a bucket and had stopped in the corridor at the sound of it, very quietly put the bucket on the floor and went back up.

Nobody told her off.

She had got the word from him, obviously. Nobody remarked on that either.

Iris waited.

She was not going to say it again and she was not going to take it back, and she had nowhere else to put nine days.

“Okay,” Lyle said.

“It’s not okay. She’s cold, Dad, she’s been down here nine days—”

“I know. I meant okay, I’m going to do it.”

He got up off the wet stone with his knees complaining and went and asked Ruth for her phone.

He made the call from the alley, in the rain, four feet from the blue line, standing where Margot had stood.

He did it with the door open and his daughter watching from the bottom of the steps, because it had occurred to him halfway up that if he went somewhere quiet to do it she would have to take his word for it, and she had taken his word for nine days and it had cost her.

It took four minutes.

When he came back down she had not moved, and he handed Ruth the phone, and Iris said:

“What did they say?”

“They’re sending someone. This morning. Before dinner, definitely.”

“That’s not soon.”

“No,” he said. “It’s Tuesday, half nine.”

Iris thought about that.

Then she came over and stood against his side, not hugging, just leaning, the way she had at the school gates when she was five and had decided to be tired.

They waited there together until the light changed. Not cathedral mourning. Wet stone. Fountain. A child who would not take soon.

When the first residents arrived, they found:

Margot’s body dry and intact

the erased chart

the dropped tile

Tomas’s statement that Freya had exceeded his consent

Lyle waiting beside the dead

They came at twenty past eleven.

He told them Margot had died during the storm.

He did not tell them the rest.

Before they carried her away, he stood beside the fountain and spoke toward the empty room.

“Thank you.”

No answer came.

He did not know whether Freya heard him.

He did not know whether gratitude was appropriate.

She had not saved Margot.

She had not resurrected her.

She had only chosen not to transform her.

She had accepted the boundary not because resurrection was impossible.

Because it might have worked.

She did not turn it into another procedure.

She did not turn Margot into another manuscript.

She let the dead woman keep her death.

41

THE WATER BECOMES WATER

It started in the kitchen, the way things did.

After the tile and the chart and soon becoming a Tuesday half nine — after Freya let the dead keep their death — the city kept feeding people and the fruit forgot how to wait. Vi came in on the Sunday morning and found that the bowl of pears on the shelf above the sink had gone over. Not one of them. All nine, at once, in the manner of fruit that has been holding its breath. Warm kitchen. Wrong smell under the good one.

She stood looking at them for a while.

Then she picked one up, and it gave under her thumb, and she made a sound that Lyle, who was three feet away with a mug he was not drinking, did not have a name for at the time and would recognise years later at a funeral.

“Ruth,” she called, without turning round. “Ruth.”

By Tuesday there was a list, because Osman was the kind of man who makes a list.

Fruit — 2 days

Bread — 3 days now, was 3 weeks

Milk — turning

Soup stock in the big pot — off, threw out

Rice, dried goods — fine

Meat — DO NOT

Osman pinned it by the CRTs and people came and read it the way you read a notice about a bus route changing.

Vi threw out eleven kilos of stock that had been going, continuously, in one form or another, since before her daughter was born.

She did it herself, in one trip, and would not let anybody help her carry it.

The medicine was the part that frightened people.

Nobody had explained it to Lyle, because nobody had ever needed to explain it to anybody who lived there. The city had no GP and no pharmacy and one nurse, retired, who came down on Thursdays. What it had instead was a cupboard in the second corridor with a padlock on it and a temperature nobody maintained, in which there had been, for as long as anyone could remember, things that should not have kept.

Insulin. Two boxes of antibiotics with dates on them from a decade nobody wanted to say out loud. A bag of saline. Six ampoules of something for Ruth’s heart that Ruth’s own doctor had told her, in 2011, to use within a month.

On the Wednesday, Gil’s mother’s insulin went cloudy.

She was not diabetic. She had been keeping it for a man on the upper level who was.

Ruth stood in front of the open cupboard for a long time with her hand on the door and then said, “Right,” and started sorting it into two piles, and the pile on the left was very small.

“How long?” Mr. Hallow asked.

He had come to find Lyle, which was itself unusual. He asked it in the corridor, quietly, in the way you ask a question you have already asked somebody more qualified.

“I don’t know.”

“You’re the one she talks to.”

“She doesn’t know either. She says it’s not switching off. She says it’s — ” Lyle looked for the word Freya had used through Cora’s mouth on the Monday and could not improve on it. “Forgetting.”

“Forgetting.”

“It kept things because she wouldn’t let them finish. She’s stopped.” Lyle heard himself and kept going anyway. “That’s all it was. It was never the water.”

Mr. Hallow absorbed that.

“So it was a woman,” he said. “Twenty-six years. Every apple and every stitch and every bag of blood in that cupboard, that was a dead woman refusing to let go of things.”

“Yes.”

“And now she’s let go.”

“Yes.”

Mr. Hallow looked down the corridor towards the wet district for a while.

“Well,” he said. “Good for her.”

Then, without any change of tone at all: “There’s a boy on the upper level who’s going to need that insulin in nine days and there isn’t any.”

They were not unanimous. He had not anticipated that, and it went on not being anticipated for the rest of the winter.

A woman called Sunniva, who had lived down there forty-one years and whom Lyle had spoken to twice, stopped him by the folding table on the Thursday and said, with enormous and completely genuine warmth:

“Thank her for me.”

“I—”

“When you see her. When she’s in somebody.” Sunniva had a carrier bag with her. “My husband’s been on that platform since March.”

Lyle did not understand for a second, and then understood entirely.

“Nine months,” Sunniva said. “Nine months I’ve been going down there and he’s been exactly the same, and I’ve been sitting with him, and he’s been exactly the same, and I have not been able to do a single thing about it because he wouldn’t go.”

She adjusted the bag.

“He’s gone off this week.”

“Sunniva—”

“No, love, listen to me, because I want somebody to hear it said properly.” Her voice did not shake at all. “It’s horrible. It’s the worst thing I’ve ever smelled and I’m going to have to bury my husband in the cold and the ground’s like iron. And it’s the first honest week I’ve had since March.” She was already moving. “Tell her thank you. She’ll know what for.”

Two hours later a man Lyle did not know at all put a hand flat on his chest in the second corridor and said, “You want to keep her away from the west side.”

“Sorry?”

“Your woman. Whatever she’s in this week.” He was not shouting. He was very calm, which was worse. “My mother’s on that cupboard list. She’s eighty-one and she’s got about two months of what’s in there and now she’s got about nothing, and I’ve been told it’s because somebody upstairs has finally decided to be at peace.”

“That’s not—”

“I don’t care what it is.” He took the hand away. “I’ve read the list. Everybody’s read the list. Half this city thinks she’s done a beautiful thing and the other half’s counting ampoules, and I want you to know that I’m in the second half and I’m going to stay there.”

He went.

Lyle stood in the corridor for a while.

He could not make either answer cancel the other. He tried, walking back, and got as far as the second corridor before he stopped trying.

He went down to the wet district on the Friday.

He had not been since the night with the corpse and he did not have a reason and he went anyway.

The room with the dome was colder than he remembered. The water still came down the carved stone, but it came down like water — no looping, no pattern that looked like circulation from the wrong angle, just a wet noise going where wet noises go. Ordinary path at civic scale.

The fountain smelled of stone and cold and, underneath that, faintly, of the thing the platform room smelled of now.

She had settled.

That was the only word. The body in the basin had been held for twenty-six years in a shape that was not the shape a body takes, and it had let go of that shape over the course of about ten days, and the vines had come away from the ribs, and one arm had drifted down against the stone, and the dress had begun to go.

Lyle crouched at the rim for a long time.

“They’re arguing about you upstairs,” he said.

The water did what water does.

“Half of them want to thank you. There’s a man on the west side wants you nowhere near his mother.” He put his hand flat on the stone. “Sunniva’s burying her husband on Tuesday. She said tell you thank you and she said you’d know what for.”

Nothing.

He had known there would be nothing. He was not sure, afterwards, whether that had been the reason he came.

“You’ve killed the cupboard,” he said. “You know that. You did the right thing and it’s going to kill somebody’s mum.”

The fountain ran.

“I don’t know what to do with that,” Lyle said. “I don’t think there’s anything to do with it. I think it’s just true.”

Freya was in Cora on the Monday, with Ruth in the room, under the second agreement.

Lyle told her all of it because Cora’s clause forbade lying and because he had discovered that he wanted to.

She listened without interrupting, which was new.

At the end she said, “Sunniva’s husband. Was that the one with the boat engine?”

“…Yes.”

“He used to come and tell me about it,” Freya said. “Before. He’d come down to the district and talk at the water for an hour because he thought there was something in it and he wasn’t sure what.” Cora’s hands stayed flat. “I could hear about one word in nine. He had no idea I was in there. He just liked talking to it.”

Lyle waited.

“Tell her she’s welcome,” Freya said, and then, after a moment, in a completely different voice: “And find out the boy’s name. On the upper level. The insulin one.”

“Why?”

“Because I’m going to want to know it,” she said, “and I’d rather know it now than find out later.”

PART X

MOVEMENT FOUR: THE OFFERS

42

TERMS OF HER OWN

Cora brought it in a folder.

That was the thing Lyle could never get past afterward. Not a conversation at two in the morning. Not a breakdown. A green cardboard folder with a rubber band around it, produced at the kitchen table on a Tuesday afternoon, after she had already put the shopping away. Soup weather still. Fruit still going soft upstairs in the city. Paper trying one more time — not protection, not loophole. Agency with weight. Shopping bags still on the floor like ordinary life had not been asked to leave the room.

“I’ve written something,” she said. “I’d like you both to read it before anyone says anything.”

Freya was in Martin. It had been Martin for eleven days — not continuously; nothing was continuous, and Martin still put himself down for an hour at a time in a notebook by the phone. But every hour she had spent anywhere at all in those eleven days had been an hour inside Martin, because it had been Martin or nothing since the wet district, and Martin had stopped taking the fee.

“Cora—”

“Before anyone says anything, Freya.”

She took the rubber band off.

There were four pages. They were typed. There was a header on each one with the date and a page number, because Cora had spent nine years processing insurance claims and had never in her life produced an unnumbered document.

Lyle read the first line and put it down.

Then he picked it up again, because putting it down was not going to help.

PROPOSAL — PERMANENT ARRANGEMENT

Prepared by C. Bell

1. Purpose

This document sets out terms under which I would make my body permanently available to Freya, and the conditions under which I would consider that arrangement valid.

I am aware this is not a normal document. I have written it anyway, because the alternative is a conversation, and in conversations I get talked out of things.

She had thought about all of it.
