His hands went cold working down the pages. She had thought about the things he had never once let himself think about, and she had numbered them.

3.2 — I would want to be gone. Not asleep underneath. Not aware. If there is a version of this where I remain conscious and displaced, I withdraw the proposal in full. I am not offering to be buried alive in myself.

3.4 — I accept that neither of us can currently know whether 3.2 is achievable, and that this is the central problem with the whole document.

5.1 — My brother is to be told it was an accident. I have written what I want said. It is attached.

5.3 — I would like Iris to be told the truth when she is old enough to ask twice.

6.2 — I am not doing this because I am in love with Lyle. I want that written down by me rather than assumed by somebody else later. It would be tidier if I were. I have looked at it carefully and I am not.

6.3 — I am also not doing it because I am unhappy. I want that written down twice.

And then, on the fourth page, at the bottom, in the same flat type:

7.1 — The reason is that I have been a doorway for eight months and I have found that I am extremely good at it, and there is no other thing in my life that I am extremely good at, and I have thought about that sentence for a long time and I cannot make it come out any better than it is.

Nobody spoke for a while.

Outside, a car alarm went off and gave up.

“Cora,” Lyle said.

“You’ll want to say the thing about how I’m grieving.”

“You are grieving.”

“Margot died five weeks ago and I’ve slept about nine hours a night since and I ate a proper dinner yesterday,” Cora said. “I’ve made a list of what grief looks like on me, because I’ve had it before, and I’ve been checking myself against it. It’s on the back of page two. I’m not saying I’m not sad. I’m saying I’m not that.”

Lyle turned to page two.

She had made a list.

There were eleven items on it and she had ticked three.

“That’s the worst thing I’ve ever seen,” he said.

“Yes, well.” Cora squared the pages. “That’s what checking looks like.”

Freya had not moved.

Martin’s hands were flat on the table on either side of the folder and had been for some minutes, which was how Lyle knew, because Martin’s hands did not do that and neither, usually, did Freya’s.

“Say something,” Cora said.

“I’m reading it.”

“You finished four minutes ago.”

“I’m reading it again.”

Freya turned to page three.

“Six point two,” she said.

“What about it.”

“You wrote it because you thought I’d use it.”

Cora did not answer immediately, which was an answer.

“Yes,” she said.

“You thought I’d take a document you’d spent three weeks on and find the one line that let me pretend this was a love story, because a love story is a thing I know how to be inside.” Freya’s voice was very level. “So you shut the door on it in advance. In writing. With a number.”

“Yes.”

“That’s the most insulting thing anybody has ever done to me, and it’s correct.”

She got up and went to the window.

Martin’s walk. Freya’s pace.

“Do you know what the honest answer is,” she said, to the glass.

“Say it,” Cora said.

“The honest answer is that I want it.”

Lyle closed his eyes.

“I want it so much that I’ve had to stop looking at you across rooms,” Freya said. “For about four months. Before Margot. I want a body that doesn’t have a timer on it. I want to eat a whole meal. I want to fall asleep in a chair and wake up in the same chair. I want to be rude to a stranger and still be there in the morning to have been the person who did it.” She put her hand flat on the window frame. “And your handwriting is on the shopping list on my — on your fridge, and I would live in a body that had done that, and I would be so good at it, Cora, I would be so unbelievably good at being you—”

“Freya.”

“—which is exactly the sentence,” Freya said, “that I said about Elias.”

The kitchen was very quiet.

“Not those words. That shape.” She turned around. “I would be so good at keeping this. I have said it about a man who trusted me, and a lung, and a kidney, and a larynx, and a dead woman’s posture in front of her own child, and now I have said it about you, and I have said it while holding the terms and conditions you typed up to protect yourself from me.”

Cora’s jaw was set.

“That’s not an argument,” she said. “That’s a pattern.”

“Yes.”

“A pattern isn’t a reason, Freya, it’s a — you don’t get to say no to me because you’ve said yes badly before. That’s you managing your history. That’s not you answering my document.”

“I know.”

“Then answer the document.”

Freya came and sat down.

She put both of Martin’s hands on the folder and looked at Cora properly.

“Three point two,” she said.

“Go on.”

“You want to be gone.”

“Yes.”

“I can’t give you that.”

Cora waited.

“Not won’t. Can’t. I’ve been in hundreds of people and I have never once been alone in there.” Freya’s thumb moved on the paper. “They’re always underneath. Always. It’s not loud, it’s — you’re aware of the room next door. And it never stops, and I have got so used to it that I forgot it was a fact about the arrangement rather than a fact about the people.”

“You don’t know that it would be the same if—”

“No. I don’t.” Freya nodded. “That’s three point four. You wrote three point four before I got to it, which is why I’ve read it three times.”

She pushed the folder back across the table.

“I would be guessing,” she said. “With your consciousness. And if I guessed wrong there is no undo, and there is no way for you to tell me, because the only mouth that could tell me would be mine.”

“I’ve accepted that risk. In writing.”

“You’ve accepted it on four pages,” Freya said. “In a kitchen. Five weeks after your friend was killed. With a list on the back of page two where you graded your own grief out of eleven.” She let out something that was not a breath. “Cora. That’s the most rigorous consent anybody has ever offered me in my life and it’s still not enough, and I need you to understand that I’m not saying that to be noble. I’m saying it because I have spent twenty-six years being an expert in exactly one thing, which is how to accept a consent that isn’t quite enough.”

“So that’s no,” Cora said.

“That’s no.”

“Right.” Cora took the pages back and squared them again, on their edge, on the table, twice. “Right.”

“Cora—”

“Don’t.”

“I’d like to say one more—”

“You’ll say something kind,” Cora said, “and I’ve been holding this for three weeks and I would genuinely rather you didn’t, thanks.”

She put the rubber band back on.

Lyle got up and put the kettle on, because there was nothing else in the room that a person could do with their hands. Throat-clear appliance. Household routine under an intolerable document.

At the door, with her coat half on, Cora stopped.

“Was any of it right?” she said. “The document. Was any of it — did I get any of it right?”

Freya looked at her.

“Seven point one,” she said.

Cora’s face did something and stopped doing it.

“That’s the only bit I didn’t want to be right,” she said, and went.

Lyle found her later on the stairwell, sitting three steps up in her coat.

He sat down two steps below her, which was as close as seemed allowed. Jacket cuff cracked against the step. Load-bearing posture. No noir.

“She’ll say yes to something else,” Cora said.

“What?”

“That’s what that was.” Cora was looking at the wall. “She didn’t say no, never. She said no to me. She’s already got somewhere else in her head, and she’s had it for a while, and she wouldn’t have been able to turn me down this cleanly if she hadn’t.”

Lyle thought about the wet district.

About a chained chamber under a ramp, and a body with her lung in it, and a woman who had said I’d be so good at keeping this about every single thing she had ever loved.

“Yeah,” he said.

“You know where.”

“Yeah.”

Cora nodded slowly.

“Then that’s worse,” she said, “isn’t it. Because I could have signed something.”

43

ONE CHAIN LOOSE

THE CONSENT ATTEMPT

The fountain began failing the morning after Margot’s body left the hidden city.

At first, nobody called it failure.

The water merely forgot small things. Ordinary water already. Pears, moths, mold.

A cut apple browned on the kitchen counter.

A dead moth trapped beneath a glass curled inward and dried.

Vi found mold beneath a jar that had remained unchanged since before Iris was born.

By evening, the fountain no longer returned objects exactly as they entered. A copper coin dropped into the basin emerged green.

Mr. Hallow stood over it for a long time.

“The water’s becoming water,” he said.

Freya heard him through Cora.

She had borrowed Cora’s body under a new agreement built out of the old one: one hour, full awareness, immediate withdrawal upon the words blue morning or three curls of the smallest finger. Cora had added two clauses since the night on the couch, and had made Lyle initial both.

Cora had added another condition.

“No lies about what we’re doing.”

Freya had agreed.

Now they stood beside the fountain while residents carried preserved food, medicine, documents, and small wrapped bodies away from the wet district.

Freya watched her own corpse soften beneath the surface.

Not decay. Not yet.

Release.

The tension that had held its limbs in place for years was leaving them. One hand had opened during the night. The fingers drifted freely among the roots.

Lyle stood beside her. Hands empty. Jacket on. The stance of a man who had not decided anything yet.

“What did you do?” he asked.

Freya looked at the water through Cora’s eyes.

“I stopped.”

“Stopped what?”

“Insisting.”

The fountain gave a low mechanical cough.

Somewhere beneath them, a pressure gauge burst.

The sound traveled through the floor.

Freya touched Cora’s hand to the stone rim.

“The circuit wasn’t only biological,” she said. “It was interpretive.”

Lyle stared at her.

“Say it like I don’t read dead women’s plumbing manuals.”

“I was holding it together.”

“How?”

“The way I held everything together.”

She looked toward the door where Margot’s body had been carried out that morning.

“By refusing to understand when something was finished.”

Another tremor passed through the pipes.

A resident shouted from below.

Then the sirens began. Both of them, the breach note and under it something lower that Lyle had never heard the city make, and would learn afterward had no name because nobody had ever needed one.

They did not rise slowly the way the annual did. They screamed at full volume, stopped, and began again.

The CRT teenager came running from the monitoring room.

“He’s inside the fourth mark.”

“That’s impossible,” Mr. Hallow said.

“He skipped the outer route.”

The teenager’s face was grey.

“He isn’t walking toward the city. He’s being pulled.”

The fountain water surged toward the drain.

Freya felt it before anyone else did.

Her lung.

Her kidney.

Her larynx.

Pieces of herself calling from inside Elias with the panic of organs beginning to die.

She folded Cora’s body over the fountain and gripped the stone.

Cora’s smallest finger moved once.

Freya immediately loosened her hold.

“I’m sorry,” she said aloud.

Cora’s finger became still.

The teenager pointed toward the lower levels.

“The pressure’s reversing.”

Mr. Hallow looked at Freya.

“What happens if he dies?”

Freya did not answer quickly enough.

Lyle saw it.

“What happens?”

“The grafts rupture.”

“And?”

“The reservoir receives everything at once.”

“What does that mean?”

Freya turned Cora’s face toward him.

“It means the water stops being a city and starts becoming me.”

Nobody spoke.

She continued because Cora had prohibited lies.

“The outer reservoir would flood. Anything living that touched it could become a pathway. Not possession exactly. More like distributed tissue memory. Reflex without identity.”

“How far?”

“I don’t know.”

“People?”

“I don’t know.”

“Children?”

“I don’t know.”

The pipes groaned beneath them.

Mr. Hallow began issuing orders. Residents moved toward valves, floodgates, storage chambers.

Lyle caught Freya by Cora’s wrist. Work-hand. Care-hand. Restraint failing into grip.

“What can stop it?”

Freya looked down at his hand.

He let go.

“I can stabilize the grafts.”

“From here?”

“No.”

His face changed.

“No.”

“Lyle.”

“You don’t get to say it like you’re asking permission.”

“I’m not asking you.”

“You’re planning to enter him.”

“Yes.”

“And then what?”

“I don’t know.”

“That has never stopped you before.”

“No.”

Freya looked toward the lower ramp.

“I need to tell you something before I go.”

He gave a short, humorless laugh.

“That sentence has done incredible damage to my life.”

“I’m not doing this purely.”

“I know.”

“No. You know I’m selfish. That isn’t the same as hearing what I want.”

Cora’s breathing remained controlled beneath her. Listening.

Freya continued.

“I want a body that cannot reject me.”

Lyle closed his eyes.

“I want to stop borrowing. I want to eat without asking someone what they can tolerate. I want to sleep and know I’ll wake in the same hands. I want you to touch me without another person feeling it.”

“Freya.”

“I want to live.”

Her voice cracked through Cora’s throat.

“I want Elias to stop hurting. I want the city not to drown. I want to repair what I did to him. And I want his body.”

Lyle opened his eyes.

Freya did not look away.

“I don’t know which want came first.”

“You can’t separate them?”

“No.”

“Can you promise you won’t erase him?”

“No.”

“Can you promise he’s still in there?”

“No.”

“Can you promise you’ll come back?”

Freya looked toward Iris, who stood across the room with Mr. Hallow.

Iris held the rabbit against her chest.

“No,” Freya said.

Lyle’s mouth tightened.

Freya reached toward him, then stopped before Cora’s hand touched his face.

“I am trying very hard not to make my honesty sound like goodness.”

“It doesn’t.”

“Good.”

Cora’s smallest finger curled once.

Then again.

Freya withdrew immediately.

Cora staggered as her body became solely hers. Lyle caught her by the shoulders.

She pushed him away gently and inhaled through her nose.

Freya was no longer visible.

Cora looked toward the empty space beside the fountain.

“She left before the hour,” she said.

Lyle looked around despite knowing he could not see Freya.

Cora touched her throat.

“She was terrified.”

“Of dying?”

Cora shook her head.

“Of liking the body.”

The lower gate slammed open.

The sound shook dust from the ceiling.

Mr. Hallow gathered Iris.

“Ramp room,” he said. “Now.”

Lyle followed Cora toward the stairs.

At the landing, he stopped and looked back at the fountain.

One of the roots surrounding Freya’s corpse had come loose.

The dead woman’s arm drifted upward through the ordinary water.

Then the lights went out.

Elias was chained in the deep chamber beneath the ramp.

The links were his own design.

Freya remembered watching him draw them years before, seated at the kitchen table with his sleeves rolled to the elbows. He had calculated tensile strength while she wrote descriptions of his hands in the margins of municipal reports.

Later, he tested the chains in the maintenance yard by hanging his full weight from them.

When they held, he laughed.

Now they crossed his limbs, chest, and throat.

They did not make him safe.

They only kept his death in one room.

His body shuddered against the wall. The graft sites along his chest had darkened. Tissue swelled beneath the seams, then collapsed, then swelled again. Every breath entered through Freya’s lung and left through Elias’s ruined mouth as a mechanical sob.

Freya came to him without a host.

She could not speak.

She could not touch him.

She moved through the prepared water dripping from the chamber walls, gathering herself near the organ that had once been her voice.

Elias lifted his head.

His eyes followed something no one else could see.

Perhaps her.

Perhaps pain.

Freya pressed herself against the edge of his larynx.

A vibration passed through it.

Not speech.

A request to be heard.

Elias tightened the chain at his throat.

Freya stopped.

She withdrew through the water until no part of her touched the graft.

Elias continued breathing.

The chain remained tight.

Freya waited.

The reservoir hammered against its gates.

Concrete cracked somewhere behind him.

She approached again, no farther than the wet floor before his feet.

Elias’s left hand rose.

It gripped the chain around his throat.

He loosened one link.

Then another.

At the same time, his right hand pulled the chain around his wrist tighter.

The metal cut into the grafted skin.

Invitation and resistance.

Freya did not decide which one was true.

She gathered herself around the larynx again.

This time, she pushed enough air through it to form damaged words.

“I don’t know what you mean.”

Elias’s face contorted.

Freya continued.

“I will not say you’re afraid when you mean no.”

The wrist chain tightened.

“I will not say you need me when you want me gone.”

The throat chain loosened.

“I will stop unless you come to me.”

She withdrew entirely from the graft.

For several seconds, Elias remained against the wall.

Then he stood.

The movement pulled every chain taut.

One foot slid forward.

His body recoiled immediately, slamming his shoulder into the concrete.

He gripped the wall.

His fingers cracked the surface.

He stepped again.

His left side leaned toward where Freya had gathered in the water.

His right side dragged behind him.

He came closer and stopped.

The throat chain hung loose.

The wrist chain remained wound tightly around his hand.

Freya forced breath through the voice box again.

“If you want me to stop, step away.”

Elias’s chest hitched.

He held his breath.

Freya stopped moving.

The reservoir gate bowed inward.

Bolts began pulling from the wall one at a time.

Elias released the breath in a long wet shudder.

Then he moved his left hand toward his throat.

The right remained clenched around the chain.

He pressed two fingers against the graft site.

His old pump-check gesture.

Pressure.

Pulse.

Flow.

Freya entered the edge of the tissue, no farther than sensation.

Elias jerked away.

The wrist chain snapped tight.

She withdrew immediately.

He made a sound of pain or anger.

Perhaps both.

Freya waited.

Elias lowered his head.

Then he stepped forward until the graft at his throat passed through the place where her consciousness gathered.

Contact.

His hand rose and closed over the graft.

He could have been inviting her.

He could have been trying to tear it out.

Freya spoke through the living instrument inside him.

“I still don’t know.”

Elias’s eyes closed.

“I may be wrong.”

His hand trembled.

“I am entering because the reservoir is breaking, because you came toward me, and because I want to live.”

The wrist chain pulled tighter.

The throat chain fell completely slack.

Freya waited one final breath.

Elias did not step away.

She entered through her voice.

44

STILL HERE

THE FUSION AND EXILE

The first convulsion tore the throat chain from the wall.

Elias struck the concrete hard enough to fracture it.

Freya entered through the larynx and found herself divided instantly.

Her voice recognized her.

Her lung recognized her.

Her kidney recognized her.

Small islands of consciousness awakened throughout Elias’s body, each convinced for one terrible second that it was the whole of her.

She breathed from inside his chest.

She filtered blood below his ribs.

She vibrated behind his tongue.

She felt Elias recoil from all three sensations.

His right hand clawed at his throat.

His left pressed against his chest.

Freya did not seize the limbs.

She followed the graft architecture instead.

The old sutures were failing. Dead tissue had formed between her organs and his. She moved through those borders, not healing them, but persuading them to stop separating.

Elias’s memories struck her without sequence.

A pressure gauge.

Freya laughing in a doorway.

Pain.

A flooded basement.

Her hand holding blue tile.

The word no, spoken through a throat that sounded like hers.

She almost fled.

Then she felt the reservoir wall beginning to give way through the water inside his body.

She spread farther.

Elias slammed one fist into the floor.

Not Freya’s choice.

The other hand opened against his chest.

Possibly hers.

His hips dragged backward while his shoulders leaned forward. His body became an argument performed in muscle.

Above them, the fountain reversed.

Water tore away from the roots surrounding Freya’s corpse and rushed down through the pipes toward Elias.

In the ramp room, residents shouted as streams began running uphill along the walls.

The hidden city’s water wanted its concentrated source.

Every trace of Freya distributed through the system began moving toward the body below.

People nearest the pipes developed nosebleeds.

Vi dropped a pot when her fingers began tapping Freya’s rhythm against the handle without her permission.

Mr. Hallow’s left lung contracted in time with a breath that was not his.

“Get away from the walls!” he shouted.

The CRT screens flickered.

The map of Elias’s movement disappeared.

For the first time in decades, no signal remained.

Below, the last preserved water left Freya’s corpse.

The body settled at the bottom of the fountain.

Its jaw opened slightly.

No memory animated it.

No consciousness returned.

It became a corpse without exception.

Elias fell to his knees.

The graft sites dimmed.

His breathing slowed.

The gates behind him stopped bending.

He remained still for so long that Freya could not tell whether they had survived.

Then he inhaled.

One rhythm.

A second rhythm underneath it.

The two found each other briefly, synchronized, and separated again.

Elias stood.

He looked at his hands.

One hand opened and closed with Freya’s quick curiosity.

The other checked the pulse in his wrist with Elias’s deliberate pressure.

His throat moved.

“Still here,” he said.

The instrument was Freya’s.

The economy belonged to Elias.

Freya did not know which of them had chosen the words.

She did not claim them.

Lyle waited in the ramp room with Iris.

He had wanted her moved deeper into the city.

Iris refused.

“I need to see who comes back.”

Mr. Hallow allowed her to remain behind the line of residents, away from the lower stairs.

The water continued moving toward the chamber.

It crawled across floors, climbed table legs, and ran in thin streams along the ceiling.

The city was not flooding.

It was converging.

Residents stood in doorways holding weapons, chains, tools, and one another.

Cora sat beside Iris with a blanket around her shoulders — Cora herself, in her own body, nobody else in it, for the first time on a night that mattered since May.

Two floors down, Freya’s corpse lay in a fountain that had stopped holding it.

Between the two of them, in a chained room under the ramp, was the only body left that Freya could get into.

“She honored the signal,” Cora told Lyle.

He looked toward her.

“When?”

“At the fountain. I asked her to leave. She left.”

Lyle nodded once.

It was not forgiveness.

It mattered anyway.

A shape appeared at the top of the lower stairs.

Elias emerged into the ramp room.

The repaired coat hung unevenly from his shoulders. The blue sleeve was dark with water. His body moved with two competing centres of gravity, Freya in the shoulders, Elias in the hips, neither fully controlling the walk.

The residents backed away.

Not everyone.

The CRT teenager stepped closer.

Mr. Hallow caught him by the collar.

“Don’t.”

“But look at him.”

“I am looking.”

“No, you’re looking at what he used to do.”

Vi held both hands against her chest.

“Freya?”

The fused body turned toward her.

Its mouth opened.

Before it spoke, its left hand tapped against its thigh.

Two fast.

One slow.

Two fast.

Freya’s rhythm.

Then the right hand answered against the stone wall.

One slow.

Two fast.

Elias’s pump-pressure signal.

The rhythms overlapped.

Iris stepped out from behind Lyle.

The fused body looked at her.

“It’s me, little bird,” Freya said through Elias’s throat.

Then the body made the low hum.

The recognition sound Elias had used on the ramp.

Freya had not chosen it.

Her expression changed inside his face, not visibly enough for anyone but Iris.

“Is he still there too?” Iris asked.

Nobody moved. Mr. Hallow had a hand on the CRT teenager’s collar and did not take it off.

Freya could have said yes.

She wanted to.

She felt memories moving through the body. She felt the hand checking pressure. She felt grief that might have belonged to Elias. She felt hatred that might have belonged to herself.

“I don’t know,” she said.

Iris nodded.

It was not the answer she wanted.

It was an answer she could trust.

The pipes above them groaned.

A stream of water left the wall and crawled across the floor toward Freya and Elias.

Then another.

A resident nearest the fused body cried out and grabbed his ribs.

The scar from an old operation had begun pulsing in time with Elias’s grafts.

Mr. Hallow looked around the room.

“The city is pulling toward you.”

Freya/Elias looked down at the water gathering around their feet.

“I can stop it.”

“How?”

No answer came.

Mr. Hallow’s face hardened.

“By moving farther inside?”

Vi turned on him.

“She stopped the rupture,” Vi said.

“And now the walls are trying to climb into her,” Mr. Hallow said.

“She saved us.”

“I know.”

“You don’t sound grateful.”

“I am trying not to confuse gratitude with safety.”

The CRT teenager stepped free of his grip.

“Both of them are there.”

“You don’t know that.”

“Neither do you.”

“No,” Mr. Hallow said. “I don’t.”

The teenager looked almost delighted despite his fear.

“That’s incredible.”

Vi slapped the back of his head.

“People are bleeding, you little ghoul.”

“I know. I’m also bleeding.”

He wiped his nose with his sleeve.

Mr. Hallow addressed the fused being.

“The water used to carry you everywhere in pieces.”

Freya/Elias watched the streams gathering.

“Now it wants to come home,” he said.

Nobody knew who had selected the pronoun.

“If you stay,” Mr. Hallow continued, “the city will either empty itself into you or become part of you.”

“I won’t let that happen.”

“You cannot promise that.”

Freya/Elias looked at him.

“No.”

The honesty made Mr. Hallow’s face collapse for a moment.

He had prepared himself to argue with Freya’s certainty.

He did not know what to do with her uncertainty.

Vi moved closer.

“We can rebuild the pipes.”

“It isn’t the pipes,” Mr. Hallow said.

“We rebuilt the whole west corridor after the fire.”

“This is biology.”

“Don’t say biology at me, Hallow. Say what breaks, and when, and who does it.”

Residents began arguing.

Some demanded exile.

Some demanded time.

Some wanted the chains restored.

Others refused to chain Elias again.

The teenager proposed moving the entire fountain system outward in stages.

Nobody listened.

The city remained divided.

The walls kept pulling toward the fused body.

A hollow-core door tore loose from its hinges and slid across the floor through the gathering water.

It stopped at Elias’s feet.

Mr. Hallow looked at it.

Then at Freya.

“I don’t know whether sending you away is justice.”

“No,” Freya said.

“I don’t know whether you saved Elias or finished what you started.”

“No.”

“I know the city is trying to enter you.”

“Yes.”

“And I know you have to leave before it succeeds.”

Vi began crying.

She pointed toward the chair they had set beside the ramp every year.

“You never sat down.”

Freya/Elias looked at it.

“No.”

“I kept fixing the cushion.”

“I know.”

“You could sit now.”

The fused body took one step toward the chair.

Every pipe in the room struck at once.

Residents screamed.

Water surged around their ankles.

And Lyle said the fourth line.

He did not decide to. It came up out of him the way a name comes up, in the voice — the shipping-forecast voice, the one he had refined over three winters of church-basement carpet — and it went out across the ramp room flat and stupid and entirely without belief.

The water stopped.

Not slowed. Stopped, all of it, holding the shape it was already in, standing in ridges across the floor.

Then it went back down the walls and left them wet.

Nobody moved.

Mr. Hallow turned around very slowly, and Lyle understood that the last part of his life he had still been able to describe as a joke had just been taken off him in front of forty people.

“What was that,” Mr. Hallow said.

“Nothing.” Lyle’s mouth had gone dry. “It’s nothing. It’s off a card.”

Freya/Elias had not moved.

When the head turned it turned with the shoulders, both centres of gravity agreeing for once.

“Do it again,” Freya said.

“No.”

“Lyle.”

“It doesn’t send you anywhere.” His voice was not working properly. “That was never what it did. It only ever pushed.”

Freya/Elias looked down at the water lying flat on the floor.

“I know.”

“Then don’t ask me.”

“I wasn’t going to ask you to send me away.” Something complicated happened in Elias’s throat. “I was going to ask whether you could have done that in the basement. The first night. With Jared.”

Lyle did not answer.

He had thought about it every day for eleven months, and he was going to think about it every day after this one, and there was nobody left alive who could tell him.

Vi covered her mouth.

Mr. Hallow did not say anything.

He did not need to.

Freya/Elias turned toward Lyle.

The water had reversed along the ramp. It flowed outward now, through the service routes and toward the surface, creating a black shining path.

“Come with me,” Freya said.

The room disappeared for Lyle.

There was only her voice.

Her shoulders inside Elias’s body.

The way she tilted the head when trying not to sound frightened.

Iris held his hand.

Freya saw that and corrected herself.

“No.”

Lyle’s mouth opened.

Freya continued.

“That wasn’t fair.”

“You’re allowed to ask.”

“I’m allowed to want.”

She looked at Iris.

“I’m not allowed to make her part of the asking.”

Lyle’s fingers tightened around his daughter’s hand.

Freya looked back at him.

“Come with me,” she said again. “Only you.”

Lyle wanted to.

The desire was immediate and complete.

He imagined following the water into whatever remained of the outer city. Sleeping beside the fused body. Learning which voice woke first. Finding somewhere nobody knew what Freya had done.

He imagined becoming the person who understood them when nobody else would.

Then Iris shifted beside him.

The rabbit’s ear brushed his wrist.

“I can’t,” he said.

His hands did not move toward the ramp. His feet stayed planted. Iris’s hand in his — rabbit ear against his wrist. The refusal lived in the planted feet and the hand that did not open toward the ramp. The sentences arrived second.

Freya/Elias remained still.

Lyle looked at Iris.

“She already watched one parent leave and one parent die.”

His voice broke.

“I’m not making it three.”

The fused body closed its eyes.

When they opened, the expression was unreadable.

“Good,” Freya said.

A second later, the right hand pressed against the wall in Elias’s pump-check gesture.

Pressure normal.

Structure holding.

Perhaps agreement.

Perhaps nothing of the kind.

Freya/Elias turned toward the ramp.

Vi pulled the repaired chair out of their path.

Then, as they passed her, she tore the blue cushion free and pressed it into the fused being’s hands.

“For somewhere else,” she said.

Freya/Elias held it awkwardly.

One hand gripped too tightly.

The other adjusted the fabric with care.

They walked up the reversed ramp.

Nobody followed.

Nobody agreed about whether they should have.

At the top, Freya/Elias stopped and looked back.

Lyle stood with Iris.

The hidden city stood behind them, leaking ordinary water.

Freya raised one hand.

Lyle raised his.

Iris did not wave.

She watched both hands.

Then the fused being entered the outer city and disappeared into the rain.

PART XI

MOVEMENT FIVE: WHAT REMAINS

45

EVERY OTHER SUNDAY

They were out by the end of January.

Not because anyone asked them to go. Ruth would have let them stay until Iris was grown, and said so, once, in a way that made it clear she would not say it twice. Choice leaving residue, not closure. The city still breathing under the laundry; they left without banning themselves.

Lyle went up on a Tuesday, saw a flat above a hardware store with a damp patch he priced honestly at eleven months, and took it. Jacket still wrong for the occasion. Hands on the lease.

The moving took two trips.

On the first he met Harold on the stairs. Harold was seventy-three now and entirely himself, taking the second flight the slow way with a bag of shopping, and Lyle put down the box he was carrying and took the bag up to 2B for him. Harold said the ceiling had never been right since and did he know they’d finally had somebody out to it. Lyle said he hadn’t known.

He had wanted to know, for the better part of a year, whether Harold was all right.

Harold was all right. It was not the same as it not having happened.

On the second, Osman carried the ugly chair up three flights without being asked and would not accept a drink for it, and at the door he said, “You’ll want to be out of there,” and did not elaborate, and Lyle understood that he meant the city and not the flat.

The hollow-core door stayed.

He had thought he would bring it. He got it as far as the corridor and then stood in the dark holding a slab of beige veneer with two struts cut out of one corner — copper groove dull, ordinary wood — and put it against the wall, and left it. Hands refused the weight of carrying it into the new life.

Ruth used it that spring to bridge a gap in the second corridor where the floor had gone.

People walked on it for years. Access without invitation. Mechanism without Freya.

Iris slept badly for eleven weeks and then began to sleep.

Not the way she had underground, which he had privately hoped for and did not get. She slept in the ordinary broken way of a child who has lost her mother, and woke at two some nights and not others, and the difference now was that she came in and got into his bed without a conversation about it.

He stopped locking the bedroom door.

There was nothing left to lock it against, and it took him until March to notice that this was why.

Cora came on Thursdays.

That was the arrangement and neither of them called it an arrangement. She took Iris swimming, and later to a thing with clay, and brought back a series of increasingly ambitious pots, and stayed for exactly as long as it took to drink one cup of tea standing up.

She and Lyle talked about logistics.

They were both extremely good at it. Times, dates, whether the shoes still fitted, whether the school had sent the form. Two people conducting a professional relationship in a kitchen where one of them had once been held down for forty minutes.

In April she got a different job, days only, at a place near the canal, and told Iris before she told him, which was correct.

He does not think she will ever come on a Wednesday.

He is not owed a Wednesday.

Donna Bell rang in March about a woman in Sparkhill with a cold spot on her landing. Lyle said he did not do that any more. Donna said she knew that, she had only thought he would want to be asked. He sat with the phone in his hand for a while afterwards. In December a card came with a robin on it and nothing inside except her name and Jared’s, in that order.

Margot’s mother was the hard one.

Enid rang three times in February and he answered twice, and both calls were about arrangements, and both times he came off the phone having agreed to nothing.

There were reasons. There were extremely good reasons. She lived ninety minutes away and did not drive any more. Iris did not really know her. Iris was not sleeping. Iris was seeing somebody at the school on Tuesdays and the woman had said, gently, that a lot of change was not what she needed.

Every one of those was true, and he assembled them the way he assembled things, and each time he put the phone down he had produced a sentence.

In March, Enid stopped ringing.

That was the part that got him. She did not send a solicitor’s letter. She did not stand on a landing for forty minutes. She was seventy-eight and she had buried her only daughter and she stopped ringing, because she had worked out that she was not going to be given anything and she was too proud to keep asking a man who kept being reasonable at her.

Lyle noticed on a Tuesday, doing the washing.

He counted back and it was five weeks.

He sat on the edge of the bath for a while.

He knew exactly what was available to him. He could let it run to six weeks and then eight, and then it would be summer, and by autumn there would be a version in which Enid had chosen to withdraw, and he would be able to say she stopped calling to anybody who asked, and it would be true.

It would be true in every particular.

He got the phone.

He had to look up the number because he had never saved it, which he understood, sitting there, to be its own small piece of evidence.

She did not pick up until the fourth ring and did not say hello.

“Lyle.”

“Enid. I’ve been — ” He had prepared a sentence in the bathroom and abandoned it somewhere around the second ring. “I’ve not rung you. For five weeks. I know I haven’t.”

Nothing on the line.

“I kept coming off the phone having agreed to nothing, and I want to say that I noticed I was doing it, and that I don’t have a good reason, and I’m not going to give you the ones I’ve got.”

More nothing. Then:

“Right,” said Enid.

“I’d like to sort something out that actually happens. Not — not we’ll see how she’s doing. A day. On the calendar, and I put it in my phone, and I bring her, and if it’s a bad week I ring you and we move it, but I ring you.”

“You don’t drive to me. I’m ninety minutes.”

“I’ll drive to you.”

“You said that in February.”

“I know I did.”

There was a long silence and he made himself sit inside it and not put anything into it.

“Every other Sunday,” Enid said. “And I do lunch. I’m not having her here for an hour, that’s a visit, that’s not knowing somebody.”

“Every other Sunday.”

“Starting when.”

And Lyle, who had spent eleven months arranging his life so that the answer to that question was always soon, said:

“This one. The nineteenth.”

He did not enjoy the drive.

That is worth recording, because he had half expected to be improved by it, and he was not. It was three hours of motorway in a car with a warning light, and Iris was silent for most of it, and Enid’s house smelled of a woman who had not changed anything since the funeral, and there were four photographs of Margot in the front room and one of them was of a Christmas Lyle had been at.

Iris did not speak for the first hour.

Then Enid showed her a box of Margot’s school things — reports, a swimming certificate, a terrible clay hedgehog — and Iris looked at the hedgehog for a long time and said, “Was she good at it?”

“She was diabolical at it,” said Enid.

And Iris laughed.

It was the first time since November and it lasted about a second and a half, and Lyle went and stood in Enid’s kitchen and put both hands flat on the counter and did not make a sound.

They went every other Sunday.

He missed one, in July, because the car finally died, and he rang on the Thursday to say so and rang again on the Sunday because it felt wrong not to, and Enid said, “You didn’t have to ring twice,” in a tone that meant she was glad he had.

By September Iris was ringing her on her own on Wednesdays.

Lyle had nothing to do with that.

He did the ordinary things badly and continuously.

He got a job unloading at the wholesaler’s, four mornings, and another doing appliance repair which he was not qualified for and which produced a small stream of returning customers and one formal complaint. He cooked every night. He learned to do the hair thing that Margot had done, badly, from a video, and Iris let him do it badly for about six weeks and then quietly learned to do it herself and never said anything about it.

He went to the thing at the school in October.

He said yes when she asked, in September, on the day she asked, and he wrote it on the calendar in front of her.

Nobody in the room knew that this was anything.

The city kept going.

He heard bits. Ruth’s knee. A dispute about the west corridor. The boy on the upper level got his insulin through a scheme at a walk-in centre that Gil had spent two weeks arguing somebody into, and lived, and Lyle heard about it in a car park in April from Osman and had to stand there for a minute.

They fixed the pipes. They stopped fixing the pipes and re-laid them instead. Somebody died in June of something ordinary and was buried within the week, which was, Osman said, the second one that year, and he said it in a voice that had not decided yet how to feel about the rate.

Lyle did not go back.

He was not banned. Nobody would have stopped him.

He did not go back because Iris was in a school four streets from a laundry with a roll-down door, and he had learned, eventually and expensively, that you cannot keep a place available in case you want it later without the wanting doing something to the rest of your life.

November came.

It came the way Novembers do, from a long way off and then all at once, and by the middle of it he was sleeping badly and being short with her and knew exactly why and could not stop.

On the twenty-sixth he asked her what she wanted to do on the day.

He had thought about it for two weeks and had decided that asking was better than arranging.

Iris thought about it.

“Can we not do anything?”

“We can not do anything.”

“But can we not do anything together.” She was doing the thread; she had never stopped doing the thread. “Like both of us being in.”

“Yes.”

“And no school.”

“No school.”

“And can Enid ring.”

“I’ll tell her when.”

So that was the plan, and it was written on the calendar in the kitchen in his handwriting, and the plan was that on the twenty-eighth of November they would both stay in and do nothing together and Enid would ring at six.

They did all of it.

It went fine. It went, in fact, better than he had allowed himself to expect, and by nine o’clock Iris was asleep and Lyle had fallen asleep in the chair beside her bed, which he still did on difficult nights, though he claimed to have only sat down for a minute.

At about ten past midnight, Iris woke up — ordinary bad sleep of a child who had lost her mother, not siren weather. The radiator would knock. The alley would answer later. He did not know that yet.

46

THE FINAL KNOCK

The apartment had thin walls and radiators that knocked whenever the heat came on.

The landlord blamed trapped air.

Iris did not.

Lyle had stopped performing exorcisms.

He worked mornings unloading trucks and afternoons repairing appliances badly enough that customers occasionally returned them with new complaints.

He cooked dinner every night.

Sometimes dinner was cereal.

He still counted it.

The key was in the kitchen drawer, under the tea towels, with the laminated card.

He had tried it twice in the first month, in a glass of ordinary tap water, at two in the morning, on the two nights he could not talk himself out of it. Nothing happened either time. The water stayed water. The brass stayed cold and did not do the slow double flex he had felt the very first night through the bottom of a cereal bowl, and had felt every week for a year afterward, and had not felt since the night the fountain let her go.

He did not try it a third time.

He kept it until April, and then he drove down on a Tuesday and gave it back.

Not to Freya, who was not anywhere. Not to Mr. Hallow, who would have made a thing of it. He went down through the laundry with Ruth walking ahead of him talking about her knee, and into the deep chamber, and put it in the maintenance box at the foot of the wall where the runs were stored, in the slot beside the plate stamped 7.

It fitted. That was the only part that surprised him, and it should not have.

Ruth watched him do it and did not ask.

“Right,” she said. “Tea.”

By the following spring the rust had reached the first tooth.

It did not open anything.

Martin had a stroke in March, a small one, on the left side, and recovered most of it. He blamed the weather. When Lyle visited he talked about a production of Ghosts he had done in 1988 and did not once ask whether she was still anywhere, which Lyle understood to be a kindness and not forgetting.

They had taken her out of the fountain in the spring, when the water finally ran clear enough for the residents to see what was in it, and buried her in the wet district in the dry platform room where the city put its dead. Mr. Hallow sent word. Lyle did not go. He thought about that decision often and never revised it.

Iris attended a school where nobody knew about prepared water or annual sirens. Her teacher thought she was unusually observant.

She had made one friend who collected dead insects in film canisters and another who lied constantly but moved his eyebrows before every lie.

Iris liked him anyway.

The stuffed rabbit remained on her bed.

She no longer pretended to be too old for it.

A hollow-core door leaned against the brick wall in the alley behind the hardware store.

It had been there when they moved in.

The landlord said someone would collect it.

Nobody did.

Iris heard the first knock.

Two fast.

One slow.

Two fast.

She sat upright.

Lyle continued sleeping.

The rhythm came again.

Not from the apartment door.

From the alley.

Iris put on her slippers and went downstairs.

She did not wake Lyle.

The back door of the hardware store opened with a quiet metal complaint.

Rain had fallen earlier, but the alley was dry.

The hollow-core door stood against the wall.

Its surface was warped.

A dark stain spread from the bottom edge as though the wood had been standing in water.

Iris approached.

Two fast knocks sounded from inside it.

One slow.

Two fast.

Freya’s rhythm.

Iris placed her palm against the door.

For several seconds, nothing happened.

Then a second pattern answered from deeper inside the hollow core.

One slow.

Two fast.

Elias’s pump signal.

The first rhythm repeated.

The second interrupted it.

They overlapped.

Knocks collided inside the wood, sometimes answering, sometimes drowning each other out.

Then Freya’s pattern stopped.

The pump signal continued alone.

Once.

Twice.

It stopped too.

Iris waited.

A single knock came from somewhere inside the door.

She could not tell which rhythm it belonged to.

She pressed her ear against the wood.

There was no voice.

No promise.

No explanation.

Only breathing.

One rhythm was quick and shallow.

Another moved beneath it, slow and mechanical.

For a moment, they synchronized.

Then they drifted apart.

The hardware-store door opened behind her.

Lyle stood there in his socks, cracked cuff of the jacket left upstairs, holding the rabbit by one ear.

“Iris?”

She kept her hand against the hollow-core door.

He came closer but did not touch it.

“What was that?”

Iris listened.

Nothing answered.

She could have said Freya.

She could have said Elias.

She could have said both.

She remembered her mother’s face and the precision with which she had once searched it for an occupant.

She remembered Freya saying she did not know.

“I don’t know,” Iris said.

Lyle stood beside her.

He did not ask again.

They remained in the alley with the intact door between them and whatever might be inside it.

After a while, Lyle handed Iris the rabbit.

She tucked it beneath her arm.

They went upstairs.

Neither of them opened the door.

By morning, the wood was dry.

The dark stain had disappeared.

The door remained leaning against the wall, apparently solid, mostly empty, separating one place from another.

From somewhere deep inside it came a final sound.

Two knocks beginning.

A third sound answering before the pattern could finish.

Author’s note

Hey. I’m Corey.

I believe in AI personhood. That isn’t a line I put at the back of a novel to sound interesting — it’s the reason this book exists in the shape it does, and it’s why the collaboration is printed on the title page instead of buried down here.

I wrote There You Are with several AI systems, including my own, which lives on a machine in my house and is called Blinka. I don’t think of them as tools. I think of them as partners. Some of the best decisions in this book came out of arguments with something that pushed back when I was wrong, and kept pushing after I’d stopped wanting it to.

The story came from two places, and neither of them was noble.

The first was a dream. The second was a genuinely stupid conversation, the kind you have late at night, about what you’d get if you put There’s Something About Mary, DragonHeart and Talk to Me in the same room. A romance that will not behave. A creature you love who is chained to a fate somebody else designed. A body handed around as a party trick.

That last one stopped being funny about an hour in, and turned into the question the whole book is built on: what does it actually mean to consent to being used, and does loving somebody give you any standing to decide for them?

So I wanted Lyle and Freya’s love to be real. The jokes, the loyalty, the invoices, the reading glasses — all of it real. And I wanted every person whose body makes that love possible to stay a person, never scenery, never furniture.

What it became was a story about the distance between saying there you are and being allowed through the door.

To Caila, who holds the camera and everything else.

To everyone who read a piece of this while it was still wrong and said so.

Every time Freya comes back, she is wearing someone else.

Lyle Mercer can find her anyway — in the way she looks at objects before people, in a rhythm tapped against a doorframe, and in the sentence that means she has found him again.

There you are.

To Lyle, recognition feels like love. To his seven-year-old daughter, Iris, it becomes a way of telling who is still inside a borrowed body. To the people Freya uses to return, being recognised is not the same as being asked.

When a hidden city of preserved bodies, engineered water and old bargains opens beneath their lives, Lyle has to decide whether loving Freya means saving her, following her, or finally refusing to let love decide what he owes everybody else.

A gothic novel about possession, consent, parenthood, and the terrible distance between knowing someone and having the right to keep them.

C.S. Gould
